Quantum de Sitter horizon entropy from quasicanonical bulk, edge, sphere and topological string partition functions
Dionysios Anninos, Frederik Denef, Y.T. Albert Law, Zimo Sun

Abstract
Motivated by the prospect of constraining microscopic models, we calculate the exact one-loop corrected de Sitter entropy (the logarithm of the sphere partition function) for every effective field theory of quantum gravity, with particles in arbitrary spin representations. In doing so, we universally relate the sphere partition function to the quotient of a quasi-canonical bulk and a Euclidean edge partition function, given by integrals of characters encoding the bulk and edge spectrum of the observable universe. Expanding the bulk character splits the bulk (entanglement) entropy into quasinormal mode (quasiqubit) contributions. For 3D higher-spin gravity formulated as an sl( n n ) Chern-Simons theory, we obtain all-loop exact results. Further to this, we show that the theory has an exponentially large landscape of de Sitter vacua with quantum entropy given by the absolute value squared of a topological string partition function. For generic higher-spin gravity, the formalism succinctly relates dS, AdS ± and conformal results. Holography is exhibited in quasi-exact bulk-edge cancelation.

A=\Omega_{d-1}\ell^{d-1}
,
\ell\propto 1/\sqrt{\Lambda}

)S. The semiclassical equilibrium state locally maximizes the observable entropy at a value
{\cal S}

semiclassically given by
PhysRevD.15.2752

\displaystyle{\cal S}=\log{\cal Z}\,,

(1)

where
{\cal Z}=\int e^{-S_{E}[g,\cdots]}
is the effective field theory Euclidean path integral,
expanded about the round sphere saddle related by Wick-rotation (
292
) to the de Sitter universe of interest.
At tree level in Einstein gravity, the familiar area law is recovered:

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{A}{4G_{\rm N}}\,.

(2)

The interpretation of
{\cal S}
as a (metastable) equilibrium entropy begs for a microscopic understanding of its origin. By aspirational analogy with the Euclidean AdS partition function for effective field theories with a CFT dual (see
Bhattacharyya:2012ye
for a pertinent discussion), a natural question is: are there effective field theories for which the semiclassical expansion of
{\cal S}
corresponds to a large-
N
expansion of a microscopic entropy? Given a proposal, how can it be tested?

In contrast to EAdS, without making any assumptions about the UV completion of the effective field theory, there is no evident extrinsic data constraining the problem. The sphere has no boundary, all symmetries are gauged, and physically meaningful quantities must be gauge and field-redefinition invariant, leaving little.
In particular there is no invariant information contained in the tree-level
{\cal S}^{(0)}
other than its value, which in the low-energy effective field theory merely represents a renormalized coupling constant; an input parameter.
However, in the spirit of
Banerjee:2010qc
;
Banerjee:2011jp
;
Sen:2012kpz
;
Sen:2014aja
;
Sen:2012dw
;
Bhattacharyya:2012ye
;
Giombi:2013fka
;
Giombi:2014iua
;
Gunaydin:2016amv
;
Giombi:2016pvg
;
Skvortsov:2017ldz
;
Gearhart:2010wz
, nonlocal
quantum
corrections to
{\cal S}
do offer unambiguous, intrinsic data, directly constraining models. To give a simple example, discussed in more detail under (
168
), say someone posits that for pure 3D gravity, the sought-after microscopic entropy is
S_{\rm micro}=\log d(N)
, where
d(N)
is the number of partitions of
N
. This is readily ruled out. Both macroscopic and microscopic entropy expansions can uniquely be brought to a form

\displaystyle{\cal S}={\cal S}_{0}-a\log{\cal S}_{0}+b+\mbox{$\sum_{n}$}\,c_{n}\,{\cal S}_{0}^{-2n}\,+\,O(e^{-{\cal S}_{0}/2})\,,

(3)

characterized by absence of odd (=local) powers of
1/{\cal S}^{(0)}
. The microscopic theory predicts
(a,b)=\bigl(2,\log(\pi^{2}/6\sqrt{3})\bigr)
,
refuted by the macroscopic one-loop result
(a,b)=\bigl(3,5\log(2\pi)\bigr)
. Some of the models in
Maldacena:1998ih
;
Banados:1998tb
;
Bousso:2001mw
;
Govindarajan:2002ry
;
Silverstein:2003jp
;
Fabinger:2003gp
;
Parikh:2004wh
;
Banks:2006rx
;
Dong:2010pm
;
Heckman:2011qu
;
Banks:2013qpa
;
Neiman:2017zdr
;
Dong:2018cuv
;
Arias:2019zug
are sufficiently detailed to be tested along these lines.

In this work, we focus exclusively on collecting macroscopic data, more specifically the exact one-loop (in some cases all-loop) corrected
{\cal S}=\log{\cal Z}
. The problem is old, and computations for
s\leq 1
are relatively straightforward, but for higher spin
s\geq 2
, sphere-specific complications crop up. Even for pure gravity
Hawking:1976ja
;
Gibbons:1978ji
;
Christensen:1979iy
;
Fradkin:1983mq
;
Yasuda:1983hk
;
Allen:1983dg
;
Polchinski:1988ua
;
Taylor:1989ua
;
Vassilevich:1992rk
;
Volkov:2000ih
, virtually no complete, exact results have been obtained at a level brining tests of the above kind to their full potential.

Building on results and ideas from
Witten:1995gf
;
Donnelly:2013tia
;
Donnelly:2014fua
;
Donnelly:2015hxa
;
Giombi:2013yva
;
Tseytlin:2013fca
;

;

, we obtain a universal formula solving this problem in general, for all
d\geq 2
parity-invariant effective field theories, with matter in arbitrary representations, and general gauge symmetries including higher-spin:

\boxed{{\cal S}^{(1)}\,=\,\log\prod_{a=0}^{K}\frac{\bigl(2\pi\gamma_{a})^{{\rm dim}\,G_{a}}}{{\rm vol}\,G_{a}}\,+\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,\,\chi^{\rm bos}_{\rm tot}-\frac{2\sqrt{q}}{1-q}\,\,\chi^{\rm fer}_{\rm tot}\biggr)+{\cal S}_{\rm ct}}

(4)

q\equiv e^{-t/\ell}
. Below we explain the ingredients in sufficient detail to allow application in practice. A sample of explicit results is listed in (
12
). We then summarize the content of the paper by section, with more emphasis on the physics and other results of independent interest.

G_{0}
is the subgroup of (possibly higher-spin) gravitational gauge transformations acting trivially on the
S^{d+1}
saddle. This includes rotations of the sphere.
{\rm vol}\,G_{0}
is the volume for the invariant metric normalized such that the standard rotation generators have unit norm, implying in particular
{\rm vol}\,SO(d+2)=\mbox{(\ref{vcSO})}
. The other
G_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,K
are Yang-Mills group factors, with
{\rm vol}\,G_{i}
the volume in the metric defined by the trace in the action, as in (
288
). The
\gamma_{a}
are proportional to the (algebraically defined) gauge couplings:

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\equiv\sqrt{\frac{8\pi G_{\rm N}}{A_{d-1}}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{{\cal S}^{(0)}}}\,,\qquad\qquad\gamma_{i}\equiv\sqrt{\frac{g_{i}^{2}}{2\pi A_{d-3}}}\,,

(5)

with
A_{n}\equiv\Omega_{n}\ell^{n}
,
\Omega_{n}=\mbox{(\ref{volSn})}
for
n\geq 0
, and
A_{-1}\equiv 1/2\pi\ell
for
\gamma_{i}
in
d=2
.

The functions
\chi_{\rm tot}(t)
are determined by the bosonic/fermionic physical particle spectrum of the theory. They take the form of a “bulk”
minus
an “edge” character:

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm tot}=\chi_{\rm bulk}-\chi_{\rm edge}\,.

(6)

The bulk character
\chi_{\rm bulk}(t)
is defined as follows. Single-particle states on global dS
d+1
furnish a representation
R
of the isometry group
SO(1,d+1)
. The content of
R
is encoded in its Harish-Chandra character
\tilde{\chi}(g)\equiv{\rm tr}\,R(g)
(appendix
A
).
Restricted to
SO(1,1)
isometries
g=e^{-itH}
acting as time translations on the static patch,
\tilde{\chi}(g)
becomes
\chi_{\rm bulk}(t)\equiv{\rm tr}\,e^{-itH}
.
For example for a massive integer spin-
s
particle it is given by (
208
):

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm bulk,s}=D_{s}^{d}\,\frac{q^{\frac{d}{2}+i\nu}+q^{\frac{d}{2}-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{d}}\,,\qquad\quad q\equiv e^{-|t|/\ell}\,,

(7)

where
D_{s}^{d}
is the spin degeneracy (
209
), e.g.
D_{s}^{3}=2s+1
, and
\nu
is related to the mass:

s=0:\,\nu^{2}=m^{2}\ell^{2}-\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2}\bigr)^{2}\,,\quad\qquad s\geq 1:\,\nu^{2}=m^{2}\ell^{2}-\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2}+s-2\bigr)^{2}\,.

(8)

For arbitrary massive matter
\chi_{\rm bulk}
is given by (
210
).
Massless spin-
s
characters are more intricate, but can be obtained by applying a simple “flipping” recipe (
100
) to (
98
), or from the general formulae (
358
) or (
388
) derived from this. Some low
(d,s)
examples are

\small\begin{array}[]{r|ccccccccc}(d,s)&&(2,1)&(2,2)&&(3,1)&(3,2)&&(4,1)&(4,2)\\
\hline\cr\\[-14.22636pt]
\chi_{\rm bulk,s}&&\displaystyle\frac{2\,q}{(1-q)^{2}}&0&&\displaystyle\frac{6\,q^{2}-2\,q^{3}}{(1-q)^{3}}&\displaystyle\frac{10\,q^{3}-6\,q^{4}}{(1-q)^{3}}&&\displaystyle\frac{6\,q^{2}}{(1-q)^{4}}&\displaystyle\frac{10\,q^{2}}{(1-q)^{4}}\end{array}

(9)

The
q
-expansion of
\chi_{\rm bulk}
gives the static patch
quasinormal
mode degeneracies, its Fourier transform gives the
normal
mode spectral density, and the bulk part of (
4
) is the quasicanonical ideal gas partition function at
\beta=2\pi\ell
, as we explain below (
15
).

The edge character
\chi_{\rm edge}(t)
is inferred from path integral considerations in sections
3
-
5
. It vanishes for spin
s<1
. For integer
s\geq 1
we get (
85

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm edge,s}=N_{s}\cdot\frac{q^{\frac{d-2}{2}+i\nu}+q^{\frac{d-2}{2}-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}\,,\qquad N_{s}=D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,,

(10)

e.g.
N_{1}=1
,
N_{2}=d+2
. Note this is the bulk character of
N_{s}
scalars in
two lower
dimensions. Thus the edge correction effectively
subtracts
the degrees of freedom of
N_{s}
scalars living on
S^{d-1}
,
the horizon “edge” of static time slices (yellow dot in fig.
1
). (
91
) yields analogous results for more general matter; e.g.

bulk field
\to

edge field,

bulk
\to(d+2)\times{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}
edge.
For massless spin-
s

\small\begin{array}[]{r|ccccccccc}(d,s)&&(2,1)&(2,2)&&(3,1)&(3,2)&&(4,1)&(4,2)\\
\hline\cr\\[-14.22636pt]
\chi_{\rm edge,s}&&0&0&&\displaystyle\frac{2\,q}{1-q}&\displaystyle\frac{10\,q^{2}-2\,q^{3}}{1-q}&&\displaystyle\frac{2\,q}{(1-q)^{2}}&\displaystyle\frac{10\,q}{(1-q)^{2}}\end{array}

(11)

The term

with

\mbox{integrand}=\cdots+\frac{\alpha_{d+1}}{t}+O(t^{0})

Overview

We summarize the content of sections

-
9

Quasicanonical bulk thermodynamics of the static patch (section
2
)

Figure 3:
Regularized dS
2
scalar mode density with
\nu=2
,
\Lambda_{\rm uv}\ell\approx 4000
. Blue line = Fourier transform of
\chi_{\rm bulk}
:
\rho(\omega)/\ell=\frac{2}{\pi}\log(\Lambda_{\rm uv}\ell)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum\psi\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\pm i\nu\pm i\omega\ell)
. Red dots = inverse eigenvalue spacing of numerically diagonalized
4000\times 4000
matrix
H
in globally truncated model (appendix
B.2
). Rightmost panel =
|\rho(\omega)|
on complex
\omega
-plane, with quasinormal mode poles at
\omega\ell=\pm i(\frac{1}{2}\pm i\nu+n)
.

The global dS bulk character
\chi_{\rm bulk}(t)={\rm tr}\,e^{-itH}
locally encodes the quasinormal spectrum and normal mode density of the static patch
ds^{2}=-(1-r^{2}/\ell^{2})dT^{2}+(1-r^{2}/\ell^{2})^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega^{2}
on which
e^{-itH}
acts as a time translation
T\to T+t
.
Its expansion in powers of
q=e^{-|t|/\ell}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm bulk}=\sum_{r}\,N_{r}\,q^{r}\,,

(14)

yields the number
N_{r}
of
quasinormal
modes decaying as
e^{-rT/\ell}
, in resonance with
Ng:2012xp
;
Jafferis:2013qia
;
Sun:2020sgn
. The density of
normal
modes
\propto e^{-i\omega T}
is formally given by its Fourier transform

\displaystyle\rho(\omega)\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,\chi_{\rm bulk}(t)\,e^{i\omega t}.

(15)

Because
\chi_{\rm bulk}
is singular at
t=0
, this is ill-defined as it stands. However, a standard Pauli-Villars regularization of the QFT renders it regular (
40
), yielding a manifestly covariantly regularized mode density, analytically calculable for arbitrary particle content, including gravitons and higher-spin matter. Some simple examples are shown in figs.
3
,
6
. Quasinormal modes appear as resonance poles at
\omega=\pm ir
, seen by substituting (
14
) into (
15
).

This effectively solves the problem of making covariant sense of the formally infinite normal mode density universally arising in the presence of a horizon
tHooft:1984kcu
. Motivated by the fact that semiclassical information loss can be traced back to this infinity,
tHooft:1984kcu
introduced a rough model getting rid of it by shielding the horizon by a “brick wall” (reviewed together with variants in
E.3
).
Evidently this alters the physics, introduces boundary artifacts, breaks covariance, and is, unsurprisingly, computationally cumbersome. The covariantly regularized density (
15
) suffers none of these problems.

In particular it makes sense of the
a priori
ill-defined canonical ideal gas partition function,

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm can}(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\Bigl(-\rho_{\rm bos}(\omega)\,\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}-e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)+\rho_{\rm fer}(\omega)\,\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}+e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)\Bigr)\,.

(16)

Substituting (
15
) and integrating out
\omega
, this becomes

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi^{\rm bos}_{\rm bulk}(t)\,-\,\frac{2\,e^{-\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi_{\rm bulk}^{\rm fer}(t)\biggr)}

(17)

At the static patch equilibrium
\beta=2\pi\ell
, this is precisely the
bulk
contribution to the one-loop Euclidean partition function
\log{\cal Z}^{(1)}
in (
4
). Although
Z_{\rm bulk}
is not quite a standard canonical partition function, calling it a quasicanonical partition function appears apt.

From (
17
), covariantly regularized quasicanonical bulk thermodynamic quantities can be analytically computed for general particle content, as illustrated in section
2.3
. Substituting the expansion (
14
) expresses these quantities as a sum of quasinormal mode contributions, generalizing and refining
Denef:2009kn
. In particular the contribution to the entropy and heat capacity from each physical quasinormal mode is finite and positive (fig.
8
).

S_{\rm bulk}
can alternatively be viewed as a covariantly regularized entanglement entropy between two hemispheres in the global dS Euclidean vacuum (red and blue lines in figs.
5
,
14
). In the spirit of
Jafferis:2013qia
, the quasinormal modes can then be viewed as entangled quasiqubits.

Sphere partition functions (sections
3
,
4
,
5
)

In sections
3
-
5
we obtain character integral formulae computing exact heat-kernel regularized one-loop sphere partition functions
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
for general field content, leading to (
4
).

For scalars and spinors (section
3
), this is easy.
For massive spin
s
(section
4
), the presence of conformal Killing tensors on the sphere imply naive reduction to a spin-
s
Laplacian determinant is inconsistent with locality
Tseytlin:2013fca
. The correct answer can in principle be obtained by path integrating the full off-shell action
Zinoviev:2001dt
, but this involves an intricate tower of spin
s^{\prime}<s
Stueckelberg fields. Guided by intuition from section
2
, we combine locality and unitary constraints with path integral considerations to find the terms in
\log Z
missed by naive reduction. They turn out to be obtained simply by extending the spin-
s
Laplacian eigenvalue sum to include its “subterranean” levels with formally negative degeneracies, (
83
). The extra terms capture contributions from unmatched spin
s^{\prime}<s
conformal Killing tensor ghost modes in the gauge-fixed Stueckelberg path integral. The resulting sum yields the bulk
-
edge character integral formula (
84
). Locality and unitarity uniquely determine the generalization to arbitrary parity-symmetric matter representations, (
91
).

In the massless case (section
5
), new subtleties arise: negative modes requiring contour rotations (which translate into the massless character “flipping” recipe
mentioned above (
9
)), and ghost zeromodes which must be omitted and compensated by a carefully normalized group volume division.
Non-universal factors cancel out, yielding (
112
) modulo renormalization.

3D de Sitter HS
n
quantum gravity and the topological string (section
6
)

Figure 4:

One- and all-loop entropy corrections, and dual topological string
t
,
g_{s}
, for 3D HS
n
theory in its maximal-entropy de Sitter vacuum, for different values of
n
at fixed
{\cal S}^{(0)}=10^{8}
,
l=0
.

The
{\rm sl}(2)
Chern-Simons formulation of 3D gravity
Witten:1988hc
;
Witten:1989ip
can be extended to an
{\rm sl}(n)
Chern-Simons formulation of
s\leq n
higher-spin (HS
n
) gravity
Blencowe:1988gj
;
Bergshoeff:1989ns
;
Castro:2011fm
;
Perlmutter:2012ds
. The action for positive cosmological constant is given by (
121
). It
has a real coupling constant
\kappa\propto 1/G_{\rm N}
, and an integer coupling constant
l\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
if a gravitational Chern-Simons term is included.

This theory has a landscape of dS
3
vacua, labeled by partitions
\vec{m}=\{m_{1},m_{2},\ldots\}
of
n
. Different vacua have different values of
\ell/G_{\rm N}
, with tree-level entropy

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}_{{\vec{m}}}=\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G_{\rm N}}\biggr|_{{\vec{m}}}=2\pi\kappa\cdot T_{\vec{m}}\,,\qquad T_{\vec{m}}=\mbox{$\frac{1}{6}\sum_{a}m_{a}(m_{a}^{2}-1)$}\,.

(18)

The number of vacua grows as
{\cal N}_{\rm vac}\sim e^{2\pi\sqrt{n/6}}
. The maximal entropy vacuum is
\vec{m}=\{n\}
.

We obtain the all-loop exact quantum entropy
{\cal S}_{{\vec{m}}}=\log{\cal Z}_{\vec{m}}

by analytic continuation
k_{\pm}\to l\pm i\kappa
of the
SU(n)_{k_{+}}\times SU(n)_{k_{-}}
Chern-Simons partition function on
S^{3}
, (
127
).
In the weak-coupling limit
\kappa\to\infty
, this reproduces
{\cal S}^{(1)}
as computed by (
4
) in the metric-like formulation of the theory, given in (
12
) for the maximal-entropy vacuum
\vec{m}=\{n\}
.

When
n
grows large and reaches a value
n\sim\kappa
, the 3D higher-spin gravity theory becomes strongly coupled. (In the vacuum
\vec{m}=\{n\}
this means
n^{4}\sim\ell/G_{\rm N}
.) In this regime, Gopakumar-Vafa duality
Gopakumar:1998ki
;
Marino:2004uf
can be used to express the quantum de Sitter entropy
{\cal S}
in terms of a weakly-coupled topological string partition function on the resolved conifold, (
128
):

\displaystyle\boxed{{\cal S}_{\vec{m}}=\log\left|\tilde{Z}_{\rm top}(g_{s},t)\,e^{-\pi T_{\vec{m}}\cdot 2\pi i/g_{s}}\right|^{2}}

(19)

where
g_{s}=\frac{2\pi}{n+l+i\kappa}
, and the conifold Kähler modulus
t\equiv\int_{S^{2}}J+iB=ig_{s}n=\frac{2\pi in}{n+l+i\kappa}
.

Euclidean thermodynamics of the static patch (section
7
)

In section
7
we consider the Euclidean thermodynamics of a QFT on a fixed static patch/sphere background. The partition function
Z_{\rm PI}
is the Euclidean path integral on the sphere of radius
\ell
, the Euclidean energy density is
\rho_{\rm PI}=-\partial_{V}\log Z_{\rm PI}
, where
V=\Omega_{d+1}\ell^{d+1}
is the volume of the sphere, and the entropy is

S_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm PI}+2\pi\ell\,U_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm PI}+V\rho_{\rm PI}=(1-V\partial_{V})\log Z_{\rm PI}
, or

\displaystyle\boxed{S_{\rm PI}=\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{d+1}\ell\partial_{\ell}\bigr)\log Z_{\rm PI}}

(20)

Using the exact one-loop sphere partition functions obtained in sections
3
-
5
, this allows general exact computation of the one-loop Euclidean entropy
S_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
, illustrated in section
7.2
. Euclidean Rindler results are recovered in the limit
m\ell\to\infty
. The sphere computation avoids introducing the usual conical deficit angle, varying the curvature radius
\ell
instead.

For minimally coupled scalars,
S_{\rm PI}^{(1)}=S_{\rm bulk}
, but more generally this is false, due to edge (and other) corrections. Our results thus provide a precise and general version of observations made in the work reviewed in appendix
E.5
. Of note, these “corrections” actually
dominate
the one-loop entropy, rendering it negative, increasingly so as
s
grows large.

Quantum gravitational thermodynamics (section
8
)

In section
8
(with details in appendix
I
), we specialize to theories with dynamical gravity. Denoting
Z_{\rm PI}
,
\rho_{\rm PI}
and
S_{\rm PI}
by
{\cal Z}
,
\varrho
and
{\cal S}
in this case, (
20
) trivially implies
\varrho=0
,
{\cal S}=\log{\cal Z}
, reproducing (
1
). All UV-divergences can be absorbed into renormalized coupling constants, rendering the Euclidean thermodynamics well-defined in an effective field theory sense.

Integrating over the geometry is similar in spirit to integrating over the temperature in statistical mechanics, as one does to extract the microcanonical entropy
S(U)
from the canonical partition function.
3
3

3

Along the lines of
S(U)=\log\bigl(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\frac{d\beta}{\beta}\,{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\beta H+\beta U}\bigr)
, with contour
\beta=\beta_{*}+iy
,
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for any
\beta_{*}>0
.

The analog of this in the case of interest is

\displaystyle S(\rho)\equiv\log\int{\cal D}g\,\cdots\,e^{-S_{E}[g,\ldots]\,+\,\rho\int\!\sqrt{g}}\,,

(21)

for some suitable metric path integration contour. In particular
S(0)={\cal S}
. The analog of the microcanonical
\beta\equiv\partial_{U}S
is
V\equiv\partial_{\rho}S
, and the analog of the microcanonical free energy is the Legendre transform
\log Z\equiv S-V\rho
, satisfying
\rho=-\partial_{V}\log Z
.
If we furthermore
define

\ell
by
\Omega_{d+1}\ell^{d+1}\equiv V
, the relation between
\log Z
,
\rho
and
S
is by construction identical to (
20
).

Equivalently, the free energy
\Gamma\equiv-\log Z
can be thought of as a quantum effective action for the volume. At tree level,
\Gamma
equals the classical action
S_{E}
evaluated on the round sphere of radius
\ell
. For example for 3D Einstein gravity,

\displaystyle\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{8\pi G}\bigl(-\Lambda\,\ell^{3}+3\,\ell\bigr)\,,\qquad S^{(0)}=\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{3}\ell\partial_{\ell}\bigr)\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}\,.

(22)

The tree-level on-shell radius
\ell_{0}
maximizes
\log Z^{(0)}
, i.e.
\rho^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=0
.

We define renormalized
\Lambda,G,\ldots
from the
\ell^{d+1},\ell^{d-1},\ldots
coefficients in the
\ell\to\infty
expansion of the quantum
\log Z
, and fix counterterms by equating tree-level and renormalized couplings for the UV-sensitive subset. For 3D Einstein, the renormalized one-loop correction is

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}=-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)\,.

(23)

The
quantum
on-shell radius
\bar{\ell}=\ell_{0}+O(G)
maximizes
\log Z
, i.e.
\rho(\bar{\ell})=0
. The on-shell entropy
can be expressed in two equivalent ways to this order:

\displaystyle\boxed{{\cal S}=S^{(0)}(\bar{\ell})+S^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})+\log Z^{(1)}}

(24)

This clarifies why the one-loop correction
{\cal S}^{(1)}\equiv{\cal S}-{\cal S}^{(0)}
to the dS entropy is given by
\log Z^{(1)}
rather than
S^{(1)}
: the extra term
-V\rho^{(1)}
accounts for the change in entropy of the reservoir (= geometry) due to energy transfer to the system (= quantum fluctuations).

The final result is (
4
).
We work out several examples in detail. We consider higher-order curvature corrections and discuss invariance under local field redefinitions, identifying the invariants
{\cal S}_{M}^{(0)}=-S_{E}[g_{M}]
for different saddles
M
as and their large-
\ell
expanded quantum counterparts
{\cal S}_{M}
as the
\Lambda>0
analogs of tree-level and quantum scattering amplitudes, defining invariant couplings and physical observables of the low-energy effective field theory.

dS, AdS
±
, and conformal higher-spin gravity (section
9
)

Massless
{\mathfrak{g}}={\rm hs}({\rm so}(d+2))
higher-spin gravity theories on dS
d+1
or
S^{d+1}

Vasiliev:1990en
;
Vasiliev:2003ev
;
Bekaert:2005vh
have infinite spin range and infinite
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}
, obviously posing problems for the one-loop formula (
4
):

1.

Spin sum divergences untempered by the UV cutoff, for example
\dim G=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{s}s(4s^{2}-1)
for
d=3
and
\chi_{\rm tot}=\sum_{s}\mbox{{\small$(2s+1)$}}\frac{2q^{2}}{(1-q)^{4}}-\sum_{s}\frac{s(s+1)(2s+1)}{6}\frac{2q}{(1-q)^{2}}
for
d=4
.

2.

Unclear how to make sense of
{\rm vol}\,G
.

We compare the situation to analogous one-loop expressions
Basile:2018zoy
;
Basile:2018acb
;
Sun:2020ame
for Euclidean AdS with standard (AdS
{}^{+}_{d+1}
)
Giombi:2013fka
;
Giombi:2014iua
;
Gunaydin:2016amv
;
Giombi:2016pvg
;
Skvortsov:2017ldz
and alternate (AdS
{}^{-}_{d+1}
)
Giombi:2013yva
gauge field boundary conditions, and to the associated conformal higher-spin theory on the boundary
S^{d}
(CHS
d
)
Tseytlin:2013fca
;
Tseytlin:2013jya
. For AdS
+
the above problems are absent, as
{\mathfrak{g}}
is not gauged and
\Delta_{s}>s
. Like a summed KK tower, the spin-summed bulk character has increased UV dimensionality
d^{\rm bulk}_{\rm eff}=2d-2
. However, the edge character almost completely cancels this, leading to a
reduced

d_{\rm eff}=d-1
in (
181
)-(
183
). This realizes a version of a stringy picture painted in
Susskind:1994sm
repainted in fig.
18
. A HS “swampland” is identified: lacking a holographic dual, characterized by
d_{\rm eff}>d-1
.

For AdS
-
and CHS, the problems listed for dS all reappear.
{\mathfrak{g}}
is gauged, and the character spin sum divergences are identical to dS, as implied by the relations (
187
):

\displaystyle\boxed{\chi_{s}({\rm CHS}_{d})=\chi_{s}({\rm AdS}_{d+1}^{-})-\chi_{s}({\rm AdS}_{d+1}^{+})=\chi_{s}({\rm dS}_{d+1})-2\,\chi_{s}({\rm AdS}_{d+1}^{+})}

(25)

The spin sum divergences are not UV. Their origin lies in low-energy features: an infinite number of quasinormal modes decaying as slowly as
e^{-2T/\ell}
for
d\geq 4
(cf. discussion below (
361
)). We see no justification for zeta-regularizing such divergences away. However, in certain supersymmetric extensions, the spin sum divergences cancel in a rather nontrivial way, leaving a finite residual as in (
194
). This eliminates problem 1, but leaves problem 2. Problem 2 might be analogous to
{\rm vol}\,G=\infty
for the bosonic string or
{\rm vol}\,G=0
for supergroup Chern-Simons: removed by appropriate insertions. This, and more, is left to future work.

2
Quasicanonical bulk thermodynamics

Figure 5:

a:
Penrose diagram of global dS, showing flows of
SO(1,1)
generator
H=M_{0,d+1}
, S = southern static patch.
b:
Wick-rotated
S
= sphere; Euclidean time = angle.
c:

Pelagibacter ubique
inertial observer in dS with
\ell=1.2\,\mu{\rm m}
finds itself immersed in gas of photons, gravitons and higher-spin particles at a pleasant
30{}^{\circ}{\rm C}
. More details are provided in fig.
14
and appendix
D.3
.

2.1
Problem and results

From the point of view of an inertial observer, such as
Pelagibacter ubique
in fig.
5
c
, the global de Sitter vacuum appears thermal
PhysRevD.15.2738
;
PhysRevD.15.2752
;
Israel:1976ur
:
P. ubique
perceives
its universe, the southern static patch (S in fig.
5
a
),

ds^{2}=-(1-r^{2}/\ell^{2})dT^{2}+(1-r^{2}/\ell^{2})^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega_{d-1}^{2},

(26)

as a static ball of finite volume, whose boundary
r=\ell
is a horizon at temperature
T=1/2\pi\ell
, and whose bulk is populated by field quanta in thermal equilibrium with the horizon.

P. ubique
wishes to understand its universe, and figures the easiest thing to understand should be the thermodynamics of its thermal environment in the ideal gas approximation. The partition function of an ideal gas is

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\beta H}=\exp\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\Bigl(-\rho(\omega)_{\rm bos}\,\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}-e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)+\rho(\omega)_{\rm fer}\,\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}+e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)\Bigr),

(27)

where
\rho(\omega)=\rho(\omega)_{\rm bos}+\rho(\omega)_{\rm fer}
is the density of bosonic and fermionic single-particle states at energy
\omega
. However to its dismay, it immediately runs into trouble: the dS static patch mode spectrum is continuous and infinitely degenerate, leading to a pathologically divergent density
\rho(\omega)=\delta(0)\sum_{\ell m\cdots}
. It soon realizes the unbounded redshift is to blame, so it imagines a brick wall excising the horizon, or some variant thereof (appendix
E.3
). Although this allows some progress, it is aware this alters what it is computing and depends on choices. To check to what extent this matters, it tries to work out nontrivial examples. This turns out to be painful. It feels there should be a better way, but its efforts come to an untimely end
pubiqueend
.

Here we will make sense of the density of states and the static patch bulk thermal partition function in a different way, manifestly preserving the underlying symmetries, allowing general exact results for arbitrary particle content.
The main ingredient is the Harish-Chandra group character
(reviewed in appendix
A
) of the
SO(1,d+1)
representation
R
furnished by the physical single-particle Hilbert space of the free QFT quantized on
global
dS
d+1
. Letting
H
be the global
SO(1,1)
generator acting as time translations in the southern static patch and globally as in fig.
5
a
, the character restricted to group elements
e^{-itH}
is

\displaystyle\chi(t)\equiv{\rm tr}_{G}\,e^{-itH}\,.

(28)

Here
{\rm tr}_{G}
traces over the
global
dS single-particle Hilbert space furnishing
R
. (More generally we denote
{\,{\rm tr}\,}

\equiv
single-particle trace,
{\rm Tr}

\equiv
multi-particle trace,
G

\equiv
global,
S

\equiv
static patch. Our default units set the dS radius
\boxed{\ell\equiv 1}
.)

For example for a scalar field of mass
m^{2}=(\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}
, as computed in (
207
),

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\frac{e^{-t\Delta_{+}}+e^{-t\Delta_{-}}}{\left|1-e^{-t}\right|^{d}}\,,\qquad\Delta_{\pm}=\tfrac{d}{2}\pm i\nu\,.

(29)

For a massive spin-
s
field this simply gets an additional spin degeneracy factor
D_{s}^{d}
, (
208
). Massless spin-
s
characters take a similar but somewhat more intricate form, (
358
)-(
360
).

As mentioned in the introduction, (
14
), the character has a series expansion

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\sum_{r}N_{r}\,e^{-r|t|}

(30)

encoding the degeneracy
N_{r}
of quasinormal modes
\propto e^{-rT}
of the dS static patch background. For example expanding the scalar character yields two towers of quasnormal modes with
r_{n\pm}=\frac{d}{2}\pm i\nu+n
and degeneracy
N_{n\pm}={n+d-1\choose n}
.

Our main result, shown in
2.2
below, is the observation that

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi(t)_{\rm bos}\,-\,\frac{2\,e^{-\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi(t)_{\rm fer}\biggr)}\,,

(31)

suitably regularized,
provides a physically sensible, manifestly covariant regularization of the static patch bulk thermal partition. The basic idea is that
\rho(\omega)
can be obtained as a well-defined Fourier transform of the covariantly UV-regularized character
\chi(t)
, which upon substitution in the ideal gas formula (
27
) yields the above character integral formula.
Arbitrary thermodynamic quantities at the horizon equilibrium
\beta=2\pi
can be extracted from this in the usual way, for example
S_{\rm bulk}=(1-\beta\partial_{\beta})\log Z_{\rm bulk}|_{\beta=2\pi}
,
which can alternatively be interpreted as the “bulk” entanglement entropy between the northern and southern
S^{d}
hemispheres (red and blue lines fig.
5
a
).
4
4

4

In part because subregion entanglement entropy does not exist in the continuum, an infinity of different notions of it exist in the literature
Lin:2018bud
. Based on
Soni:2016ogt
,
S_{\rm bulk}
appears perhaps most akin to the “extractable”/“distillable” entropy considered there. Either way, our results are nomenclature-independent.
We work out various examples of such thermodynamic quantities in section
2.3
. General exact solution are easily obtained. The expansion (
30
) also allows interpreting the results as a sum over quasinormal modes along the lines of
Denef:2009kn
.

Figure 6:
Regularized scalar
\rho(\omega)
,
d=2
,
\nu=2,i/2,0.9\,i
; top:
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}
; bottom:
\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}
, showing quasinormal mode poles. See figs.
10
,
13
for details.

We conclude this part with some comments on the relation with the Euclidean partition function. As reviewed in appendix
E
, general physics considerations, or formal considerations based on Wick-rotating the static patch to the sphere and slicing the sphere path integral along the lines of fig.
5
b
, suggests a relation between the one-loop Euclidean path integral
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
on
S^{d+1}
and the bulk ideal gas thermal partition function
Z_{\rm bulk}
at
\beta=2\pi
. More refined considerations suggest

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}+\mbox{edge corrections}\,,

(32)

where the edge corrections are associated with the
S^{d-1}
horizon edge of the static patch time slices, i.e. the yellow dot in fig.
5
. The formal slicing argument breaks down here, as does the underlying premise of spatial separability of local field degrees of freedom (for fields of spin
s\geq 1
). Similar considerations apply to other thermodynamic quantities and in other contexts, reviewed in appendix
E
and more specifically
E.5
.

In sections
3
-
5
we will obtain the exact edge corrections by direct computation, logically independent of these considerations, but guided by the physical expectation (
32
) and more generally the intuition developed in this section.

2.2
Derivation

We first give a formal derivation and then refine this by showing the objects of interest become rigorously well-defined in a manifestly covariant UV regularization of the QFT.

Formal derivation

Our starting point is the observation that the thermal partition function
{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\beta H}
of a bosonic resp. fermionic oscillator of frequency
\omega
has the integral representation (
306
):

\displaystyle-\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}-e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\bigl(e^{-i\omega t}+e^{i\omega t}\bigr)

(33)

\displaystyle\log\bigl(e^{\beta\omega/2}+e^{-\beta\omega/2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{2\,e^{-\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\bigl(e^{-i\omega t}+e^{i\omega t}\bigr)\,.

with the pole in the factor
f(t)=c\,t^{-2}+O(t^{0})

multiplying
e^{-i\omega t}+e^{i\omega t}
resolved by

\displaystyle t^{-2}\to\frac{1}{2}\bigl((t-i\epsilon)^{-2}+(t+i\epsilon)^{-2}\bigr).

(34)

Now consider a free QFT on some space of finite volume, viewed as a system
S
of bosonic and/or fermionic oscillator modes of frequencies
\omega
with mode (or single-particle) density
\rho_{S}(\omega)=\rho_{S}(\omega)_{\rm bos}+\rho_{S}(\omega)_{\rm fer}
. The system is in thermal equilibrium at inverse temperature
\beta
. Using the above integral representation, we can write its thermal partition function (
27
) as

\displaystyle\log{\rm Tr}_{S}\,e^{-\beta H_{S}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi_{S}(t)_{\rm bos}\,-\,\frac{2\,e^{-\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi_{S}(t)_{\rm fer}\biggr)\,,

(35)

where we exchanged the order of integration, and we defined

\displaystyle\chi_{S}(t)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\,\rho_{S}(\omega)\bigl(e^{-i\omega t}+e^{i\omega t}\bigr)

(36)

We want to apply (
35
) to a free QFT on the southern static patch at inverse temperature
\beta
, with the goal of finding a better way to make sense of it than
P. ubique
’s approach.
To this end, we note that the global dS
d+1
Harish-Chandra character
\chi(t)
defined in (
28
) can formally be written in a similar form by
using the general property (
198
),
\chi(t)=\chi(-t)
:

\displaystyle\chi(t)={\rm tr}_{G}\,e^{-iHt}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\,\rho_{G}(\omega)\,e^{-i\omega t}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\,\rho_{G}(\omega)\bigl(e^{-i\omega t}+e^{i\omega t}\bigr)\,,

(37)

This looks like (
36
), except

\rho_{G}(\omega)={\rm tr}_{G}\,\delta(\omega-H)
is the density of single-particle excitations of the
global
Euclidean vacuum, while
\rho_{S}(\omega)
is the density of single-particle excitations of the
southern
vacuum. The global and southern vacua are very different. Nevertheless, there is a simple kinematic relation between their single-particle creation and annihilation operators: the Bogoliubov transformation (
302
) (suitably generalized to
d>0

Israel:1976ur
). This provides an explicit one-to-one, inner-product-preserving map between southern and global single-particle states with
H=\omega>0
. Hence, formally,

\displaystyle\rho_{S}(\omega)=\rho_{G}(\omega)\,\qquad(\omega>0)\,,\qquad\quad\rho_{S}(\omega)=0\qquad(\omega<0)\,.

(38)

While formal in the continuum, this relation becomes precise whenever
\rho
is rendered effectively finite, e.g. by a brick-wall cutoff or by considering finite resolution projections (say if we restrict to states emitted/absorbed by some apparatus built by
P. ubique
).

At first sight this buys us nothing though, as computing
\rho_{G}(\omega)={\rm tr}_{G}\,\delta(\omega-H)
for say a scalar in dS
4
in a basis
|\omega\ell m\rangle_{G}
immediately leads to
\rho_{G}(\omega)=\delta(0)\sum_{\ell m}
, in reassuring but discouraging agreement with
P. ubique
’s result for
\rho_{S}(\omega)
.
On second thought however, substituting this into (
37
) leads to a nonsensical
\chi(t)=2\pi\delta(t)\delta(0)\sum_{\ell m}
, not remotely resembling the correct expression (
29
).
How could this happen? As explained under (
211
), the root cause is the seemingly natural but actually ill-advised idea of computing
\chi(t)={\rm tr}_{G}\,e^{-iHt}
by diagonalizing
H
: despite its lure of seeming simplicity,
|\omega\ell m\rangle_{G}
is in fact the
worst
possible choice of basis to compute the character trace. Its wave functions on the global future boundary
S^{d}
of dS
d+1
are singular at the north and south pole, exactly the fixed points of
H
at which the correct computation of
\chi(t)
in appendix
A.2
localizes. Although
|\omega\ell m\rangle
is a perfectly fine basis on the cylinder obtained by a conformal map from sphere, the information needed to compute
\chi
is irrecoverably lost by this map.

However we can turn things around, and use the properly computed
\chi(t)
to extract
\rho_{G}(\omega)
as its Fourier transform, inverting (
37
). As it stands, this is not really possible, for (
29
) implies
\chi(t)\sim|t|^{-d}
as
t\to 0
, so its Fourier transform does not exist. Happily, this problem is automatically resolved by standard UV-regularization of the QFT, as we will show explicitly below. For now let us proceed formally, as at this level we have arrived at our desired result: combining (
38
) with (
37
) and (
36
) implies
\chi_{S}(t)=\chi(t)
, which by (
35
) yields

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{S}\,e^{-\beta H_{S}}=Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)\qquad\mbox{(formal)}

(39)

with
Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)
as defined in (
31
). The above equation formally gives it its claimed thermal interpretation. In what follows we will make this a bit more precise, and spell out the UV regularization explicitly.

Covariant UV regularization of
\rho
and
Z_{\rm bulk}

We begin by showing that
\rho_{G}(\omega)
in (
37
) becomes well-defined in a suitable standard UV-regularization of the QFT.
As in
Demers:1995dq
, it is convenient to consider Pauli-Villars regularization, which is manifestly covariant and has a conceptually transparent implementation on both the path integral and canonical sides. For e.g. a scalar of mass
m^{2}=(\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}
, a possible implementation is adding
{k\choose n}
,
n=1,\ldots,k\geq\frac{d}{2}
fictitious particles of mass
m^{2}=(\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}
and positive/negative norm for even/odd
n
,
5
5

5

This is equivalent to inserting a heat kernel regulator
f(\tau\Lambda^{2})=\bigl(1-e^{-\tau\Lambda^{2}}\bigr)^{k}
in (
66
), with
k\geq\frac{d}{2}+1
.

turning the character
\chi_{\nu^{2}}(t)
of (
29
) into

\displaystyle\chi_{\nu^{2},\Lambda}(t)={\rm tr}_{G_{\Lambda}}\,e^{-itH}=\sum_{n=0}^{k}(-1)^{n}\mbox{\large${k\choose n}$}\,\chi_{\nu^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}}(t).

(40)

This effectively replaces
\chi(t)\sim|t|^{-d}
by
\chi_{\Lambda}(t)\sim|t|^{2k-d}
with
2k-d\geq 0
, hence, assuming
\chi(t)
falls off exponentially at large
t
, which is always the case for unitary representations
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
,
\chi_{\Lambda}(t)
has a well-defined Fourier transform, analytic in
\omega
:

\displaystyle\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,\chi_{\Lambda}(t)\,e^{i\omega t}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,\chi_{\Lambda}(t)\,\bigl(e^{i\omega t}+e^{-i\omega t}\bigr)\,.

(41)

The above character regularization can immediately be transported to arbitrary massive
SO(1,d+1)
representations, as their characters
\chi_{s,\nu^{2}}
(
210
) only differ from the scalar one by an overall spin degeneracy factor.
6
6

6

For massless spin-
s
, the PV-regulating characters to add to the physical character (e.g. (
102
),(
359
)) are
\hat{\chi}_{s,n}=\chi_{s,\nu_{\phi}^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}}-\chi_{s-1,\nu_{\xi}^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}}
where
\nu_{\phi}^{2}=-(s-2+\frac{d}{2})^{2}
and
\nu_{\xi}^{2}=-(s-1+\frac{d}{2})^{2}
, based on (
95
) and (
97
).

Although we won’t need to in practice for computations of thermodynamic quantities (which are most easily extracted directly as character integrals),
\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)
can be computed explicitly. For the dS
d+1
scalar, using (
29
) regularized with
k=1
, we get for
\omega\ll\Lambda

\displaystyle d=1:

\displaystyle\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)=\frac{2}{\pi}\log\Lambda-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\pm,\pm}\psi\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\pm i\nu\pm i\omega)+O(\Lambda^{-1})

(42)

\displaystyle d=2:

\displaystyle\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)=\Lambda-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}(\omega\pm\nu)\coth\bigl(\pi(\omega\pm\nu)\bigr)+O(\Lambda^{-1})

where
\psi(x)=\Gamma^{\prime}(x)/\Gamma(x)
. Denoting the
\Lambda
-independent parts of the above
\omega\ll\Lambda
expansions by
\tilde{\rho}_{\nu^{2}}(\omega)
, the exact
\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)
for general
\omega
and
k
is
\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{k}(-1)^{n}{k\choose n}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\nu^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}}(\omega)
, illustrated in fig.
7
for
k=1,2
. The
\omega\ll\Lambda
result is independent of
k
up to rescaling of
\Lambda
. The result for massive higher-spin fields is the same up to an overall degeneracy factor
D_{s}^{d}
from (
208
).

Figure 7:
\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)
for dS
d+1
scalar of mass
m^{2}=(\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}
,
\nu=10
, for
d=1,2
in
k=1,2
Pauli-Villars regularizations (
40
). Faint part is unphysical UV regime
\omega\gtrsim\Lambda
.
The peaks/kinks appearing at
\omega=\sqrt{\nu^{2}+n\Lambda^{2}}
are related to quasinormal mode resonances
\langle\ref{sec:resonances}\rangle
.

To make sense of the southern static patch density
\rho_{S}(\omega)
directly in the continuum, we
define
its regularized version by mirroring the formal relation (
38
), thus ensuring all of the well-defined features and physics this relation encapsulates are preserved:

\displaystyle\rho_{S,\Lambda}(\omega)\equiv\rho_{G,\Lambda}(\omega)=(\ref{PVregdost})\,\qquad(\omega>0)\,.

(43)

This definition of the regularized static patch density evidently inherits all of the desirable properties of
\rho_{G}(\omega)
: manifest general covariance, independence of arbitrary choices such as brick wall boundary conditions, and exact analytic computability.
The physical sensibility of this identification is also supported by the fact that the quasinormal mode expansion (
30
) of
\chi(t)
produces the physically expected static patch quasinormal resonance pole structure
\rho_{S}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{r,\pm}\frac{N_{r}}{r\pm i\omega}
, cf. appendix
B.3
.

Putting things together in the way we obtained the formal relation (
39
), the correspondingly regularized version of the static patch thermal partition function (
35
) is then

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm bulk,\Lambda}(\beta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi_{\Lambda}(t)_{\rm bos}\,-\,\frac{2\,e^{-\pi t/\beta}}{1-e^{-2\pi t/\beta}}\,\,\chi_{\Lambda}(t)_{\rm fer}\biggr)}

(44)

Note that if we take
k\geq\frac{d}{2}+1
, then

\chi_{\Lambda}(t)\sim t^{2k-d}
with
2k-d\geq 2
and we can drop the
i\epsilon
prescription (
34
).
Z_{\rm bulk}
(or equivalently
\chi
) can be regularized in other ways, including by cutting off the integral at
t=\Lambda^{-1}
, or as in (
215
), or by dimensional regularization. For most of the paper we will use yet another variant, defined in section
3
, equivalent, like Pauli-Villars, to a manifestly covariant heat-kernel regularization of the path integral.

In view of the above observations,
Z_{\rm bulk,\Lambda}(\beta)
is naturally interpreted as a well-defined, covariantly regularized and ambiguity-free
definition
of the static patch ideal gas thermal partition in the continuum.
However we refrain from denoting
Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)
as
{\rm Tr}_{S,\Lambda}\,e^{-\beta H_{S}}
, because it is not constructed as an actual sum over states of some definite regularized static patch Hilbert space
{\cal H}_{S,\Lambda}
. This (together with the role of quasinormal modes) is also why we referred to
Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)
as a “quasi”-canonical partition function in the introduction.

2.3
Example computations

In this section we illustrate the use and usefulness of the character formalism by computing some examples of bulk thermodynamic quantities at the equilibrium inverse temperature
\beta=2\pi
of the static patch. The precise relation of these quantities with their Euclidean counterparts will be determined in
3
-
5
and
7
.

Character formulae for bulk thermodynamic quantities at
\beta=2\pi

At
\beta=2\pi
, the bulk free energy, energy, entropy and heat capacity are obtained by taking the appropriate derivatives of (
31
) and putting
\beta=2\pi
, using the standard thermodynamic relations
F=-\frac{1}{\beta}\log Z
,
U=-\partial_{\beta}\log Z
,
S=\log Z+\beta U
,

C=-\beta\partial_{\beta}S
.
Denoting
q\equiv e^{-t}
,

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm bulk}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,\,\chi_{\rm bos}\,-\,\frac{2\sqrt{q}}{1-q}\,\,\chi_{\rm fer}\biggr),

(45)

\displaystyle 2\pi U_{\rm bulk}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2}\biggl(-\frac{2\sqrt{q}}{1-q}\,\frac{\sqrt{q}}{1-q}\,\chi_{\rm bos}+\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,\frac{\sqrt{q}}{1-q}\,\chi_{\rm fer}\biggr),

(46)

and similarly for
S_{\rm bulk}
and
C_{\rm bulk}
.
The characters
\chi
for general massive representation are given by (
210
), for massless spin-
s
representations by (
358
)-(
360
), and for partially massless
(s,s^{\prime})
representations by (
388
). Regularization is implicit here.

Leading divergent term

The leading
t\to 0
divergence of the scalar character (
29
) is
\chi(t)\sim 2/t^{d}
. For more general representations this becomes
\chi(t)\sim 2n/t^{d}
with
n
the number of on-shell internal (spin) degrees of freedom. The generic leading divergent term of the bulk (free) energy is then given by
F_{\rm bulk},U_{\rm bulk}\sim-\frac{1}{\pi}(n_{\rm bos}-n_{\rm fer})\int\!\frac{dt}{t^{d+2}}\sim\pm\Lambda^{d+1}\ell^{d}
, while for the bulk heat capacity and entropy we get
C_{\rm bulk},S_{\rm bulk}\sim(\frac{1}{3}n_{\rm bos}+\frac{1}{6}n_{\rm fer})\int\!\frac{dt}{t^{d}}\sim+\Lambda^{d-1}\ell^{d-1}
, where we reinstated the dS radius
\ell
.
In particular
S_{\rm bulk}\sim+\Lambda^{d-1}\times\mbox{horizon area}
, consistent with an entanglement entropy area law. The energy diverges more strongly because we included the QFT zero point energy term in its definition, which drops out of
S
and
C
.

Coefficient of log-divergent term

The coefficient of the logarithmically divergent part of these thermodynamic quantities is universal. A pleasant feature of the character formalism is that this coefficient can be read off trivially as the coefficient of the
1/t
term in the small-
t
expansion of the integrand, easily computed for any representation. In odd
d+1
, the integrand is even in
t
, so log-divergences are absent. In even
d+1
, the integrand is odd in
t
, so generically we do get a log-divergence
=a\log\Lambda
.
For example the
\log Z
integrand for a dS
2
scalar is expanded as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2t}\frac{1+e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\frac{e^{-t(\frac{1}{2}+i\nu)}+e^{-t(\frac{1}{2}-i\nu)}}{1-e^{-t}}=\frac{2}{t^{3}}+\frac{\frac{1}{12}-\nu^{2}}{t}+\cdots\quad\Rightarrow\quad a=\tfrac{1}{12}-\nu^{2}\,.

(47)

For a
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu
spin-
s
particle in even
d+1
, the
\log\Lambda
coefficient for
U_{\rm bulk}
is similarly read off as
a_{U_{\rm bulk}}=-D^{d}_{s}\frac{1}{\pi(d+1)!}\prod_{n=0}^{d}(\Delta-n)
. For a conformally coupled scalar,
\nu=i/2
, so
a_{U_{\rm bulk}}=0
. Some examples of
a_{S_{\rm bulk}}=a_{\log Z_{\rm bulk}}
in this case are

\begin{array}[]{l|ccccccccccc}d+1&2&4&6&8&10&\cdots&100&\cdots&1000&\cdots\\
\hline\cr a_{S_{\rm bulk}}&\frac{1}{3}&-\frac{1}{90}&\frac{1}{756}&-\frac{23}{113400}&\frac{263}{7484400}&\cdots&-8.098\times 10^{-34}&\cdots&-3.001\times 10^{-306}&\cdots\end{array}

Finite part and exact results

\bullet

Energy:
For future reference (comparison to previously obtained results in section
7
), we consider dimensional regularization here. The absence of a
1/t
factor in the integral (
46
) for
U_{\rm bulk}
then allows straightforward evaluation for general
d
. For a scalar of mass
m^{2}=(\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}
,

\displaystyle U^{\rm fin}_{\rm bulk}=\frac{m^{2}\cosh(\pi\nu)\,\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}+i\nu)\,\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}-i\nu)}{2\pi\,\Gamma(d+2)\cos(\frac{\pi d}{2})}\qquad\mbox{(dim reg)}\,.

(48)

For example for
d=2
, this becomes

\displaystyle U^{\rm fin}_{\rm bulk}=-\frac{1}{12}(\nu^{2}+1)\nu\coth(\pi\nu)\,.

(49)

\bullet

Free energy:
The UV-finite part of the
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
integral (
45
) for a massive field in even
d
can be computed simply by extending the integration contour to the real line avoiding the pole, closing the contour and summing residues.
For example for a
d=2
scalar this gives

\displaystyle\log Z^{\rm fin}_{\rm bulk}=\frac{\pi\nu^{3}}{6}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{\nu^{k}}{k!}\,\frac{{\rm Li}_{3-k}(e^{-2\pi\nu})}{(2\pi)^{2-k}}\,,

(50)

where
{\rm Li}_{n}
is the polylogarithm,
{\rm Li}_{n}(x)\equiv\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{x^{k}}/{k^{n}}
. For future reference, note that

\displaystyle{\rm Li}_{1}(e^{-2\pi\nu})=-\log(1-e^{-2\pi\nu})\,,\qquad{\rm Li}_{0}(e^{-2\pi\nu})=\frac{1}{e^{2\pi\nu}-1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\coth(\pi\nu)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,.

(51)

For odd
d
, the character does not have an even analytic extension to the real line, so a different method is needed to compute
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
. The exact evaluation of arbitrary character integrals, for any
d
and any
\chi(t)
is given in (
251
) in terms of Hurwitz zeta functions. Simple examples are given in (
253
)-(
254
). In (
251
) we use the covariant regularization scheme introduced in section
3
. Conversion to PV regularization is obtained from the finite part as explained below.

\bullet

Entropy
: Combined with our earlier result for the bulk energy
U_{\rm bulk}
, the above also gives the finite part of the bulk entropy
S_{\rm bulk}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}+2\pi U_{\rm bulk}
. In the Pauli-Villars regularization (
40
), the UV-divergent part is obtained from the finite part by mirrorring (
40
). For example for
k=1
,
S_{\rm bulk,\Lambda}=S_{\rm bulk}^{\rm fin}|_{\nu^{2}}-S_{\rm bulk}^{\rm fin}|_{\nu^{2}+\Lambda^{2}}
. For the
d=2
example this gives for
\nu\ll\Lambda

\displaystyle S_{\rm bulk,\Lambda}=\frac{\pi}{6}(\Lambda-\nu)-\frac{\pi}{3}\,\nu\,{\rm Li}_{0}(e^{-2\pi\nu})-\sum_{k=0}^{3}\frac{\nu^{k}}{k!}\,\frac{{\rm Li}_{3-k}(e^{-2\pi\nu})}{(2\pi)^{2-k}}\,,

(52)

where we used (
51
).

S_{\rm bulk}
decreases monotonically with
m^{2}=1+\nu^{2}
. In the massless limit
m\to 0
, it diverges logarithmically:
S_{\rm bulk}=-\log m+\cdots
. For
\nu\gg 1
,
S_{\rm bulk}=\frac{\pi}{6}(\Lambda-\nu)
up to exponentially small corrections. Thus
S_{\rm bulk}>0
within the regime of validity of the low-energy field theory, consistent with its quasi-canonical/entanglement entropy interpretation. For a conformally coupled scalar
\nu=\frac{i}{2}
, this gives
S^{\rm fin}_{\rm bulk}=\frac{3\zeta(3)}{16\pi^{2}}-\frac{\log(2)}{8}
.

Quasinormal mode expansion

Substituting the quasinormal mode expansion (
30
),

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\sum_{r}N_{r}e^{-rt}

(53)

in the PV-regularized
\log Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)
(
44
), rescaling
t\to\frac{\beta}{2\pi}t
, and using (
263
) gives

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)=\sum_{r}N_{r}^{\rm bos}\log\frac{\Gamma(br+1)}{(b\mu)^{br}\sqrt{2\pi br}}-N_{r}^{\rm fer}\log\frac{\Gamma(br+\frac{1}{2})}{(b\mu)^{br}\sqrt{2\pi}}\,,\qquad b\equiv\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\,.

(54)

Truncating the integral to the IR part (
263
) is justified because the Pauli-Villars sum (
40
) cancels out the UV part. The dependence on
\mu
likewise cancels out, as do some other terms, but it is useful to keep the above form.
At the equilibrium
\beta=2\pi
,
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
is given by (
54
) with
b=1
. This provides a PV-regularized version of the quasinormal mode expansion of
Denef:2009kn
. Since it is covariantly regularized, it does not require matching to a local heat kernel expansion. Moreover it applies to general particle content, including spin
s\geq 1
.
7
7

7

The expansion of
Denef:2009kn
pertains to
Z_{\rm PI}
for
s\leq\frac{1}{2}
. In the following sections we show
Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{\rm bulk}
for
s\leq\frac{1}{2}
but not for
s\geq 1
. Hence in general the QNM expansion of
Denef:2009kn
computes
Z_{\rm bulk}
, not
Z_{\rm PI}
.

Figure 8:
Contribution to
\beta=2\pi
bulk entropy and heat capacity of a quasinormal mode
\propto e^{-rT}
,
r\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
{\rm Re}\,r>0
. Only the real part is shown here because complex
r
come in conjugate pairs
r_{n,\pm}=\frac{d}{2}+n\pm i\nu
. The harmonic oscillator case corresponds to the imaginary axis.

QNM expansions of other bulk thermodynamic quantities are readily derived from (
54
) by taking derivatives
\beta\partial_{\beta}=b\partial_{b}=\mu\partial_{\mu}+r\partial_{r}
.
For example
S_{\rm bulk}=(1-\beta\partial_{\beta})\log Z_{\rm bulk}|_{\beta=2\pi}
is

\displaystyle S_{\rm bulk}=\sum_{r}N^{\rm bos}_{r}s_{\rm bos}(r)+N^{\rm fer}_{r}s_{\rm fer}(r)

(55)

where the entropy
s(r)
carried by a single QNM
\propto e^{-rT}
at
\beta=2\pi
is given by

s_{\rm bos}(r)=r+(1-r\partial_{r})\log\frac{\Gamma(r+1)}{\sqrt{2\pi r}}\,,\qquad s_{\rm fer}(r)=-r-(1-r\partial_{r})\log\frac{\Gamma(r+\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,.

(56)

Note the
\mu
-dependence has dropped out, reflecting the fact that the contribution of each
individual
QNM to the entropy is UV-finite, not requiring any regularization. For massive representations,
r
can be complex, but will always appear in a conjugate pair
r_{n\pm}=\frac{d}{2}+n\pm i\nu
.
Taking this into account, all contributions to the entropy are real and positive for the physical part of the PV-extended spectrum.
The small and large
r
asymptotics are

\displaystyle r\to 0:\,s_{\rm bos}\to\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{e}{2\pi r}\,,\quad s_{\rm fer}\to\frac{\log 2}{2}\,,\qquad r\to\infty:\,s_{\rm bos}\to\frac{1}{6\,r}\,,\quad s_{\rm fer}\to\frac{1}{12\,r}\,.

(57)

The QNM entropies at general
\beta
are obtained simply by replacing

\displaystyle r\to\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\,r\,.

(58)

The entropy of a normal bosonic mode of frequency
\omega
,
\tilde{s}(\omega)=-\log\bigl(1-e^{-\beta\omega}\bigr)+\frac{\beta\omega}{e^{\beta\omega}-1}
, is recovered for complex conjugate pairs
r_{\pm}
in the scaling limit
\beta\to 0
,
\beta\nu=\omega
fixed, and likewise for fermions. At any finite
\beta
, the
n\to\infty
UV tail of QNM contributions is markedly different however. Instead of falling off exponentially, if falls off as
s\sim 1/n
. PV or any other regularization effectively cuts off the sum at
n\sim\Lambda\ell
, so since
N_{n}\sim n^{d-1}
,
S_{\rm bulk}\sim\Lambda^{d-1}\ell^{d-1}
.

The bulk heat capacity
C_{\rm bulk}=-\beta\partial_{\beta}S_{\rm bulk}
, so the heat capacity of a QNM at
\beta=2\pi
is

\displaystyle c(r)=-r\partial_{r}s(r)\,.

(59)

The real part of
s(r)
and
c(r)
on the complex
r
-plane are shown in fig.
8
.

An application of the quasinormal expansion

The above QNM expansions are less useful for exact computations of thermodynamic quantities than the direct integral evaluations discussed earlier, but can be very useful in computations of certain UV-finite quantities. A simple example is the following. In thermal equilibrium with a 4D dS static patch horizon, which set of particle species has the largest bulk heat capacity: (A) six conformally coupled scalars + graviton, (B) four photons? The answer is not obvious, as both have an equal number of local degrees of freedom:
6+2=4\times 2=8
. One could compute each in full, but the above QNM expansions offers a much easier way to get the answer. From (
29
) and (
359
) we read off the scalar and massless spin-
s
characters:

\displaystyle\chi_{0}=\frac{q+q^{2}}{(1-q)^{3}}\,,\qquad\chi_{s}=\frac{2(2s+1)\,q^{s+1}-2(2s-1)\,q^{s+2}}{(1-q)^{3}}\,,

(60)

where
q=e^{-|t|}
.
We see
\chi_{A}-\chi_{B}=\chi_{2}+6\,\chi_{0}-4\,\chi_{1}=6\,q
, so
\chi_{A}
and
\chi_{B}
are
almost
exactly equal: A has just 6 more quasinormal modes than B, all with
r=1
. Thus, using (
59
),

\displaystyle C_{\rm bulk}^{A}-C_{\rm bulk}^{B}=6\cdot c_{\rm bos}(1)=\pi^{2}-9\,.

(61)

Pretty close, but
\pi>3

pihist
, so A wins. The difference is
\Delta C\approx 0.87
. Along similar lines,
\Delta S=6\,s_{\rm bos}(1)=3(2\gamma+1-\log(2\pi))\approx 0.95
.

Another UV-finite example: relative entropies of graviton, photon, neutrino

Less trivial to compute but more real-world in flavor is the following UV-finite linear combination of the 4D graviton, photon, and (assumed massless) neutrino bulk entropies:

\displaystyle S_{\text{graviton}}+\tfrac{60}{7}S_{\text{neutrino}}-\tfrac{37}{7}S_{\text{photon}}=\tfrac{48}{7}\zeta^{\prime}(-1)-\tfrac{60}{7}\zeta^{\prime}(-3)+6\gamma+\tfrac{149}{56}-\tfrac{33}{14}\log(2\pi)\approx 0.61

(62)

Finiteness can be checked from the small-
t
expansion of the total integrand computing this, and the integral can then be evaluated along the lines of (
247
)-(
248
). We omit the details.

Vasiliev higher-spin example

Non-minimal Vasiliev higher-spin gravity on dS
4
has a single conformally coupled scalar and a tower of massless spin-
s
particles of all spins
s=1,2,3,\ldots
. The prospect of having to compute bulk thermodynamics for this theory by brick wall or other approaches mentioned in appendix
E.3
would be terrifying. Let us compare this to the character approach. The
total
character obtained by summing the characters of (
60
) takes a remarkably simple form:

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm tot}\,=\,\chi_{0}+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\chi_{s}\,=\,2\cdot\biggl(\frac{q^{1/2}+q^{3/2}}{(1-q)^{2}}\biggr)^{2}-\frac{q}{(1-q)^{2}}\,\,=\,\,\frac{q+q^{3}}{(1-q)^{4}}+3\cdot\frac{2q^{2}}{(1-q)^{4}}\,.

(63)

The first expression is two times the square of the character of a 3D conformally coupled scalar, plus the character of 3D conformal higher-spin gravity (
188
).
8
8

8

For AdS
4
, the analogous
\chi_{\rm tot}
equals
one
copy of the 3D scalar character squared, reflecting the single-trace spectrum of its holographic dual
U(N)
model
\langle\ref{sec:AdSswamp}\rangle
. The dS counterpart thus encodes the single-trace spectrum of two copies of this 3D CFT + 3D CHS gravity, reminiscent of
Alishahiha:2004md
. This is generalized by (
187
).

The second expression equals the character of one
\nu=i
and three
\nu=0
scalars on dS
5
. Treating the character integral as such, we immediately get, in
k=3
Pauli-Villars regularization (
40
),

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm bulk}^{\rm div}

\displaystyle=a_{0}\Lambda^{5}+a_{2}\Lambda^{3}-a_{4}\Lambda\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm bulk}^{\rm fin}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\zeta(5)}{4\pi^{4}}-\tfrac{\zeta(3)}{24\pi^{2}}

(64)

\displaystyle S_{\rm bulk}^{\rm div}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{25}{4}a_{2}\Lambda^{3}-\tfrac{103}{20}a_{4}\Lambda\,,\qquad

\displaystyle S_{\rm bulk}^{\rm fin}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\zeta(5)}{4\pi^{4}}-\tfrac{\zeta(3)}{24\pi^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{20}\,.

where
a_{0}=\frac{1-4\sqrt{2}+3\sqrt{3}}{10}\,\pi\approx 0.17
,
a_{2}=-\frac{1-2\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{3}}{12}\,\pi\approx 0.025
, and
a_{4}=\frac{3-3\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{3}}{48}\,\pi\approx 0.032
. The tower of higher-spin particles alters the
bulk
UV dimensionality much like a tower of KK modes would. (We will later see edge “corrections” rather dramatically alter this.)

3
Sphere partition function for scalars and spinors

3.1
Problem and result

In this section we consider the one-loop Gaussian Euclidean path integral
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
of scalar and spinor field fluctuations on the round sphere.
For a free scalar of mass
m^{2}
on
S^{d+1}
,

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}=\int{\cal D}\phi\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\phi(-\nabla^{2}+m^{2})\phi}\,,

(65)

A convenient UV-regularized version is defined using standard heat kernel methods
Vassilevich:2003xt
:

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI,\epsilon}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\tau(-\nabla^{2}+m^{2})}\,.

(66)

The insertion
e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}
implements a UV cutoff at length scale
\sim\epsilon
. We picked this regulator for convenience in the derivation below. We could alternatively insert the PV regulator of footnote
5
, which would reproduce the PV regularization (
40
). However, being uniformly applicable to all dimensions, the above regulator is more useful for the purpose of deriving general evaluation formulae, as in appendix
C
.

In view of (
32
) we wish to compare
Z_{\rm PI}
to the corresponding Wick-rotated dS static patch bulk thermal partition function
Z_{\rm bulk}(\beta)
(
31
), at the equilibrium inverse temperature
\beta=2\pi
. Here and henceforth,
Z_{\rm bulk}
by default means
Z_{\rm bulk}(2\pi)
:

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm bulk}\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\biggl(\,\frac{1+e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\,\chi(t)_{\rm bos}\,-\,\frac{2\,e^{-t/2}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\,\chi(t)_{\rm fer}\biggr)

(67)

Below we show that for free scalars and spinors,

\displaystyle\boxed{Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{\rm bulk}}

(68)

with the specific regularization (
66
) for
Z_{\rm PI}
mapping to a specific regularization (
73
) for
Z_{\rm bulk}
. The relation is exact, for any
\epsilon
. This makes the physical expectation (
32
) precise, and shows that for scalars and spinors, there are in fact no edge corrections.

In appendix
C
we provide a simple recipe for extracting both the UV and IR parts in the
\epsilon\to 0
limit in the above regularization, directly from the unregularized form of the character formula (
67
). This yields the general closed-form solution
[formula]
for the regularized
Z_{\rm PI}
in terms of Hurwitz zeta functions. The heat kernel coefficient invariants are likewise read off from the character using (
252
). For simple examples see (
253
), (
254
), (
264
)-(
265
).

3.2
Derivation

The derivation is straightforward:

Scalars:

The eigenvalues of
-\nabla^{2}
on a sphere of radius
\ell\equiv 1
are
\lambda_{n}=n(n+d)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with degeneracies
D^{d+2}_{n}
given by (
209
), that is
D_{n}^{d+2}={n+d+1\choose d+1}-{n+d-1\choose d+1}
.
Thus (
66
) can be written as

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}e^{-\tau\nu^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{d+2}_{n}\,e^{-\tau(n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}}\,,\qquad\nu\equiv\sqrt{m^{2}-\tfrac{d^{2}}{4}}\,.

(69)

To perform the sum over
n
, we use the Hubbard-Stratonovich trick, i.e. we write

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{d+2}_{n}\,e^{-\tau(n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}}=\int_{A}du\,\frac{e^{-u^{2}/4\tau}}{\sqrt{4\pi\tau}}\,f(u)\,,\qquad f(u)\equiv\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{d+2}_{n}\,e^{iu(n+\frac{d}{2})}\,.

(70)

with integration contour
A={\mathbb{R}}+i\delta
,
\delta>0
, as shown in fig.
9
. The sum evaluates to

\displaystyle f(u)=\frac{1+e^{iu}}{1-e^{iu}}\,\frac{e^{i\frac{d}{2}u}}{(1-e^{iu})^{d}}\,,

(71)

Figure 9:
Integration contours for
Z_{\rm PI}
. Orange dots are poles, yellow dots branch points.

We first consider the case
m>\frac{d}{2}
, so
\nu
is real and positive. Then, keeping
{\rm Im}\,u=\delta<\epsilon
, we can perform the
\tau
-integral first in (
69
) to get

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int_{A}\frac{du}{2\sqrt{u^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}}\,e^{-\nu\sqrt{u^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}}\,f(u)\,.

(72)

Deforming the contour by folding it up along the two sides of the branch cut to contour
B
in fig.
9
, changing variables
u=it
and using that the square root takes opposite signs on both sides of the cut, we transform this to an integral over
C
in fig.
9
:

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\!\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}\,\frac{1+e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\frac{e^{-\frac{d}{2}t+i\nu\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}+e^{-\frac{d}{2}t-i\nu\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}}{(1-e^{-t})^{d}}\,,

(73)

The result for
0<m\leq\frac{d}{2}
, i.e.
\nu=i\mu
with
0\leq\mu<\frac{d}{2}
can be obtained from this by analytic continuation.
Putting
\epsilon=0
, this formally becomes

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\chi(t)\,,\qquad\chi(t)=\frac{e^{-(\frac{d}{2}-i\nu)t}+e^{-(\frac{d}{2}+i\nu)t}}{(1-e^{-t})^{d}}\,,

(74)

which we recognize as (
67
) with
\chi(t)
the scalar character (
29
). Thus we conclude that for scalars,
Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{\rm bulk}
, with
Z_{\rm PI}
regularized as in (
66
) and
Z_{\rm bulk}
as in (
73
).

Spinors:

For a Dirac spinor field of mass
m
we have
Z_{\rm PI}=\int{\cal D}\psi\,e^{-\int\bar{\psi}(\not{\nabla}+m)\psi}
. The relevant formulae for spectrum and degeneracies for general
d
can be found in appendices
D.1
and
F.2
. For
concreteness we just consider the case
d=3
here, but the conclusions are valid for Dirac spinors in general. The spectrum of
\not{\nabla}+m
on
S^{4}
is
\lambda_{n}=m\pm(n+2)i
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with degeneracy
D^{5}_{n+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}=4{n+3\choose 3}
, so
Z_{\rm PI}
regularized as in (
66
) is given by

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mbox{$4{n+3\choose 3}$}\,e^{-\tau((n+2)^{2}+m^{2})}

(75)

Following the same steps as for the scalar case, this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\log Z_{\text{PI}}=-\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\!\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}\,\frac{2\,e^{-t/2}}{1-e^{-t}}\cdot 4\cdot\frac{e^{-\frac{3}{2}t+im\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}+e^{-\frac{3}{2}t-im\sqrt{t^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}}}{(1-e^{-t})^{3}}\,.

(76)

Putting
\epsilon=0
, this formally becomes

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{2\,e^{-t/2}}{1-e^{-t}}\,\,\chi(t)\,,\qquad\chi(t)=4\cdot\frac{e^{-(\frac{3}{2}+im)t}+e^{-(\frac{3}{2}-im)t}}{(1-e^{-t})^{3}}\,,

(77)

which we recognize as the fermionic (
67
) with
\chi(t)
the character of the
\Delta=\frac{3}{2}+im
unitary
SO(1,4)
representation carried by the single-particle Hilbert space of a Dirac spinor quantized on dS
4
, given by twice the character (
210
) of the irreducible representation
(\Delta,S)
with
S=(\frac{1}{2})
. Thus we conclude
Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{\rm bulk}
. The comment below (
93
) generalizes this to all
d
.

4
Massive higher spins

We first formulate the problem, explaining why it is not nearly as simple as one might have hoped, and then state the result, which turns out to be much simpler than one might have feared. The derivation of the result is detailed in appendix
F.1
.

4.1
Problem

Consider a massive spin-
s\geq 1
field, more specifically a totally symmetric tensor field
\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}
on dS
d+1
satisfying the Fierz-Pauli equations of motion:

\displaystyle\bigl(-\nabla^{2}+\overline{m}_{s}^{2}\bigr)\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}=0\,,\qquad\nabla^{\nu}\phi_{\nu\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s-1}}=0\,,\qquad{\phi^{\nu}}_{\nu\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s-2}}=0\,.

(78)

Upon quantization, the global single-particle Hilbert space furnishes a massive spin-
s
representation of
SO(1,d+1)
with
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu
, related to the effective mass
\overline{m}_{s}
appearing above (see e.g.
Sleight:2017krf
), and to the more commonly used definition of mass
m
(see e.g.
Hinterbichler:2016fgl
) as

\displaystyle\overline{m}_{s}^{2}=(\tfrac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}+s\,,\qquad m^{2}=(\tfrac{d}{2}+s-2)^{2}+\nu^{2}=(\Delta+s-2)(d+s-2-\Delta)\,.

(79)

Then
m=0
for the photon, the graviton and their higher-spin generalizations, and for
s=1
,
m
is the familiar spin-1 Proca mass.

The massive spin-
s

bulk
thermal partition function is immediately obtained by substituting the massive spin-
s
character (
208
) into the character formula (
67
) for
Z_{\rm bulk}
. For
d\geq 3
,
9
9

9

For
d=2
, the single-particle Hilbert space splits into
(\Delta,\pm s)
with
D_{\pm s}^{2}=1
, so
D^{2}_{s}\to\sum_{\pm}D^{2}_{\pm s}=2
.

\displaystyle\log Z_{{\rm bulk}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,\,D^{d}_{s}\cdot\frac{q^{\frac{d}{2}+i\nu}+q^{\frac{d}{2}-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{d}}\,,\qquad q=e^{-t}\,,

(80)

with spin degeneracy factor read off from (
209
) or (
283
).

The corresponding free massive spin-
s
Euclidean path integral on
S^{d+1}
takes the form

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\,e^{-S_{E}[\Phi]}.

(81)

where
\Phi
includes at least
\phi
. However it turns out that in order to write down a local, manifestly covariant action for massive fields of general spin
s
, one also needs to include a tower of auxiliary Stueckelberg fields of all spins
s^{\prime}<s

Zinoviev:2001dt
, generalizing the familiar Stueckelberg action (
340
) for massive vector fields. These come with gauge symmetries, which in turn require the introduction of a gauge fixing sector, with ghosts of all spins
s^{\prime}<s
. The explicit form of the action and gauge symmetries is known, but intricate
Zinoviev:2001dt
.

Classically, variation of the action with respect the Stueckelberg fields merely enforces the transverse-traceless (TT) constraints in (
78
), after which the gauge symmetries can be used to put the Stueckelberg fields equal to zero. One might therefore hope the intimidating off-shell
Z_{\rm PI}
(
81
) likewise collapses to just the path integral
Z_{\rm TT}
over the TT modes of
\phi
with kinetic term given by the equations of motion (
78
). This is easy to evaluate. The TT eigenvalue spectrum on the sphere follows from
SO(d+2)
representation theory. As detailed in eqs. (
321
)-(
323
), we can then follow the same steps as in section
3
, ending up with
10
10

10

For
d=2
,
D^{4}_{n,s}\to\sum_{\pm}D^{4}_{n,\pm s}=2D^{4}_{n,s}
.

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm TT}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\bigl(q^{i\nu}+q^{-i\nu}\bigr)\sum_{n\geq s}D^{d+2}_{n,s}\,q^{\frac{d}{2}+n}\,.

(82)

Here
D^{d+2}_{n,s}
is the dimension of the
SO(d+2)
representation labeled by the two-row Young diagram
(n,s)
, given explicitly by the dimension formulae in appendix
D.1
.

Unfortunately,
Z_{\rm TT}
is
not
equal to
Z_{\rm PI}
on the sphere. The easiest way to see this is to consider an example in odd spacetime dimensions, such as (
324
), and observe the result has a logarithmic divergence. A manifestly covariant local QFT path integral on an odd-dimensional sphere cannot possibly have logarithmic divergences. Therefore
Z_{\rm PI}\neq Z_{\rm TT}
.
The appearance of such nonlocal divergences in
Z_{\rm TT}
can be traced to the existence of (normalizable) zeromodes in tensor decompositions on the sphere
Fradkin:1983mq
;
Tseytlin:2013fca
. For example the decomposition
\phi_{\mu}=\phi_{\mu}^{T}+\nabla_{\mu}\varphi
has the constant
\varphi
mode as a zeromode,
\phi_{\mu\nu}=\phi_{\mu\nu}^{\rm TT}+\nabla_{(\mu}\varphi_{\nu)}+g_{\mu\nu}\varphi
has conformal Killing vector zeromodes, and
\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}=\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}^{\rm TT}+\nabla_{(\mu_{1}}\varphi_{\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{s})}+g_{(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\varphi_{\mu_{3}\cdots\mu_{s})}
has rank
s-1
conformal Killing tensor zeromodes.
As shown in
Fradkin:1983mq
;
Tseytlin:2013fca
, this implies
\log Z_{\rm TT}
contains a nonlocal UV-divergent term
c_{s}\,\log\Lambda
, where
c_{s}
is the number of rank
s-1
conformal Killing tensors. This divergence cannot be canceled by a local counterterm. Instead it must be canceled by contributions from the non-TT part. Thus, in principle, the full off-shell path integral must be carefully evaluated to obtain the correct result.
Computing
Z_{\rm PI}
for general
s
on the sphere is not as easy as one might have hoped.

4.2
Result

Rather than follow a brute-force approach, we obtain
Z_{\rm PI}
in appendix
F.1
by a series of relatively simple observations. In fact, upon evaluating the sum in (
82
), writing it in a way that brings out a term
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
as in (
80
), and observing a conspicuous finite sum of terms bears full responsibility for the inconsistency with locality, the answer suggests itself right away: the non-TT part restores locality simply by canceling this finite sum. This turns out to be equivalent to the non-TT part effectively extending the sum
n\geq s
in (
82
) to
n\geq-1
:
11
11

11

For
d=2
use (
91
):
D^{4}_{n,s}\to\sum_{\pm}D^{4}_{n,\pm s}=2D^{4}_{n,s}
for
n>-1
, and
D^{4}_{-1,s}\to\sum_{\pm}\frac{1}{2}D^{4}_{-1,\pm s}=D^{4}_{-1,s}=D^{4}_{s-1}
.

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\bigl(q^{i\nu}+q^{-i\nu}\bigr)\sum_{n\geq-1}D^{d+2}_{n,s}\,q^{\frac{d}{2}+n}\,,

(83)

where
D^{d+2}_{n,s}
is given by the explicit formulae in appendix
D.1
, in particular (
284
). For
n<s
, this is no longer the dimension of an
SO(d+2)
representation, but it can be rewritten as
minus
the dimension of such a representation, as
D^{d+2}_{n,s}=-D^{d+2}_{s-1,n+1}
.
This extension also turns out to be exactly what is needed for consistency with the unitarity bound (
339
) and more refined unitarity considerations. A limited amount of explicit path integral considerations combined with the observation that the coefficients
D^{d+2}_{s-1,n+1}
count conformal Killing tensor mode mismatches between ghosts and longitudinal modes then suffice to establish this is indeed the correct answer. We refer to appendix
F.1
for details.

Using the identity (
329
), we can write this in a rather suggestive form:

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}-\log Z_{\rm edge}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\bigl(\chi_{\rm bulk}-\chi_{\rm edge}\bigr)}\,\,,

(84)

where

\chi_{\rm bulk}
and
\chi_{\rm edge}
are explicitly given by
12
12

12

For
d=2
,
D_{s}^{2}\to\sum_{\pm}D_{s}^{2}=2
in
\chi_{\rm bulk}
as in (
80
).
\chi_{\rm edge}
remains unchanged.

\displaystyle\boxed{\chi_{\rm bulk}\equiv D_{s}^{d}\,\frac{q^{\frac{d}{2}+i\nu}+q^{\frac{d}{2}-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{d}}\,,\qquad\chi_{\rm edge}\equiv D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,\frac{q^{\frac{d-2}{2}+i\nu}+q^{\frac{d-2}{2}-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}}

(85)

The
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
term is the character integral for the bulk partition function (
80
). Strikingly, the correction
\log Z_{\rm edge}

also
takes the form a character integral, but with an “edge” character
\chi_{\rm edge}
in
two lower
dimensions. By our results of section
3
for scalars,
Z_{\rm edge}
effectively equals the Euclidean path integral of
D_{s-1}^{d+2}
scalars of mass
\tilde{m}^{2}=\bigl(\tfrac{d-2}{2}\bigr)^{2}+\nu^{2}
on
S^{d-1}
:

\displaystyle Z_{\rm edge}=\mbox{\Large$\int$}{\cal D}\phi\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{d-1}}\phi^{a}(-\nabla^{2}+\tilde{m}^{2})\phi^{a}}\,,\qquad a=1,\ldots,D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,,

(86)

In particular this gives 1 scalar for
s=1
and
d+2
scalars for
s=2
. The
S^{d-1}
is naturally identified as the static patch horizon, the edge of the global dS spatial
S^{d}
hemisphere at time zero, the yellow dot in fig.
5
. Thus (
84
) realizes in a precise way the somewhat vague physical expectation (
32
). Notice the relative minus sign here and in (
84
): the edge corrections effectively
subtract
degrees of freedom.
We do not have a physical interpretation of these putative edge scalars for general
s
along the lines of the work reviewed in appendix
E.5.3
. Some clues are that their multiplicity equals the number of conformal Killing tensor modes of scalar type appearing in the derivation in appendix
F.1
(the

-modes for
s=4
in (
335
)), and that they become massless
at the unitarity bound
\nu=\pm i(\frac{d}{2}-1)
, eq. (
339
), where a partially massless field emerges with a scalar gauge parameter.

Independent of any interpretation, we can summarize the result (
84
)-(
85
) as

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}^{d+1}(s)=\log Z_{\rm bulk}^{d+1}(s)-D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,\log Z_{\rm PI}^{d-1}(0)\,.

(87)

Examples

For a
d=2
spin-
s\geq 1
field of mass
m^{2}=(s-1)^{2}+\nu^{2}
,
\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int\frac{dt}{2t}\frac{1+q}{1-q}(\chi_{\rm bulk}-\chi_{\rm edge})
with

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm bulk}=2\,\frac{q^{1+i\nu}+q^{1-i\nu}}{(1-q)^{2}},\qquad\chi_{\rm edge}=s^{2}(q^{i\nu}+q^{-i\nu})\,.

(88)

That is,
Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{\rm bulk}/Z_{\rm edge}
, with the finite part of
\log Z_{\rm bulk}
explicitly given by twice (
50
), and with
Z_{\rm edge}
equal to the Euclidean path integral of
D^{4}_{s-1}=s^{2}
harmonic oscillators of frequency
\nu
on
S^{1}
, naturally identified with the
S^{1}
horizon of the dS
3
static patch, with finite part

\displaystyle Z_{\rm edge}^{\rm fin}=\biggl(\frac{e^{-\pi\nu}}{1-e^{-2\pi\nu}}\biggr)^{s^{2}}\,.

(89)

The heat-kernel regularized
Z_{\rm PI}
is then, restoring
\ell
and recalling
\nu=\sqrt{m^{2}\ell^{2}-(s-1)^{2}}
,

\log Z_{\rm PI}=2\biggl(\!\frac{\pi\nu^{3}}{6}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{\nu^{k}}{k!}\,\frac{{\rm Li}_{3-k}(e^{-2\pi\nu})}{(2\pi)^{2-k}}-\frac{\pi\nu^{2}\ell}{4\epsilon}+\frac{\pi\ell^{3}}{2\epsilon^{3}}\biggr)-s^{2}\biggl(\!-\pi\nu-\log(1-e^{-2\pi\nu})+\frac{\pi\ell}{\epsilon}\biggr)

(90)

The
d=3
spin-
s
case is worked out as another example in (
C.2
).

General massive representations

(
83
) has a natural generalization, presented in appendix
F.2
, to arbitrary parity-invariant massive
SO(1,d+1)
representations
R=\oplus_{a}(\Delta_{a},S_{a})
,
\Delta_{a}=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu_{a}
,
S_{a}=(s_{a1},\ldots,s_{ar})
:

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{2t}\sum_{a}(-1)^{F_{a}}\bigl(q^{i\nu_{a}}+q^{-i\nu_{a}}\bigr)\sum_{n\in\frac{F_{a}}{2}+{\mathbb{Z}}}\Theta\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2}+n\bigr)\,D^{d+2}_{n,S_{a}}\,q^{\frac{d}{2}+n}}

(91)

where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside step step function with
\boxed{\Theta(0)\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}}
, and
F_{a}=0,1
for bosons resp. fermions. This is the unique TT eigenvalue sum extension consistent with locality and unitarity constraints. As in the
S=(s)
case, this can be rewritten as a bulk-edge decomposition
\log Z_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}-\log Z_{\rm edge}
. For example, using (
285
) and the notation explained above it, the analog of (
87
) for an
S=(s,1^{m})
field becomes

\displaystyle\log Z^{d+1}_{\rm PI}(s,1^{m})=\log Z^{d+1}_{\rm bulk}(s,1^{m})-D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,\log Z^{d-1}_{\rm PI}(1^{m})\,,

(92)

so here
Z_{\rm edge}
is the path integral of
D_{s-1}^{d+2}
massive
m
-form fields
living on the
S^{d-1}
edge. In particular this implies the recursion relation
\log Z^{d+1}_{\rm PI}(1^{p})=\log Z^{d+1}_{\rm bulk}(1^{p})-\log Z^{d-1}_{\rm PI}(1^{p-1})
.
Similarly for a spin
s=k+\frac{1}{2}
Dirac fermion, in the notation explained under table (
283
)

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}^{d+1}(s,\mbox{${\bf\frac{1}{2}}$})=\log Z^{d+1}_{\rm bulk}(s,\mbox{${\bf\frac{1}{2}}$})-\tfrac{1}{2}D_{s-1,{\bf\frac{1}{2}}}^{d+2}\,\log Z^{d-1}_{\rm PI}(\mbox{${\bf\frac{1}{2}}$})\,,

(93)

where
Z_{\rm bulk}
now takes the form of the
fermionic
part of (
67
), with
\chi_{\rm bulk}
as in (
210
) with
D^{d}_{S}=2\,D^{d}_{s,{\bf\frac{1}{2}}}
, the factor 2 due to the field being Dirac. The edge fields are Dirac spinors. Note that because
D^{d+2}_{-\frac{1}{2},{\bf\frac{1}{2}}}=0
, the above implies in particular
Z_{\rm PI}({\bf\frac{1}{2}})=Z_{\rm bulk}({\bf\frac{1}{2}})
.

We do not have a systematic group-theoretic or physical way of identifying the edge field content. For evaluation of
Z_{\rm PI}
using (
251
), this identification is not needed however. Actually the original expansions (
83
), (
91
) are more useful for this, as illustrated in (
255
)-(
C.2
).

5
Massless higher spins

5.1
Problems
