\chi_{\mu}
, and a scalar
\chi
, subject to the gauge transformations
Zinoviev:2001dt

\displaystyle\delta\chi=a_{-1}\,\xi\,,\qquad\delta\chi_{\mu}=a_{0}\,\xi_{\mu}+\sqrt{\tfrac{d-1}{2d}}\,\nabla_{\mu}\xi\,,\qquad\delta\phi_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}+\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{d(d-1)}}\,a_{0}\,\xi\,,

(344)

where
a_{0}\equiv m
and
a_{-1}\equiv\sqrt{m^{2}-(d-1)}
. Equivalently, recalling (
79
),
a_{n}=\sqrt{(\tfrac{d}{2}+n)^{2}+\nu^{2}}
. Gauge fixing by putting
\chi=0
,
\chi_{\mu}=0
, we get a ghost action

\displaystyle S_{\rm gh}=\int a_{-1}\,\bar{c}c+a_{0}\,\bar{c}^{\mu}c_{\mu}\,.

(345)

We can decompose
\phi_{\mu\nu}
into a TT part and a non-TT part orthogonal to it as
\phi_{\mu\nu}=\phi_{\mu\nu}^{\rm TT}+\phi^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}
, where
\phi^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}
can be decomposed into vector and scalar modes as
\phi^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{(\mu}\varphi_{\nu)}+g_{\mu\nu}\varphi
. Analogous to the
s=1
example, we should expect that integrating out
\phi^{\prime}
cancels against integrating out the ghosts, up to unmatched modes of the latter. The unmatched modes correspond to mixed vector-scalar modes solving
\nabla_{(\mu}\varphi_{\nu)}+g_{\mu\nu}\varphi=0
. This is equivalent to the conformal Killing equation. Hence the unmatched modes are the conformal Killing modes. As discussed below (
328
), the conformal Killing modes split according to
{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt\to\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt+\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt}
into
D_{1,1}
vector

-modes and
D_{1,0}
scalar

-modes. Integrating out the

-modes of the vector ghost
c_{\mu}
then yields an unmatched factor
(a_{0}/M)^{D_{1,1}}
, while integrating out the

-modes of the scalar ghost
c
yields an unmatched factor
(a_{-1}/M)^{D_{1}}
.
All in all, we get

\displaystyle Z_{\text{non-TT}}=(a_{-1}/M)^{D_{1,0}}(a_{0}/M)^{D_{1,1}}=\Bigl(M^{-1}\sqrt{(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)^{2}+\nu^{2}}\Bigr)^{D_{1,0}}\Bigl(M^{-1}\sqrt{(\tfrac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2}}\Bigr)^{D_{1,1}}

(346)

in agreement with (
337
) for
s=2
.

Spin
s
:

The pattern is now clear: according to
Zinoviev:2001dt
, the Stueckelberg system for a massive spin-
s
field consists of an unconstrained symmetric
s
-index tensor
\phi^{(s)}
and of a tower of unconstrained symmetric
s^{\prime}
-index auxiliary Stueckelberg fields
\chi^{(s^{\prime})}
with
s^{\prime}=0,1,\ldots,s-1
, with gauge symmetries of the form

\displaystyle\delta\chi^{(s^{\prime})}=a_{s^{\prime}-1}\,\xi^{(s^{\prime})}+\cdots\,,\qquad\delta\phi^{(s)}=\cdots\,,\qquad a_{n}\equiv\sqrt{(\tfrac{d}{2}+n)^{2}+\nu^{2}}\,,

(347)

where the dots indicate terms we won’t technically need — which is to say, as transpired from
s=1,2
already, we need very little indeed. The ghost action is
S=\sum_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s-1}a_{s^{\prime}-1}\bar{c}^{(s^{\prime})}c^{(s^{\prime})}
. The unmatched modes correspond to the conformal Killing tensors modes on
S^{d+1}
, decomposed for say
s=4
as in (
335
) into
D_{3,3}

-modes,
D_{3,2}

-modes,
D_{3,1}

-modes, and
D_{3,0}

-modes. The corresponding unmatched modes of respectively
c^{(3)}
,
c^{(2)}
,
c^{(1)}
and
c^{(0)}
then integrate to unmatched factors
(a_{2}/M)^{D_{3,3}}(a_{1}/M)^{D_{3,2}}(a_{0}/M)^{D_{3,1}}(a_{-1}/M)^{D_{3,0}}
. For general
s
:

\displaystyle Z_{\text{non-TT}}=\prod_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s-1}\bigl(a_{s^{\prime}-1}/M\bigr)^{D_{s-1,s^{\prime}}}=\prod_{n=-1}^{s-2}\biggl(M^{-1}\sqrt{(\tfrac{d}{2}+n)^{2}+\nu^{2}}\,\biggr)^{D_{s-1,n+1}^{d+2}}\,,

(348)

in agreement with (
337
) for general
s
. This establishes our claims.

The above computation was somewhat schematic of course, and one could perhaps still worry about missed purely numerical factors independent of
\nu
, perhaps leading to an additional finite constant term being added to our final formulae (
84
) -(
83
) for
\log Z_{\rm PI}
. However at fixed UV-regulator scale, the limit
\nu\to\infty
of these final expressions manifestly approaches zero, as should be the case for particles much heavier than the UV cutoff scale. This would not be true if there was an additional constant term. Finally, we carefully checked the analogous result in the massless case (which has a more compact off-shell formulation
Fronsdal:1978rb
), discussed in section
5
, by direct path integral computations in complete gory detail
Law:2020cpj
, for all
s
.

Also, the result is pretty.

F.2
General massive representations

Here we give a generalization of (
83
) for arbitrary massive representations of the dS
d+1
isometry group
SO(1,d+1)
.

Massive irreducible representations of
SO(1,d+1)
are labeled by a dimension
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\nu
and an
{\rm so}(d)
highest weight
S=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})

10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
. The massive spin-
s
case considered in (
83
) corresponds to
S=(s,0,\ldots,0)
, a totally symmetric tensor field. More general irreps correspond to more general mixed-symmetry fields. The analog of (
321
) in this generalized setup is

\displaystyle\log Z^{\text{``TT''}}_{\rm PI}=\pm\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,\sum_{n\geq s_{1}}\,D_{n,S}^{d+2}\,e^{-\tau((n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2})}\,,

(349)

where for bosons the sum runs over integer
n
with an overall
+
sign and for fermions the sum runs over half-integer
n
with an overall
-
sign. The dimensions of the
{\rm so}(d+2)
irreps
(n,S)
are given explicitly as polynomials in
(n,s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})
by the Weyl dimension formulae (
281
)-(
282
). From this it can be seen that
D^{d+2}_{n,S}
is (anti-)symmetric under reflections about
n=-\frac{d}{2}
, more precisely

\displaystyle D^{d+2}_{n,S}=(-1)^{d}D^{d+2}_{-d-n,S}.

(350)

Moreover the exponent in (
349
) is symmetric under the same reflection. The most natural extension of the sum is therefore to
all
(half-)integer
n
, taking into account the sign in (
350
) for odd
d
, and adding an overall factor
\frac{1}{2}
to correct for double counting, suggesting

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,\sum_{n}\,\sigma_{d}(\tfrac{d}{2}+n)\,D_{n,S}^{d+2}\,e^{-\tau((n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2})}\,,

(351)

where
\sigma_{d}(x)\equiv 1
for even
d
and
\sigma_{d}(x)\equiv\mbox{sign}(x)
for odd
d
. Equivalently, in view of (
350
)

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm PI}=\pm\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,\sum_{n}\,\Theta(\tfrac{d}{2}+n)\,D_{n,S}^{d+2}\,e^{-\tau((n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}+\nu^{2})}}

(352)

where
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for bosons and
n\in\frac{1}{2}+{\mathbb{Z}}
for fermions, and

\Theta(x)=1
for
x>0
,
\Theta(0)={\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}}
,
\Theta(x)=0
for
x<0
.

(353)

At first sight this seems to be different from the extension to
n\geq-1
in (
83
) for the spin-
s
case
S=(s,0,\ldots,0)
. However it is actually the same, as (
281
)-(
282
) imply that

D_{n,S}
vanishes for
2-d\leq n\leq-2
when
S=(s,0,\ldots,0)
.

The obvious conjecture is then that (
352
) is true for general massive representations. Here are some consistency checks, which are satisfied precisely for the sum range in (
352
):

\bullet

Locality:
For even
d
, the summand in (
351
) is analytic in
n
. Applying the Euler-Maclaurin formula to extract the
\tau\to 0
asymptotic expansion of the sum gives in this case

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\,D_{n,S}^{d+2}\,e^{-\tau(n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}}\sim\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dn\,D_{n,S}^{d+2}\,e^{-\tau(n+\frac{d}{2})^{2}}\,.

(354)

The symmetry (
350
) tells us that the integrand on the right hand side is even in
x\equiv n+\frac{d}{2}
. Since
\int dx\,x^{2k}\,e^{-\tau x^{2}}\propto\tau^{-k-1/2}
, this implies the absence of
1/\tau
terms in the
\tau\to 0
expansion of the integrand in (
351
), and therefore, in contrast to (
349
), the absence of nonlocal log-divergences, as required by locality of
Z_{\rm PI}
in odd spacetime dimension
d+1
.

\bullet

Bulk
-
edge structure:
By following the usual steps, we can rewrite (
352
) as

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm PI}=\int\frac{dt}{2t}\,F(e^{-t})\,,\qquad\,F(q)=\pm\bigl(q^{i\nu}+q^{-i\nu}\bigr)\sum_{n}\Theta\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2}+n\bigr)\,D^{d+2}_{n,S}\,q^{\frac{d}{2}+n}}

(355)

Using (
281
)-(
282
), this can be seen to sum up to the form
\log Z_{\rm PI}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}-\log Z_{\rm edge}
, where
Z_{\rm bulk}
is the physically expected bulk character formula for an ideal gas in the dS
d+1
static patch consisting of massive particles in the
(\Delta,S)
UIR of
SO(1,d+1)
, and
Z_{\rm edge}
can be interpreted as a Euclidean path integral of local fields living on the
S^{d-1}
edge/horizon.

\bullet

Unitarity:
Note that for
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+\mu
with
\mu\equiv i\nu
real, we get a bound on
\mu
from requiring
t\to\infty
(IR) convergence of the integral (
355
), generalizing (
339
), namely

\displaystyle|\mu|<\frac{d}{2}+n_{*}(S)\,,

(356)

where
n_{*}(S)
is the lowest value of
n
in the sum for which
D_{n,S}^{d+2}
is nonvanishing. This coincides again with the unitarity bound on
\mu
for massive representations of
SO(1,d+1)

10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
;
Basile:2016aen
. Recalling the discussion below (
339
), this can be viewed as a generalization of the Higuchi bound to arbitrary representations.

Combining (
352
) with (
251
), we thus arrive at an exact closed-form solution for the Euclidean path integral on the sphere for arbitrary massive field content.

Appendix G
Derivations for massless higher spins

In this appendix we derive (
112
) and provide details of various other points summarized in section
5
.

G.1
Bulk partition function:
Z_{\rm bulk}

The bulk partition function
Z_{\rm bulk}
as defined in (
67
) for a massless spin-
s
field is given by

\displaystyle Z_{\rm bulk}=\int\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(q)\,,

(357)

where
q=e^{-t}
, and
\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(q)={\rm tr}\,q^{iH}
in the case at hand is the (restricted)
q
-character of the massless spin-
s

SO(1,d+1)
UIR. For generic
d
, this UIR is part of the exceptional series
Basile:2016aen
. More precisely in the notation of
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
it is the
D^{j}_{S;p}
representation, with
p=0
,
j=(d-4)/2
for even
d
,
j=(d-3)/2
for odd
d
, and
S=(s,s,0,\ldots,0)
. In the notation of
Basile:2016aen
this is the exceptional series with
\Delta=p=2
,
S=(s,s,0,\ldots,0)
.
36
36

36

For
d=3
, it is in the discrete series, but (
358
) still applies.
The characters
\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(q)
for these irreps are quite a bit more intricate than their massive counterparts. The full
SO(1,d+1)
characters
\tilde{\chi}(g)
were obtained in
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
. Restricting to
g=e^{-itH}
gives
\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(t)
,
37
37

37

Actually we obtained the formula from (
388
), then Mathematica checked agreement with
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
.

\displaystyle(1-q)^{d}\,\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(q)=

\displaystyle\bigl(1-(-1)^{d}\bigr)\bigl(D_{s}^{d}\,q^{s+d-2}-D_{s-1}^{d}\,q^{s+d-1}\bigr)

(358)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=0}^{r-2}(-1)^{m}D^{d}_{s,s,1^{m}}\bigl(q^{2+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-2-m}\bigr)\,,\qquad r\equiv\mbox{rank ${\rm so}(d)$}=\lfloor\tfrac{d}{2}\rfloor\,,

Here we used the notation of
Basile:2016aen
: the
{\rm so}(d)
irrep
(s,s,1^{m})\equiv(s,s,1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
with
1
repeated
m
times.
The degeneracies
D^{d}_{s,s,1^{m}}
can be read off from (
281
)-(
282
). Some explicit low-dimensional examples are

\begin{array}[]{ll|l}d&r&(1-q)^{d}\,\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}(q)\\
\hline\cr 3&1&2D_{s}^{3}\,q^{s+1}-2D_{s-1}^{3}\,q^{s+2}\\
4&2&D_{s,s}^{4}\,2\,q^{2}\\
5&2&D_{s,s}^{5}\left(q^{2}-q^{3}\right)+2D_{s}^{5}q^{s+3}-2D_{s-1}^{5}q^{s+4}\\
6&3&D_{s,s}^{6}\left(q^{2}+q^{4}\right)-D_{s,s,1}^{6}\,2\,q^{3}\\
7&3&D_{s,s}^{7}\left(q^{2}-q^{5}\right)-D_{s,s,1}^{7}\left(q^{3}-q^{4}\right)+2D_{s}^{7}\,q^{s+5}-2D_{s-1}^{7}\,q^{s+6}\,,\\
\end{array}

(359)

where
D_{s}^{3}=2s+1
,
D_{s,s}^{4}=2s+1
,
D_{s}^{5}=\frac{1}{6}(s+1)(s+2)(2s+3)
,
D_{s,s}^{5}=\frac{1}{3}(2s+1)(s+1)(2s+3)
,
D_{s,s}^{6}=\frac{1}{12}(s+1)^{2}(s+2)^{2}(2s+3)
,
D_{s,s,1}^{6}=\frac{1}{12}s(s+1)(s+2)(s+3)(2s+3)
, etc.
For
s=1
, the character can be expressed more succinctly as

\displaystyle\chi_{{\rm bulk},1}(q)=d\cdot\frac{q^{d-1}+q}{(1-q)^{d}}-\frac{q^{d}+1}{(1-q)^{d}}+1\,.

(360)

With the exception of the
d=3
case, the above
{\rm so}(1,d+1)

q
-characters encoding the
H
-spectrum of massless spin-
s
fields in dS
d+1
are very different from the
{\rm so}(2,d)
characters encoding the energy spectrum of massless spin-
s
fields in AdS
d+1
with standard boundary conditions, the latter being
\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}^{{\rm AdS}_{d+1}}=(D_{s}^{d}\,q^{s+d-2}-D_{s-1}^{d}\,q^{s+d-1})/(1-q)^{d}
. In particular for
d\geq 4
, the lowest power
q^{\Delta}
appearing in the
q
-expansion of the character is
\Delta=2
, and is associated with the
{\rm so}(d)
representation
S=(s,s)
, i.e.
{\tiny\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt,\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt,\hbox{}\hskip 0.0pt\vbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.3pt\hbox{\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt\hbox to5.4381pt{\hfil}\vrule height=4.35048pt,width=0.3pt,depth=1.0876pt}\hrule height=0.3pt}\vskip-0.3pt}}\hskip 0.0pt,\ldots}
for
s=1,2,3,\ldots
, whereas for the
{\rm so}(2,d)
character this is
\Delta=s+d-2
and
S=(s)
. An explanation for this was given in
Basile:2016aen
: in dS,
S
should be thought of as associated with the higher-spin Weyl curvature tensor of the gauge field rather than the gauge field itself.

This fits well with the interpretation of the expansion

\displaystyle\chi(q)=\sum_{r}N_{r}q^{r}\,,

(361)

as counting the number
N_{r}
of
physical
static patch quasinormal modes decaying as
e^{-rT}
(cf. section
2
and appendix
B.3
).
Indeed for
d\geq 4
, the longest-lived physical quasinormal modes of a massless spin-
s
field in the static patch of dS
d+1
always decay as
e^{-2T}

Sun:2020sgn
, which can be understood as follows. Physical quasinormal modes of the southern static patch can be thought of as sourced by insertions of gauge-invariant
38
38

38

More precisely, invariant under linearized gauge transformations acting on the conformal boundary.
local operators on the past conformal boundary
T=-\infty
of the static patch, or equivalently at the south pole of the past conformal boundary (or alternatively the north pole of future boundary) of global dS
d+1

Sun:2020sgn
;
Ng:2012xp
;
Jafferis:2013qia
. By construction, the dimension
r
of the operator maps to the decay rate
r
of the quasinormal mode
\propto e^{-rT}
. For
s=1
, the gauge-invariant operator with the smallest dimension
r=\Delta
is the magnetic field strength
F_{ij}=\partial_{i}A_{j}-\partial_{j}A_{i}
of the boundary gauge field
A_{i}
, which has
\Delta=\dim\partial+\dim A=1+1=2
.
For
s=2
in
d\geq 4
, the gauge-invariant operator with smallest dimension is the Weyl tensor of the boundary metric:
\Delta=2+0=2
. Similarly for higher-spin fields we get the spin-
s
Weyl tensor, with
\Delta=s+2-s=2
. The reason
d=3
is special is that the Weyl tensor vanishes identically in this case. To get a nonvanishing gauge-invariant tensor, one has to act with at least
2s-1
derivatives (spin-
s
Cotton tensor), yielding
\Delta=(2s-1)+(2-s)=s+1
. An extensive analysis is given in
Sun:2020sgn
.

Note on a literature disagreements:
The characters (
358
) agree with the characters listed in the original work
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
, computed by undisclosed methods. They do not agree with those listed in the more recent work
Basile:2016aen
, computed by Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand resolutions. Indeed
Basile:2016aen
emphasized they disagreed with
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
for even
d
. More precisely, in their eq. (2.14) applied to
p=2
,
Y_{p}=(s,s,0,\ldots,0)
,
\vec{x}=0
, they find a factor
2=(1+(-1)^{d})
instead of the factor
(1-(-1)^{d})=0
in (
358
). It is stated in
Basile:2016aen
that on the other hand their results do agree with
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
for
odd

d
. Actually we find this is not quite true either, as in that case eq. (2.13) in
Basile:2016aen
applied to
p=2
,
Y_{p}=(s,s,0,\ldots,0)
,
\vec{x}=0
) has a factor
1
instead of the factor
(1-(-1)^{d})=2
in (
358
). Our Euclidean path integral result (
388
) coupled with the physics of section
2
strongly suggests the original results in
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
and (
358
) are the correct versions. Further support is provided in
Sun:2020sgn
by direct construction of higher-spin quasinormal modes.

G.2
Euclidean path integral:
Z_{\rm PI}=Z_{G}Z_{\rm char}

The Euclidean path integral of a collection of gauge fields
\phi
on
S^{d+1}
is formally given by

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}=\frac{\int{\cal D}\phi\,e^{-S[\phi]}}{{\rm vol}({\cal G})}

(362)

where
{\cal G}
is the local gauge group generated by the local field
\xi
appearing in (
94
). This ill-defined formal expression is turned into something well-defined by BRST gauge fixing. A convenient gauge for higher-spin fields is the de Donder gauge. At the Gaussian level, the resulting analog of (
321
) is
39
39

39

A detailed discussion of normalization conventions left implicit here is given above and below (
396
).

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm TT}\equiv\sum_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\Bigl({\rm Tr}_{\rm TT}\,e^{-\tau\left(-\nabla^{2}_{s,\rm TT}+m_{{\phi,s}}^{2}\right)}-{\rm Tr}_{\rm TT}\,e^{-\tau\left(-\nabla^{2}_{s-1,\rm TT}+m_{\xi,s}^{2}\right)}\Bigr)\,,

(363)

where
\sum_{s}
sums over the spin-
s
gauge fields in the theory (possibly with multiplicities) and

m_{\phi,s}^{2}
and
m_{\xi,s}^{2}
are obtained from the relations below (
78
) using (
95
). The first term arises from the path integral over the TT modes of
\phi
, while the second arises from the TT part of the gauge fixing sector in de Donder gauge — a combination of integrating out the TT part of the spin-
(s-1)
ghost fields and the corresponding longitudinal degrees of freedom of the spin-
s
gauge fields. The above (
363
) is the difference of two expressions of the form (
321
). Naively applying the formula (
355
) or (
83
) for the corresponding full
Z_{\rm PI}
, we get

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}=\exp\sum_{s}\int\frac{dt}{2t}\,\hat{F}_{s}(e^{-t})\,\qquad\mbox{(naive)}

(364)

where (assuming
d\geq 3
)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{s}(q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq-1}D^{d+2}_{n,s}\bigl(q^{s+d-2+n}+q^{2-s+n}\bigr)-\sum_{n\geq-1}D^{d+2}_{n,s-1}\bigl(q^{s+d-1+n}+q^{1-s+n}\bigr)

(365)

However this is clearly problematic. One problem is that for
s\geq 2
, the above
\hat{F}_{s}(q)
contains
negative
powers of
q=e^{-t}
, making (
364
) exponentially divergent at
t\to\infty
. The appearance of such “wrong-sign” powers of
q
is directly related to the appearance of “wrong-sign” Gaussian integrals in the path integral, as can be seen for instance from the relation between (
355
) and the heat kernel integral (
352
). In the path integral framework, one deals with this problem by analytic continuation, generalizing the familiar contour rotation prescription for negative modes in the gravitational Euclidean path integral
Hawking:1976ja
. Thus one defines
\int dx\,e^{-\lambda x^{2}/2}
for
\lambda<0
by rotating
x\to ix
, or equivalently by rotating
\tau\to-\tau
in the heat kernel integral. Essentially this just boils down to flipping any
\lambda<0
to
-\lambda>0
. Since the Laplacian eigenvalues are equal to the products of the exponents appearing in the pairs
\bigl(q^{\Delta+n}+q^{d-\Delta+n}\bigl)
in (
365
), the implementation of this prescription in our setup is to flip the negative powers
q^{k}
in
\hat{F}_{s}(q)=\sum_{k}c_{k}\,q^{k}
to positive powers
q^{-k}
, that is to say replace

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{s}(q)\to F_{s}(q)\equiv\bigl\{\hat{F}_{s}(q)\bigr\}_{+}=\Bigl\{\sum_{k}c_{k}\,q^{k}\Bigr\}_{+}\equiv\sum_{k<0}c_{k}\,q^{-k}+\sum_{k\geq 0}c_{k}\,q^{k}\,.

(366)

In addition, each negative mode path integral contour rotation produces a phase
\pm i
, resulting in a definite, finite overall phase in
Z_{\rm PI}

Polchinski:1988ua
. The analysis of
Polchinski:1988ua
translates to each corresponding flip in (
366
) contributing with the
same
sign,
40
40

40

This can be seen in a more careful path integral analysis
Law:2020cpj
.
hence to an overall phase
i^{-P_{s}}
with
P_{s}
the total degeneracy of negative modes in (
365
). Using
D^{d+2}_{n,s}=-D_{s-1,n+1}^{d+2}
:

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}\to i^{-P_{s}}Z_{\rm PI}\,,\qquad P_{s}=\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{s-2}D_{s-1,n^{\prime}}^{d+2}+\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{s-2}D_{s-2,n^{\prime}}^{d+2}=D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+3}-D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+2}+D^{d+3}_{s-2,s-2}

(367)

In particular this implies
P_{1}=0
and
P_{2}=d+3
in agreement with
Polchinski:1988ua
.

After having taken care of the
negative
powers of
q
, the resulting amended formula
Z_{\rm PI}=\int\frac{dt}{2t}\,F_{s}(q)
is still problematic, however, as
F_{s}(q)
still contains terms proportional to
q^{0}
, causing the integral to diverges (logarithmically) for
t\to\infty
. These correspond to zeromodes in the original path integral. Indeed such zermodes were to be expected: they are due to the existence of normalizable rank
s-1
traceless Killing tensors
\bar{\xi}^{(s-1)}
, which by definition satisfy
\nabla_{(\mu_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{s})}=0
, and therefore correspond to vanishing gauge transformations (
94
), leading in particular to ghost zeromodes. Zeromodes of this kind must be omitted from the Gaussian path integral. They are easily identified in (
365
) as the values of
n
for which a term proportional to
q^{0}
appears. Since we are assuming
d>2
, this is
n=s-2
in the first sum and
n=s-1
in the second. Thus we should refine (
366
) to

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{s}\to F_{s}-F_{s}^{0}\,,

(368)

where
F_{s}^{0}=D_{s-2,s}^{d+2}(q^{2s+d-4}+1)-D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+2}(q^{2s+d-2}+1)
. Noting that
D^{d+2}_{s-2,s}=-D^{d+2}_{s-1,s-1}
and
D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+2}
is the number
N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}
of rank
s-1
traceless Killing tensors on
S^{d+1}
, we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle F_{s}^{0}

\displaystyle=-N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}\bigl(q^{2s+d-4}+1+q^{2s+d-2}+1\bigr)\,,\qquad N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}=D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+2}\,,

(369)

making the relation to the existence of normalizable Killing tensors manifest.
For example
N^{\rm KT}_{0}=1
, corresponding to constant
U(1)
gauge transformations;
N^{\rm KT}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(d+2)(d+1)={\rm dim}\,SO(d+2)
, corresponding to the Killing vectors of the sphere; and
N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}\propto s^{2d-3}
for
s\to\infty
, corresponding to large-spin generalizations thereof.

We cannot just drop the zeromodes and move on, however. The original formal path integral expression (
362
) is local by construction, as both numerator and denominator are defined with a local measure on local fields. In principle BRST gauge fixing is designed to maintain manifest locality, but if we remove any finite subset of modes by hand, including in particular zeromodes, locality is lost. Indeed the
-F^{(0)}_{s}
subtraction results in nonlocal log-divergences in the character integral, i.e. divergences which cannot be canceled by local counterterms. From the point of view of (
362
), the loss of locality is due the fact that we are no longer dividing by the volume of the local gauge group
{\cal G}
, since we are effectively omitting the subgroup
G
generated by the Killing tensors. To restore locality, and to correctly implement the idea embodied in (
362
), we must divide by the volume of
G
by hand. This volume must be computed using the same local measure defining
{\rm vol}({\cal G})
, i.e. the invariant measure on
{\cal G}
normalized such that integrating the gauge fixing insertion in the path integral over the gauge orbits results in a factor 1. Hence the appropriate measure defining the volume of
G
in this context is inherited from the BRST path integral measure. As such we will denote it by
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
. A detailed general discussion of the importance of these specifications for consistency with locality and unitarity in the case of Maxwell theory can be found in
Donnelly:2013tia
.
Relating
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
to a “canonical”, theory-independent definition of the group volume
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
(such as for example
{\rm vol}(U(1))_{\rm c}\equiv 2\pi
) is not trivial, requiring considerable care in keeping track of various normalization factors and conventions. Moreover
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
depends on the nonlinear interaction structure of the theory, as this determines the Lie algebra of
G
. We postpone further analysis of
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
to section
G.4
.

Conclusion

To summarize, instead of the naive (
364
), we get the following formula for the 1-loop Euclidean path integral on
S^{d+1}
for a collection of massless spin-
s
gauge fields:

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}=i^{-P_{s}}\,\bigl({\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}\bigr)^{-1}\,\exp\sum_{s}\int\frac{dt}{2t}\bigl(F_{s}-F_{s}^{0}\bigr)\,,

(370)

where
F_{s}=\{\hat{F}_{s}\}_{+}
and
F_{s}^{0}
were defined in (
365
), (
366
) and (
369
);
G
is the subgroup of gauge transformations generated by the Killing tensors
\bar{\xi}^{(s-1)}
, i.e. the zeromodes of (
94
); and
i^{-P_{s}}
is the phase (
367
).
We can split up the integrals by introducing an IR regulator:

\displaystyle\boxed{Z_{\rm PI}=i^{-P_{s}}\,Z_{G}\,Z_{\rm char}\,,\qquad Z_{G}\equiv\frac{\exp\bigl(-\sum_{s}\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}F_{s}^{0}\bigr)}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}}\,,\qquad Z_{\rm char}\equiv\exp\sum_{s}\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\,F_{s}}

(371)

where the notation
\int^{\times}
means we IR regulate by introducing a factor
e^{-\mu t}
, take
\mu\to 0
, and subtract the
\log\mu
divergent term. For a function
f(t)
such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}f(t)=c
, this means

\displaystyle\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{t}\,f(t)\equiv\lim_{\mu\to 0}\Bigl(c\log\mu+\int\frac{dt}{t}\,f(t)\,e^{-\mu t}\Bigr)

(372)

For example for
f(t)=\frac{t}{t+1}
this gives
\int_{0}^{\times}\frac{dt}{t}\,\frac{t}{t+1}=\log\mu-\log(e^{\gamma}\mu)=-\gamma
, and for
f(t)=1
with the integral UV-regularized as in (
262
) we get
\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{t}=\log(2e^{-\gamma}/\epsilon)
.

In section
G.3
we recast
Z_{\rm char}
as a character integral formula. In section
G.4
we express
Z_{G}
in terms of the canonical group volume
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
and the coupling constant of the theory.

G.3
Character formula:
Z_{\rm char}=Z_{\rm bulk}/Z_{\rm edge}Z_{\rm KT}

In this section we derive a character formula for
Z_{\rm char}
in (
371
). If we start from the naive
\hat{F}_{s}
given by (
365
) and follow the same steps as those bringing (
83
) to the form (
84
), we get

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{s}=\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,{\hat{\chi}}_{s}\,,\qquad{\hat{\chi}}_{s}={\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm bulk},s}-{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm edge},s}\,,

(373)

where

\displaystyle{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm bulk},s}

\displaystyle=D_{s}^{d}\,\frac{q^{s+d-2}+q^{2-s}}{(1-q)^{d}}-D_{s-1}^{d}\,\frac{q^{s+d-1}+q^{1-s}}{(1-q)^{d}}\,

(374)

\displaystyle{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm edge},s}

\displaystyle=D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,\frac{q^{s+d-3}+q^{1-s}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}-D_{s-2}^{d+2}\,\frac{q^{s+d-2}+q^{-s}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}\,.

(375)

Note that these take the form of “field
-
ghost” characters obtained respectively by substituting the values of
\nu_{\phi}
and
\nu_{\xi}
given by (
95
) into the massive spin
s
and spin
s-1
characters (
85
). The naive bulk characters
\hat{\chi}_{\rm bulk,s}
thus obtained cannot possibly be the character of any UIR of
SO(1,d+1)
, as is obvious from the presence of negative powers of
q
. In particular it is certainly not equal to the physical exceptional series bulk character (
358
). Now let us consider the actual
F_{s}=\{\hat{F}_{s}\}_{+}
appearing in (
371
).
Then we find
41
41

41

To check (
376
) starting from (
366
), observe that
\bigl\{\frac{1+q}{1-q}\bigl(q^{k}+q^{-k}-2\bigr)\bigr\}_{+}=0
for any integer
k
, so
\bigl\{\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,q^{k}\bigr\}_{+}=\frac{1+q}{1-q}\bigl(-q^{-k}+2\bigr)
for
k<0
, while of course
\bigl\{\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,q^{k}\bigr\}_{+}=\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,q^{k}
for
k\geq 0
, . This accounts for the
k<0
and
k>0
terms in the expansion
\sum_{k}c_{k}\,q^{k}
of (
376
). The coefficient
2N_{s-1}^{\rm KT}
of the
q^{0}
term is most easily checked by comparing the
q^{0}
terms on the left and right hand sides of (
376
), taking into account that, by definition,
[\chi]_{+}
has no
q^{0}
term, and that the
q^{0}
terms of the left hand side are given by (
369
).

\displaystyle F_{s}=\Bigl\{\frac{1+q}{1-q}\,{\hat{\chi}}_{s}\Bigr\}_{+}=\frac{1+q}{1-q}\Bigl(\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{s}\bigr]_{+}-2\,N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}\Bigr)\,,

(376)

where
the “flipped” character
[\hat{\chi}]_{+}
is obtained from

\hat{\chi}=\sum_{k}c_{k}q^{k}
by flipping
c_{k}q^{k}\to-c_{k}q^{-k}
for
k<0
and dropping the
k=0
terms:

\displaystyle\bigl[\hat{\chi}\bigr]_{+}=\Bigl[\sum_{k}c_{k}\,q^{k}\Bigr]_{+}\equiv\sum_{k<0}(-c_{k})\,q^{-k}+\sum_{k>0}c_{k}\,q^{k}\,=\hat{\chi}-c_{0}-\sum_{k<0}c_{k}\bigl(q^{k}+q^{-k}\bigr)\,.

(377)

Thus this flipping prescription can be thought of as the character analog of contour rotations for “wrong-sign” Gaussians in the path integral.
Notice the slight differences in the map
\hat{\chi}\to[\hat{\chi}]_{+}
and the related but different map
\hat{F}\to\{\hat{F}\}_{+}
defined in (
366
).

Substituting (
376
) into (
371
), we conclude

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z_{\rm char}=\sum_{s}\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\Bigl(\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm bulk},s}\bigr]_{+}-\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm edge},s}\bigr]_{+}-2N_{s-1}^{\rm KT}\Bigr)}

(378)

Consistency with ideal gas bulk character formula

Consistency with the physical ideal gas picture used to derive
Z_{\rm bulk}
in section
G.1
requires the bulk part of
\log Z_{\rm char}
as given in (
378
) agrees with (
357
), that is to say it requires

\displaystyle\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}=[{\hat{\chi}}_{{\rm bulk},s}]_{+}\,\qquad(?)

(379)

where
\chi_{{\rm bulk},s}
is one of the intricate exceptional series characters (
358
), while
[\hat{\chi}_{{\rm bulk},s}]_{+}
is obtained from the simple naive bulk character (
374
) just by flipping some polar terms as in (
377
). At first sight this might seems rather unlikely. Nevertheless, quite remarkably, it turns out to be true. Let us first check this in some simple examples:

•

s=2
in
d=3
: The naive character (
374
) is

\displaystyle{\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}

\displaystyle=5\cdot\frac{q^{3}+1}{(1-q)^{3}}-3\cdot\frac{q^{4}+q^{-1}}{(1-q)^{3}}\,.

(380)

The polar and
q^{0}
terms are obtained by expanding
{\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}=-\frac{3}{q}-4+{\cal O}(q)
. Thus

\displaystyle\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}\bigr]_{+}={\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}+4+\frac{3}{q}+3\,q=\frac{2\cdot 5\cdot q^{3}-2\cdot 3\cdot q^{4}}{(1-q)^{3}}\,,

(381)

correctly reproducing the
d=3,s=2
character in (
359
).

•

s=1
, general
d\geq 3
: In this case the map
[...]_{+}
merely eliminates the
q^{0}
term in the naive character (
374
) by adding
+1
:

\displaystyle\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}\bigr]_{+}={\hat{\chi}}_{\rm bulk}+1=d\cdot\frac{q^{d-1}+q}{(1-q)^{d}}-\frac{q^{d}+1}{(1-q)^{d}}+1\,,

(382)

correctly reproducing (
360
).

Using Mathematica, it is straightforward to check an arbitrary large number of examples in this way.
Below we will derive a general explicit formula for the character flip map
\hat{\chi}\to[\hat{\chi}]_{+}
. This will provide a general proof of (
379
), and more generally will enable efficient closed-form computation of the proper bulk and edge characters for general
s
and
d
. Generalizations are implemented with equal ease: as an illustration thereof we compute the bulk and edge characters for partially massless fields.

Flipping formula

We wish to derive a general explicit formula for
\bigl[\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)^{d}}\bigr]_{+}
defined in (
377
), for

\displaystyle\Delta\in\{0,-1,-2,-3,\ldots\}

(383)

which suffices to obtain an explicit expression for
[\hat{\chi}]_{+}
for any bosonic character of interest, including in particular (
374
)-(
375
).
This is achieved by expanding
(1-q)^{-d}=\sum_{k}{d-1+k\choose k}q^{k}
, splitting the sum in its polar and nonpolar part, incorporating the appropriate sign flips, and resumming in terms of hypergeometric functions
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c;q)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}{(c)_{n}}\frac{q^{n}}{n!}
. After a bit more sanding and polishing we find

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)^{d}}\biggr]_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{d+1}q^{d-\Delta}+p_{\Delta}(q)+(-1)^{d}p_{d-\Delta}(q)}{(1-q)^{d}}\,,

(384)

where
p_{\Delta}(q)=\tbinom{d-\Delta}{d-1}\cdot q\cdot{}_{2}F_{1}\bigl(1-d,1-\Delta,2-\Delta,q\bigr)=\tbinom{d-\Delta}{d-1}\cdot q\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}(-1)^{k}\tbinom{d-1}{k}\tfrac{1-\Delta}{k+1-\Delta}\,q^{k}
.
The hypergeometric series terminates because
1-d\leq 0
. A more interesting version is

\displaystyle\biggl[\frac{q^{\Delta}}{(1-q)^{d}}\biggr]_{+}=\frac{{\cal P}_{\Delta}(q)}{(1-q)^{d}}\,

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{\Delta}(q)\equiv(-1)^{d+1}q^{d-\Delta}+\sum_{m=0}^{r-1}(-1)^{m}\,D^{d}_{1-\Delta,1^{m}}\bigl(q^{1+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-1-m}\bigr)\qquad r=\left\lfloor\tfrac{d}{2}\right\rfloor\,.

(385)

Here
D^{d}_{1-\Delta,1^{m}}
is the dimension of the irrep of
{\rm so}(d)
corresponding to the Young diagram
S=(1-\Delta,1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
, with
1
repeated
m
times, explicitly given in (
281
)-(
282
).
We obtained this formula using Mathematica and also obtained a proof of (
G.3
) by expressing (
281
)-(
282
) in terms of gamma functions and comparing to (
384
). This is somewhat tedious and not especially illuminating, so we omit it here.

Bulk characters for (partially) massless fields

Now let us apply this to a slight generalization of the massless
\hat{\chi}_{\rm bulk,s}
given in (
374
),

\displaystyle{\hat{\chi}}^{\rm bulk}_{s{s^{\prime}}}(q)\equiv D_{s}^{d}\,\frac{q^{1-{s^{\prime}}}+q^{d-1+{s^{\prime}}}}{(1-q)^{d}}-D_{s^{\prime}}^{d}\,\frac{q^{1-s}+q^{d-1+s}}{(1-q)^{d}}\,.

(386)

This is the naive bulk character for a
partially massless
spin-
s
field
\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}
with a spin-
s^{\prime}
(
0\leq{s^{\prime}}\leq s-1
) gauge parameter field
\xi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s^{\prime}}}

Bekaert:2013zya
. The massless case (
374
) corresponds to

\displaystyle{s^{\prime}}=s-1\,\qquad\mbox{(massless case)}.

(387)

We consider the more general partially massless case here to illustrate the versatility of (
G.3
), and because in a sense the resulting formulae are more elegant than in the massless case, due to the neat
s\leftrightarrow{s^{\prime}}
symmetry already evident in (
386
).
Applying (
G.3
), still with
r=\left\lfloor\tfrac{d}{2}\right\rfloor
,

\boxed{\begin{aligned} (1-q)^{d}\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}^{\rm bulk}_{s{s^{\prime}}}(q)\bigr]_{+}&=\bigl(1+(-1)^{d+1}\bigr)\bigl(D_{s}^{d}\,q^{d-1+{s^{\prime}}}-D_{s^{\prime}}^{d}\,q^{d-1+s}\bigr)\\
&\quad+\sum_{m=1}^{r-1}(-1)^{m-1}\,D^{d}_{s,{s^{\prime}}+1,1^{m-1}}\bigl(q^{1+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-1-m}\bigr)\,.\end{aligned}}

(388)

We used
D_{s}^{d}\,{\cal P}_{1-{s^{\prime}}}(q)-D_{s^{\prime}}^{d}{\cal P}_{1-s}(q)=\sum_{m=0}^{r-1}(-1)^{m}\,\bigl(D_{s}^{d}\,D^{d}_{{s^{\prime}},1^{m}}-D_{s^{\prime}}^{d}\,D^{d}_{s,1^{m}}\bigr)\bigl(q^{1+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-1-m}\bigr)\\
=\sum_{m=1}^{r-1}(-1)^{m-1}\,D^{d}_{s,{s^{\prime}}+1,1^{m-1}}\bigl(q^{1+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-1-m}\bigr)
with
{\cal P}_{\Delta}
as defined in (
G.3
) to get the second term. Like for (
G.3
), we obtained this formula using Mathematica.
It can be proven starting from (
281
)-(
282
).

Remarkably, (
388
) precisely reproduces the massless exceptional series characters (
358
) for
{s^{\prime}}=s-1
, further strengthening our physical picture, adding evidence for (
355
), proving (
379
), and generalizing it moreover to partially massless gauge fields. Comparing to
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
, the partially massless gauge field characters we find here coincide with those of the unitary exceptional series
D^{j}_{S,p}
with
p=0
,
S=(s,{s^{\prime}}+1)
, and
j=(d-3)/2
for odd
d
,
j=(d-4)/2
for even
d
. In the notation of
Basile:2016aen
this is the exceptional series with
\Delta=p=2
,
S=(s,{s^{\prime}}+1)
, which was indeed identified in
Basile:2016aen
as the
{\rm so}(1,d+1)
UIR for partially massless fields.

Edge characters for (partially) massless fields

For the edge correction we proceed analogously. The naive PM edge character is

\displaystyle{\hat{\chi}}_{s{s^{\prime}}}^{\rm edge}(q)=D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,\frac{q^{-{s^{\prime}}}+q^{d-2+{s^{\prime}}}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}-D_{{s^{\prime}}-1}^{d+2}\,\frac{q^{-s}+q^{d-2+s}}{(1-q)^{d-2}}\,,

(389)

reducing to the massless case (
375
) for
{s^{\prime}}=s-1
.
Applying (
G.3
) gives, still with
r=\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor
,

\boxed{\begin{aligned} (1-q)^{d-2}\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}^{\rm edge}_{s{s^{\prime}}}(q)\bigr]_{+}&=\bigl(1+(-1)^{d+1}\bigr)\bigl(D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,q^{d-2+{s^{\prime}}}-D_{{s^{\prime}}-1}^{d+2}\,q^{d-2+s}\bigr)\\
&\quad+\sum_{m=0}^{r-2}(-1)^{m}\,\tilde{D}_{m}\bigl(q^{1+m}+(-1)^{d}q^{d-3-m}\bigr)\end{aligned}}

(390)

where
\tilde{D}_{m}\equiv D_{s-1}^{d+2}\,D^{d-2}_{{s^{\prime}}+1,1^{m}}-D_{{s^{\prime}}-1}^{d+2}\,D^{d-2}_{s+1,1^{m}}
.

Note that in the massless spin-1 case

\displaystyle\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{1}^{\rm edge}(q)\bigr]_{+}=\frac{q^{d-2}+1}{(1-q)^{d-2}}-1\,.

(391)

In the notation of
10.3792/pja/1195522333
;
10.3792/pja/1195523378
;
10.3792/pja/1195523460
, this equals the character for the unitary
SO(1,d-1)
irrep in the exceptional series
D^{j}_{S;p=0}
with
S=(1)
and
j=(d-4)/2
for even
d
and
j=(d-3)/2
for odd
d
— the irreducible representation indeed of a massless scalar on dS
d-1
with its zeromode removed. The fact that
S=(1)
is analogous to what happens in the 2D CFT of a massless free scalar
X
: the actual CFT primary operators are the spin
\pm 1
derivatives
\partial_{\pm}X(0)
.

In contrast to (
388
), we did not find a way of rewriting
\tilde{D}_{m}
for general spin to suggest an interpretation along these lines in general. Indeed unlike (
388
),
\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}^{\rm edge}_{s{s^{\prime}}}(q)\bigr]_{+}
in general does not appear to be proportional to the character of a
single
exceptional series irrep of
SO(1,d-1)
. This is not in conflict with the picture of edge corrections as a Euclidean path integral of some collection of local fields on
S^{d-1}
, since if the fields have nontrivial spin /
{\rm so}(d-2)
weights, the corresponding character integrals will have a complicated structure, involving sums of iterations of
SO(1,d-1-2k)
characters with
k=0,1,2,\ldots
, exhibiting patterns that might be hard to discern without knowing what to look for. It should also be kept in mind we have not identified a reason the edge correction
must
have a local QFT path integral interpretation. On the other hand,
the coefficients of the
q
-expansion of the effective edge character
do
turn out to be positive, consistent with an interpretation in terms of some collection of fields corresponding to unitary representations of dS
d-1
. A more fundamental group-theoretic or physics understanding of the edge correction would evidently be desirable.

For practical purposes, the interpretation does not matter of course. The formula (
390
) gives a general formula for
\chi_{\rm edge}
, which is all we need. For example for
d=3
, this gives
\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{s}^{\rm edge}(q)\bigr]_{+}=2\,\frac{D_{s-1}^{5}\,q^{s}-D_{s-2}^{5}\,q^{s+1}}{1-q}=2D_{s-1}^{5}\,q^{s}+2D_{s-1}^{4}\frac{q^{s+1}}{1-q}
, where
D_{s-1}^{5}=\frac{1}{6}s(s+1)(2s+1)
and
D_{s-1}^{4}=D_{s-1}^{5}-D_{s-2}^{5}=s^{2}
. The second form makes positivity of coefficients manifest. For
d=4
we get
\bigl[{\hat{\chi}}_{s}^{\rm edge}(q)\bigr]_{+}=D^{5}_{s-1}\frac{2\,q}{(1-q)^{2}}
.

Conclusion

We conclude that (
378
) can be written as

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm char}=\log Z_{\rm bulk}-\log Z_{\rm edge}-\log Z_{\rm KT}\,,

(392)

where the bulk and edge contributions are explicitly given by (
388
)-(
390
) with
{s^{\prime}}=s-1
,
42
42

42

In the partially massless case
\log Z_{\rm KT}
takes the same form, but with
D^{d+2}_{s-1,s-1}
replaced by
D^{d+2}_{s-1,s^{\prime}}
.
and

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm KT}=\dim G\,\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\cdot 2\,,\qquad\dim G=\sum_{s}N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}=\sum_{s}D_{s-1,s-1}^{d+2}\,.

(393)

The finite (IR) part of
Z_{\rm KT}
is given by (
269
):
Z_{\rm KT}|_{\rm IR}=(2\pi)^{-\dim G}
.

G.4
Group volume factor:
Z_{G}

The remaining task is to compute the factor
Z_{G}
defined in (
371
), that is

\displaystyle Z_{G}

\displaystyle=\bigl({\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}\bigr)^{-1}\exp\sum_{s}N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\bigl(q^{2s+d-4}+q^{2s+d-2}+2\bigr)

(394)

We imagine the spin range to be finite, or cut off in some way. (The infinite spin range case is discussed in section
9
.) In the heat kernel regularization scheme of appendix
C
, we can then evaluate the integral using (
262
):

\displaystyle Z_{G}

\displaystyle=\bigl({\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}\bigr)^{-1}\prod_{s}\biggl(\frac{M^{4}}{(2s+d-4)(2s+d-2)}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}N_{s-1}^{\rm KT}}\,,\qquad M\equiv\frac{2e^{-\gamma}}{\epsilon}\,,

(395)

On general grounds, the nonlocal UV-divergent factors
M
appearing here in
Z_{G}
should cancel against factors of
M
in
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
, as we will explicitly confirm below.

Generalities

Recall that
G
is the group of gauge transformations generated by the Killing tensors. Equivalently it is the subgroup of gauge transformations leaving the background invariant.
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
is the volume of
G
with respect to the path integral induced measure. This is different from what we shall call the “canonical” volume
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
, defined with respect to the invariant metric normalized such that the generators of some standard basis of the Lie algebra have unit norm. (In the case of Yang-Mills, this coincides with the metric defined by the canonically normalized Yang-Mills action, providing some justification for the (ab)use of the word canonical.) In particular, in contrast to
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
,
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
depends on the coupling constants and UV cutoff of the field theory.

As mentioned at the end of section
G.2
, the computation of
Z_{G}
brings in a series of new complications. One reason is that the Lie algebra structure constants defining
G
are not determined by the free part of the action, but by its interactions, thus requiring data going beyond the usual one-loop Gaussian level. Another reason is that due to the omission of zeromodes and the ensuing loss of locality in the path integral, a precise computation of
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
requires keeping track of an unpleasantly large number of normalization factors, such as for instance constants multiplying kinetic operators, as these can no longer be automatically discarded by adjusting local counterterms. Consequently, exact, direct path integral computationz of
Z_{G}
for general higher-spin theories requires great care and considerable persistence, although it can be done
Law:2020cpj
. Below we obtain an exact expression for
Z_{G}
in terms of
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
and the Newton constant in a comparatively painless way, by combining results and ideas from
Joung:2013nma
;
Joung:2014qya
;
Sleight:2016xqq
;
Witten:1995gf
;
Giombi:2013yva
;
Donnelly:2013tia
;
Sleight:2016dba
, together with the observation that the form of (
363
) actually determines all the normalization factors we need. Although the expressions at intermediate stages are still a bit unpleasant, the end result takes a strikingly simple and universal form.

If
G
is finite-dimensional, as is the case for example for Yang-Mills, Einstein gravity and certain (topological) higher-spin theories
Boulanger:2011qt
;
Joung:2015jza
;
Manvelyan:2013oua
;
Brust:2016gjy
;
Blencowe:1988gj
;
Bergshoeff:1989ns
;
Castro:2011fm
;
Perlmutter:2012ds
;
Campoleoni:2010zq
including the dS
3
higher-spin theory analyzed in section
6
, we can then proceed to compute
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
, and we are done. If
G
is infinite-dimensional, as is the case in generic higher-spin theories, one faces the remaining problem of making sense of
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
itself. Glossing over the already nontrivial problem of exponentiating the higher-spin algebra to an actual group
Monnier:2014tfa
, the obvious issue is that
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
is going to be divergent.
We discuss and interpret this and other infinite spin range issues in section
9
. In what follows we will continue to assume the spin range is finite or cut off in some way as before, so
G
is finite-dimensional.

We begin by determining the path integral measure to be used to compute
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
in (
395
). Then we compute
Z_{G}
in terms of
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
and the coupling constant of the theory, first for Yang-Mills, then for Einstein gravity, and finally for general higher-spin theories.

Path integral measure

To determine
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
we have to take a quick look at the path integral measure. This is fixed by locality and consistency with the regularized heat kernel definition of Gaussian path integrals we have been using throughout. For example for a scalar field as in (
66
), we have
\int{\cal D}\phi\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\phi(-\nabla^{2}+m^{2})\phi}\equiv\exp\int\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}\,{\rm Tr}\,e^{-\tau(-\nabla^{2}+m^{2})}
. An eigenmode of
-\nabla^{2}+m^{2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
contributes a factor
M/\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
to the right hand side of this equation, with
M=\exp\int\frac{d\tau}{2\tau}\,e^{-\epsilon^{2}/4\tau}e^{-\tau}=2e^{-\gamma}/\epsilon
, the same parameter as in (
395
) (essentially by definition). To ensure the left hand side matches this, we must use a path integral measure derived from the local metric
ds^{2}_{\phi}=\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi}\int(\delta\phi)^{2}
. To see this, expand
\phi(x)=\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}\psi_{i}(x)
with
\psi_{i}(x)
an orthonormal basis of eigenmodes of
-\nabla^{2}+m^{2}
on
S^{d+1}
. The metric in this basis becomes
ds^{2}=\sum_{i}\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi}d\varphi_{i}^{2}
, so a mode with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
contributes a factor
\int d\varphi_{i}\,\frac{M}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{i}\varphi_{i}^{2}}=M/\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
to the left hand side, as required.

We work with canonically normalized fields. For a spin-
s
field
\phi
this means the quadratic part of the action evaluated on its transverse-traceless part
\phi^{\rm TT}
takes the form

\displaystyle S\bigl[\phi^{\rm TT}\bigr]=\frac{1}{2}\int\phi^{\rm TT}(-\nabla^{2}+\overline{m}^{2})\phi^{\rm TT}.

(396)

Consistency with (
321
) or (
363
) then requires the measure for
\phi
to be derived again from the metric
ds^{2}_{\phi}=\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi}\int(\delta\phi)^{2}
. If
\phi
has a gauge symmetry, the formal division by the volume of the gauge group
{\cal G}
is conveniently implemented by BRST gauge fixing. For example for a spin-1 field with gauge symmetry
\delta\phi_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi
, we can gauge fix in Lorenz gauge by adding
the BRST-exact action
S_{\rm BRST}=\int iB\,\nabla^{\mu}\phi_{\mu}-\bar{c}\nabla^{2}c
. This requires specifying a measure for the Lagrange multiplier field
B
and the ghosts
c,\bar{c}
. It is straightforward to check that a ghost measure derived from
ds^{2}_{\bar{c}c}=M^{2}\int\delta\bar{c}\,\delta c
(which translates to a mode measure
\prod_{i}\frac{1}{M^{2}}d\bar{c}_{i}\,dc_{i}
) combined with a
B
-measure derived from
ds^{2}_{B}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int(\delta B)^{2}
, reproduces precisely the second term in (
363
) upon integrating out
B
,
c
,
\bar{c}
and the longitudinal modes of
\phi
. It is likewise straightforward to check that BRST gauge fixing is then formally equivalent to dividing by the volume of the local gauge group
{\cal G}
with respect to the measure derived from the following metric on the algebra of local gauge transformations:

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\xi}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\int(\delta\xi)^{2}\,.

(397)

Note that all of these metrics take the same form, with the powers of
M
fixed by dimensional analysis. An important constraint in the above was that the second term in (
363
) is exactly reproduced, without some extra factor multiplying the Laplacian. This matters when we omit zeromodes. For this to be the case with the above measure prescriptions, it was important that the gauge transformation took the form
\delta\phi_{\mu}=\alpha_{1}\partial_{\mu}\xi
with
\alpha_{1}=1
as opposed to some different value of
\alpha_{1}
, as we a priori allowed in (
94
). For a general
\alpha_{1}
, we would have obtained an additional factor
\alpha_{1}
in the ghost action, and a corresponding factor
\alpha_{1}^{2}
in the kinetic term in the second term of (
363
). To avoid having to keep track of this, we picked
\alpha_{1}\equiv 1
. For Yang-Mills theories, everything remains the same, with internal index contractions understood, e.g.
S[\phi^{\rm TT}]=\frac{1}{2}\int\phi^{a{\rm TT}}(-\nabla^{2}+\overline{m}^{2})\phi^{a{\rm TT}}
,
ds^{2}_{\phi}=\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi}\int(\delta\phi^{a})^{2}
,
ds^{2}_{\xi}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\int(\delta\xi^{a})^{2}
.

For higher-spin fields, we gauge fix in the de Donder gauge. All metrics remain unchanged, except for the obvious additional spacetime index contractions. The second term of (
363
) is exactly reproduced upon integrating out the
{\rm TT}
sector of the BRST fields together with the corresponding longitudinal modes of
\phi
, provided we pick

\displaystyle\alpha_{s}=\sqrt{s}

(398)

in (
94
), with symmetrization conventions such that
\phi_{(\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s})}=\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}
. (Technically the origin of the factor
s
can be traced to the fact that if
\phi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}}=\nabla_{(\mu_{1}}\xi_{\mu_{2}\cdots\mu_{s})}
for a TT
\xi
, we have
\int\phi^{2}=s^{-1}\int\xi(-\nabla^{2}+c_{s})\xi
.) Equation (
398
) fixes the normalization of
\xi
, and (
397
) then determines unambiguously the measure to be used to compute
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}
in (
395
). We will see more concretely how this works in what follows, first spelling out the basic idea in detail in the familiar YM and GR examples, and then moving on to the general higher-spin gauge theory case considered in
Joung:2013nma
.

Yang-Mills

Consider a Yang-Mills theory with with a simple Lie algebra

\displaystyle[L^{a},L^{b}]=f^{abc}L^{c}\,,

(399)

with the
L^{a}
some standard basis of anti-hermitian matrices and
f^{abc}
real and totally antisymmetric. For example for
{\rm su}(2)
Yang-Mills,
L^{a}=-\frac{1}{2}i\sigma^{a}
and
[L^{a},L^{b}]=\epsilon^{abc}L^{c}
. Consistent with our general conventions, we take the gauge fields
\phi_{\mu}=\phi_{\mu}^{a}L^{a}
to be canonically normalized: the curvature takes the form
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}L^{a}=F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\phi_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\mu}+g[\phi_{\mu},\phi_{\nu}]
, and the action is

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{4}\int F^{a}\cdot F^{a}

(400)

The quadratic part of
S
is invariant under the linearized gauge transformations
\delta^{(0)}_{\xi}\phi_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi
, where
\xi=\xi^{a}L^{a}
, taking the form (
94
) with
\alpha_{1}=1
as required. The full
S
is invariant under local gauge transformations
\delta_{\xi}\phi_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi+g[\phi_{\mu},\xi]
, generating the local gauge algebra

\displaystyle[\delta_{\xi},\delta_{\xi^{\prime}}]=\delta_{g[\xi^{\prime},\xi]}\,.

(401)

The rank-0 Killing tensors
\bar{\xi}
satisfy
\partial_{\mu}\bar{\xi}=0
: they are the constant gauge transformations
\bar{\xi}=\bar{\xi}^{a}L^{a}
on the sphere, forming the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of local gauge transformations acting trivially on the background
\phi_{\mu}=0
, generating the group
G
whose volume we have to divide by. The bracket of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, denoted
[\![\cdot,\cdot]\!]
in
Joung:2013nma
, is inherited from the local gauge algebra (
401
):

\displaystyle[\![\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}^{\prime}]\!]=g[\bar{\xi}^{\prime},\bar{\xi}]\,.

(402)

Evidently this is isomorphic to the original YM Lie algebra. Being a simple Lie algebra,
{\mathfrak{g}}
has an up to normalization unique invariant bilinear form/metric. The path integral metric
ds^{2}_{\rm PI}
of (
397
) corresponds to such an invariant bilinear form with a specific normalization:

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\xi}|\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle_{\rm PI}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\int\bar{\xi}^{a}\bar{\xi}^{\prime a}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\,{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})\,\bar{\xi}^{a}\bar{\xi}^{\prime a}\,.

(403)

We define the theory-independent “canonical” invariant bilinear form
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\rm c}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
as follows. First pick a “standard” basis
M^{a}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, i.e. a basis satisfying the same commutation relations as (
399
):
[\![M^{a},M^{b}]\!]=f^{abc}M^{c}
. This fixes the normalization of the
M^{a}
. Then we fix the normalization of
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\rm c}
by requiring these standard generators have unit norm, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle M^{a}|M^{b}\rangle_{c}\equiv\delta^{ab}\,.

(404)

The explicit form of (
402
) implies such a basis is given by the constant functions
M^{a}=-L^{a}/g
on the sphere. Thus we have
\langle L^{a}|L^{b}\rangle_{\rm c}=g^{2}\delta^{ab}
and

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\xi}|\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle_{\rm c}=g^{2}\bar{\xi}^{a}\bar{\xi}^{\prime a}

(405)

Comparing (
405
) and (
403
), we see the path integral and canonical metrics on
G
and their corresponding volumes are related by

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\rm PI}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\frac{{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})}{g^{2}}\,ds^{2}_{c}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\frac{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}=\biggl(\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\frac{{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})}{g^{2}}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}\dim G}\,.

(406)

From (
395
), we get
Z_{G}={\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}^{-1}\bigl(\frac{M^{4}}{(d-2)d}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}\dim G}
, hence

\displaystyle Z_{G}=\frac{\gamma^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,,\qquad\gamma\equiv\frac{g}{\sqrt{(d-2)A_{d-1}}}\,,\qquad A_{d-1}\equiv{\rm vol}(S^{d-1})=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}\,,

(407)

where we used
{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})=\frac{2\pi}{d}{\rm vol}(S^{d-1})
. (Recall we have been assuming
d>2
. The case
d=2
is discussed in appendix
H.1
.) The quantity
\gamma
may look familiar: the Coulomb potential energy for two unit charges at a distance
r
in flat space is
V(r)=\gamma^{2}/r^{d-2}
.

Practically speaking, the upshot is that
Z_{G}
is given by (
407
), with
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
the volume of the Yang-Mills gauge group with respect to the metric defined by the Yang-Mills action (
400
). For example for
G=SU(2)
with
f^{abc}=\epsilon^{abc}
as before,
{\rm vol}(G)_{\rm c}=16\pi^{2}
, because
SU(2)
in this metric is the round
S^{3}
with circumference
4\pi
, hence radius
2
.

The relation (
402
) can be viewed as defining the coupling constant
g
given our normalization conventions for the kinetic terms and linearized gauge transformations. Of course the final result is independent of these conventions. Conventions without explicit factors of
g
in the curvature and gauge transformations are obtained by rescaling
\phi\to\phi/g
,
\xi\to\xi/g
. Then there won’t be a factor
g
in (
402
), but instead
g
is read off from the action
S=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\int(F^{a})^{2}
. We could also write this without explicit reference to a basis as
S=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\int{\rm Tr}\,F^{2}
, where the trace “
{\rm Tr}
” is normalized such that
{\rm Tr}(L^{a}L^{b})\equiv\delta^{ab}
. Then we can say the canonical bilinear/metric/volume is defined by the trace norm appearing in the YM action. We could choose a differently normalized trace
{\rm Tr}^{\prime}=\lambda^{2}{\rm Tr}
. The physics remains unchanged provided
g^{\prime}=\lambda g
. Then
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}^{\prime}=\lambda^{\dim G}{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
, hence, consistently,
Z_{G}^{\prime}=Z_{G}
.

As a final example, for
SU(N)
Yang-Mills with
{\rm su}(N)
viewed as anti-hermitian
N\times N
matrices,
S=-\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\int{\rm Tr}_{N}F^{2}
in conventions without a factor
g
in the gauge algebra, and
{\rm Tr}_{N}
the ordinary
N\times N
matrix trace,
{\rm vol}(SU(N))_{\rm c}=(\ref{volsuN})
.

Einstein gravity

The Einstein gravity case proceeds analogously. Now we have single massless spin-2 field
\phi_{\mu\nu}
. The gauge transformations are diffeomorphisms generated by vector fields
\xi_{\mu}
. The subgroup
G
of diffeomorphisms leaving the background
S^{d+1}
invariant is
SO(d+2)
, generated by Killing vectors
\bar{\xi}_{\mu}
. The usual standard basis
M_{IJ}=-M_{JI}
,
I=1,\ldots,d+2
of the
{\rm so}(d+2)
Lie algebra satisfies
[M_{12},M_{23}]=M_{13}
etc. We define the canonical bilinear
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{c}
to be the unique invariant form normalized such that the
M_{IJ}
have unit norm:

\displaystyle\langle M_{12}|M_{12}\rangle_{c}=1\,.

(408)

With respect to the corresponding metric
ds^{2}_{c}
, orbits
g(\varphi)=e^{\varphi M_{12}}
with
\varphi
ranging from
0
to
2\pi
have length
2\pi
. The canonical volume is then given by (
287
).

To identify the standard generators
M_{IJ}
more precisely in our normalization conventions for
\bar{\xi}
, we need to look at the field theory realization in more detail. The
{\rm so}(d+2)
algebra generated by the Killing vectors
\bar{\xi}
is realized in the interacting Einstein gravity theory as a subalgebra of the gauge (diffeomorphism) algebra. As in the Yang-Mills case (
402
), the bracket
[\![\cdot,\cdot]\!]
of this subalgebra is inherited from the gauge algebra.
Writing the Killing vectors as
\bar{\xi}=\bar{\xi}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
, the standard Lie bracket is

[\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}^{\prime}]_{\rm L}=\bigl(\bar{\xi}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\xi}^{\prime\nu}-\bar{\xi}^{\prime\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\xi}^{\nu}\bigr)\partial_{\nu}
.
If we had normalized
\phi_{\mu\nu}
as
\phi_{\mu\nu}\equiv g_{\mu\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}^{0}
with
g_{\mu\nu}^{0}
the background sphere metric, and if we had normalized
\xi_{\mu}
by putting
\alpha_{2}\equiv 1
in (
94
), the bracket
[\![\cdot,\cdot]\!]
would have coincided with the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\rm L}
. However, we are working in different normalization conventions, in which
\phi_{\mu\nu}
is canonically normalized and
\alpha_{2}=\sqrt{2}
according to (
398
). In these conventions we have instead

\displaystyle[\![\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}^{\prime}]\!]=\sqrt{16\pi G_{\rm N}}\,[\bar{\xi}^{\prime},\bar{\xi}]_{\rm L}\,,

(409)

where
G_{\rm N}
is the Newton constant. This can be checked by starting from the Einstein-Hilbert action, expanding to quadratic order (see e.g.
Goon:2018fyu
for convenient and reliable explicit expressions in dS
d+1
), and making the appropriate convention rescalings. This is the Einstein gravity analog of (
402
). To be more concrete, let us consider the ambient space description of the sphere
S^{d+1}
, i.e.
X^{I}X_{I}=1
with
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+2}
. Then the basis of Killing vectors
M_{IJ}\equiv-(X_{I}\partial_{J}-X_{J}\partial_{I})/\sqrt{16\pi G_{\rm N}}
satisfy our standard
{\rm so}(d+2)
commutation relations
[\![M_{12},M_{23}]\!]=M_{13}
etc, hence by (
408
),
\langle M_{12}|M_{12}\rangle_{c}=1
.
The path integral metric (
397
) on the other hand corresponds to the invariant bilinear
\langle\bar{\xi}|\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle_{\rm PI}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\,\int\bar{\xi}\cdot\bar{\xi}^{\prime}
, so
\langle M_{12}|M_{12}\rangle_{\rm PI}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{16\pi G_{\rm N}}\int_{S^{d+1}}(X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2})=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{16\pi G_{\rm N}}\frac{2}{d+2}{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})
. Thus we obtain the following relation between PI and canonical metrics and volumes for
G=SO(d+2)
:

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\rm PI}=\frac{A_{d-1}}{4G_{\rm N}}\frac{1}{d(d+2)}\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\,ds^{2}_{\rm c}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\frac{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}=\left(\frac{A_{d-1}}{4G_{\rm N}}\frac{1}{d(d+2)}\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\dim G}

(410)

where
\dim G=\frac{1}{2}(d+2)(d+1)
,
A_{d-1}={\rm vol}(S^{d-1})
as in (
407
), and we again used
{\rm vol}(S^{d+1})=\frac{2\pi}{d}{\rm vol}(S^{d-1})
. Combining this with (
395
), we get our desired result:

\displaystyle Z_{G}=\frac{\gamma^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,,\qquad\qquad\gamma\equiv\sqrt{\frac{8\pi G_{\rm N}}{A_{d-1}}}\,.

(411)

Higher-spin gravity

We follow the same template for the higher-spin case.
In the interacting higher-spin theory, the Killing tensors generate a subalgebra of the nonlinear gauge algebra, with bracket
[\![\cdot,\cdot]\!]
inherited from the gauge algebra, just like in the Yang-Mills and Einstein examples, except the gauge algebra is much more complicated in the higher-spin case. Fortunately it is not necessary to construct the exact gauge algebra to determine the Killing tensor algebra: it suffices to determine the lowest order deformation of the linearized gauge transformation (
94
) fixed by the transverse-traceless cubic couplings of the theory
Joung:2013nma
. The Killing tensor algebra includes in particular an
{\rm so}(d+2)
subalgebra, that is to say an algebra of the same general form (
409
) as in Einstein gravity, with some constant appearing on the right-hand side determined by the spin-2 cubic coupling in the TT action. We
define
the “Newton constant”
G_{\rm N}
of the higher-spin theory to be this constant, that is to say we read off
G_{\rm N}
from the
{\rm so}(d+2)
Killing vector subalgebra by writing it as

\displaystyle[\![\bar{\xi},\bar{\xi}^{\prime}]\!]=\sqrt{16\pi G_{\rm N}}\,[\bar{\xi}^{\prime},\bar{\xi}]_{\rm L}\,.

(412)

The standard Killing vector basis is then again given by
M_{IJ}\equiv-(X_{I}\partial_{J}-X_{J}\partial_{I})/\sqrt{16\pi G_{\rm N}}
, satisfying
[\![M_{12},M_{23}]\!]=M_{13}
etc.

It was argued in
Joung:2013nma
that for the most general set of consistent parity-preserving cubic interactions, assuming the algebra does not split as a direct sum of subalgebras, i.e. assuming the algebra is simple, there exists an up to normalization unique invariant bilinear form
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{c}
on the Killing tensor algebra. We fix its normalization again by requiring the standard
{\rm so}(d+2)
Killing vectors
M_{IJ}
have unit norm,

\displaystyle\langle M_{12}|M_{12}\rangle_{c}\equiv 1\,.

(413)

Expressed in terms of the bilinears
\langle\bar{\xi}_{s-1}|\bar{\xi}_{s-1}\rangle_{\rm PI}=\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\,\int\bar{\xi}_{s-1}\cdot\bar{\xi}_{(s-1)}
corresponding to (
397
), the invariant bilinear on the Killing tensor algebra takes the general form

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\xi}|\bar{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle_{c}=\sum_{s}B_{s}\,\langle\bar{\xi}_{s-1}|\bar{\xi}_{s-1}^{\prime}\rangle_{\rm PI}\,,

(414)

where
B_{s}
are certain constants fixed in principle by the algebra. The arguments given in
Joung:2013nma
moreover imply that up to overall normalization, the coefficients
B_{s}
are independent of the coupling constants in the theory. More specifically, adapted (with some work, as described below) to our setting and conventions, and correcting for what we believe is a typo in
Joung:2013nma
, the coefficients are
B_{s}\propto(2s+d-4)(2s+d-2)
. We confirmed this by comparison to
Sleight:2016xqq
, where the invariant bilinear form for minimal Vasiliev gravity in AdS
d+1
, dual to the free
O(N)
model, was spelled out in detail, building on
Joung:2013nma
;
Joung:2014qya
;
Sleight:2016dba
. Analytically continuing to positive cosmological constant, implementing their ambient space
X
-contractions by a Gaussian integral, and reducing this integral to the sphere by switching to spherical coordinates, the expression in
Sleight:2016xqq
can be brought to the form (
414
). This transformation almost completely cancels the factorials in the analogous coefficients
b_{s}
in
Sleight:2016xqq
, reducing to the simple
B_{s}\propto(2s+d-4)(2s+d-2)
. (The alternating signs of
Sleight:2016xqq
are absent here due to the analytic continuation to positive cc.) Taking into account our normalization prescription (
413
) (which is different from the normalization chosen in
Sleight:2016xqq
), we thus get

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\bar{\xi}|\bar{\xi}\bigr\rangle_{{\rm c}}=\frac{2\pi}{M^{4}}\cdot\frac{4G_{\rm N}}{A_{d-1}}\sum_{s}(2s+d-4)(2s+d-2)\,\bigl\langle\bar{\xi}^{(s-1)}\bigl|\bar{\xi}^{(s-1)}\bigr\rangle_{\rm PI}\,,

(415)

with
A_{d-1}={\rm vol}(S^{d-1})
as before.
In view of the independence of the coefficients
B_{s}
of the couplings within the class of theories considered in
Joung:2013nma
, i.e. all parity-invariant massless higher-spin gravity theories consistent to cubic order, this result is universal, valid for this entire class.

As before for Einstein gravity and Yang Mills, from (
415
) we get the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{\rm PI}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}=\prod_{s}\left(\frac{M^{4}}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{A_{d-1}}{4G_{\rm N}}\cdot\frac{1}{\bigl(2s+d-4\bigr)\bigl(2s+d-2\bigr)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}N^{\rm KT}_{s-1}}\,.

(416)

Combining this with (
395
) we see that, rather delightfully, all the unpleasant-looking factors cancel, leaving us with

\displaystyle\boxed{\quad Z_{G}=\frac{\gamma^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,\,,\qquad\gamma\equiv\sqrt{\frac{8\pi G_{\rm N}}{A_{d-1}}}\quad}

(417)

This takes exactly the same form as the Einstein gravity result (
411
) except
G
is now the higher-spin symmetry group rather than the
SO(d+2)
spin-
2
symmetry group.

The cancelation of the UV divergent factors
M
is as expected from consistency with locality. The cancelation of the
s
-dependent factors on the other hand seems surprising, in view of the different origin of the numerator (spectrum of quadratic action) and the denominator (invariant bilinear form on higher spin algebra of interactions). Apparently the former somehow knows about the latter. We do not see an obvious reason why this is the case, although the simplicity and universality of the result suggests we should, and that this entire section should be replaceable by a one-line argument. Perhaps it is obvious in a frame-like formalism.

Newton constant from central charge

Recall that the Newton constant
G_{\rm N}
appearing in (
417
) was defined by the
{\rm so}(d+2)
algebra (
412
) in our normalization conventions. An analogous definition can be given in dS
d+1
or AdS
d+1
where the algebra becomes
{\rm so}(1,d+1)
resp.
{\rm so}(2,d)
. Starting from this definition,
G_{\rm N}
can also be formally related to the Cardy central charge
C
of a putative
43
43

43

There is no assumption whatsoever this CFT actually exists. One just imagines it exists and uses the formal holographic dictionary to infer the two-point function of this imaginary CFT’s stress tensor. In dS, this “dual CFT” can be thought of as computing the Hartle-Hawking wave function of the universe
Maldacena:2002vr
.
boundary CFT for AdS or dS, defined as the coefficient of the CFT 2-point function of the putative energy-momentum tensor. With our definition of
G_{\rm N}
, the computation of
Kovtun:2008kw
remains unchanged, so we can just copy the result obtained there:

\displaystyle\boxed{\quad C=\frac{(\pm 1)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\,\Gamma(d+2)}{(d-1)\,\Gamma\bigl(\frac{d}{2}\bigr)^{2}}\cdot\frac{A_{d-1}}{8\pi G_{\rm N}}\quad}

(418)

where as before
A_{d-1}=2\pi^{d/2}\ell^{d-1}/\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})
, and
\pm 1
=
+1
for AdS and
-1
for dS. The central charge of
N
free real scalars equals
C=\frac{d}{2(d-1)}\,N
in the conventions used here.
Note that (
418
) reduces to the Brown-Henneaux formula
C=3\ell/2G_{\rm N}
for
d=2
. In
Anninos:2011ui
it was argued that the Hartle-Hawking wave function of minimal Vasiliev gravity in dS
4
is perturbatively computed by a
d=3
CFT of
N
free Grassmann scalars. This CFT has central charge
C=-\frac{3}{4}N
, hence according to (
418
),
G_{\rm N}=2^{5}/\pi N
and
\gamma=\sqrt{2G_{\rm N}}=8/\sqrt{\pi N}
.

The final result of this appendix, putting everything together, is stated in (
112
).

Appendix H
One-loop and exact results for 3D theories

H.1
Character formula for
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}

For
d=2
, i.e. dS
3
/
S^{3}
, some of the generic-
d
formulae in sections
4
and
5
become a bit degenerate, requiring separate discussion. One reason
d=2
is a bit more subtle is that the spin-
s
irreducible representation of
SO(2)
actually comes in two distinct chiral versions
\pm s
, as do the corresponding
SO(1,3)
irreducible representations
(\Delta,\pm s)
. Likewise the field modes of a spin
s
field in the path integral on
S^{3}
split into chiral irreps
(n,\pm s)
of
SO(4)
. The dimensions
D^{2}_{s}=D^{2}_{-s}=1
and
D^{4}_{n,s}=D^{4}_{n,-s}=(1+n-s)(1+n+s)
of the
SO(2)
and
SO(4)
irreps are correctly reproduced by the Weyl dimension formula (
281
), rather than (
209
). It should however be kept in mind that the single-particle Hilbert space of for instance a massive spin-
s\geq 1
Pauli-Fierz field on dS
3
carries
both
helicity versions
(\Delta,\pm s)
of the massive spin-
s
SO(1,3) irrep, hence the character
\chi
to be used in expressions for
Z_{\rm PI}
in this case is
\chi=\chi_{+s}+\chi_{-s}=2\chi_{+s}=2(q^{\Delta}+q^{2-\Delta})/(1-q)^{2}
. On the other hand for a real scalar field, we just have
\chi=\chi_{0}=(q^{\Delta}+q^{2-\Delta})/(1-q)^{2}
.

For massless higher-spin gauge fields of spin
s\geq 2
, a similar reasoning implies we should include an overall factor of 2 in (
374
)-(
375
). For an
s=1
Maxwell field on the other hand, we get a factor of 2 in the first term but not in the second term (since the gauge parameter/ghost field is a scalar). The proper massless spin-
s
bulk and edge characters are then obtained from these by the polar term flip (
377
) as usual. This results in

\displaystyle\chi_{\rm bulk,s}=0\quad(s\geq 2)\,,\qquad\chi^{(s=1)}_{\rm bulk}=\frac{2q}{(1-q)^{2}}\,,\qquad\chi_{\rm edge,s}=0\quad(\mbox{all }s)\,,

(419)

expressing the absence of propagating degrees of freedom (i.e. particles) for massless spin-
s\geq 2
fields on dS
3
.

This can also be derived more directly from the general path integral formula (
355
), taking into account the
\pm s
doubling. In particular for massless
s\geq 2
, (
365
) gets replaced by

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq-1}\Theta(1+n)\,2D^{4}_{n,s}\bigl(q^{s+n}+q^{2-s+n}\bigr)-\sum_{n\geq-1}\Theta(1+n)\,2D^{4}_{n,s-1}\bigl(q^{s+1+n}+q^{1-s+n}\bigr),

(420)

which matters for the
n=-1
term because
\Theta(0)\equiv\frac{1}{2}
.
For
s=1
, we get instead

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq-1}\Theta(1+n)\,2D^{4}_{n,1}\bigl(q^{1+n}+q^{1+n}\bigr)-\sum_{n\geq 0}D^{4}_{n,0}\bigl(q^{2+n}+q^{n}\bigr)\,.

(421)

For
s\geq 2
, the computation of
Z_{\rm char}
and
Z_{G}
remains essentially unchanged. For
s=1
there are some minor changes. The edge character in (
375
) acquires an extra
q^{0}
term in
d=2
because
q^{s+d-3}=q^{0}
, so the map
\hat{\chi}_{\rm edge}\to[\hat{\chi}_{\rm edge}]_{+}
gets an extra
-1
subtraction, as a result of which the factor
-2
in (
376
) becomes a
-3
. Relatedly we get an extra
q^{0}
term in
q^{2s+d-4}+q^{2s+d-2}+2=q^{2}+3
in (
394
), and we end up with
Z_{G}=\tilde{\gamma}^{\dim G}/{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
with
\tilde{\gamma}=g\ell/\sqrt{A_{1}}=g\sqrt{\ell}/\sqrt{2\pi}
instead of (
407
). Everything else remains the same.

Finally, the phase
i^{-P_{s}}
(
367
) is somewhat modified. For
s\geq 2
, from (
420
),

\displaystyle P_{s}=-\sum_{n=-1}^{s-3}\Theta(1+n)\,2D_{n,s}^{4}-\sum_{n=-1}^{s-2}\Theta(1+n)\,2D_{n,s-1}^{4}=\frac{1}{3}(2s-3)(2s-1)(2s+1)

(422)

Note that
P_{2}=5
, in agreement with
Polchinski:1988ua
.
P_{1}=0
as before, since there are no negative modes.

Conclusion

The final result for
Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI}=Z_{G}Z_{\rm char}
in dS
3
replacing (
112
)-(
113
) is:

•

For Einstein and HS gravity theories with
s\geq 2
,

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}=i^{-P}\,\frac{\gamma^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\cdot Z_{\rm char}\,,\qquad Z_{\rm char}=e^{-2\dim G\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}}=(2\pi)^{\dim G}e^{-\dim G\cdot c\,\ell\,\epsilon^{-1}}\,,

(423)

where as before
\gamma=\sqrt{8\pi G_{\rm N}/A_{1}}=\sqrt{4G_{\rm N}/\ell}
,
P=\sum_{s}P_{s}
, and
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
is the volume with respect to the metric for which the standard
\rm so(4)
generators
M_{IJ}
have norm 1. We used (
269
) to evaluate
Z_{\rm char}
. The coefficient
c
of the linearly divergent term is an order 1 constant depending on the regularization scheme. (For the heat kernel regularization of appendix
C
, following section
C.3
,
c=\frac{3\pi}{4}
. For a simple cutoff at
t=\epsilon
as in section
C.4
,
c=2
.)
The finite part is

\displaystyle\boxed{\quad Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI,fin}=i^{-P}\,\frac{(2\pi\gamma)^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,,\qquad\gamma=\sqrt{\frac{8\pi G_{\rm N}}{2\pi\ell}}\quad}

(424)

For example for Einstein gravity with
G=SO(4)
, we get

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI,fin}=i^{-5}\,\frac{(2\pi\gamma)^{6}}{(2\pi)^{4}}=-i\,4\pi^{2}\gamma^{6}\,.

(425)

•

For Yang-Mills theories with gauge group
G
and coupling constant
g
, we get

\displaystyle Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}=\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\cdot e^{\dim G\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}\left(\frac{2q}{(1-q)^{2}}-3\right)}\,,\qquad\tilde{\gamma}=\frac{g\sqrt{\ell}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,.

(426)

Using (
251
), (
269
), the finite part evaluates to

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI,fin}=\frac{(2\pi g\sqrt{\ell}\,Z_{1})^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,,\qquad Z_{1}=e^{-\frac{\zeta(3)}{4\pi^{2}}}\,.

(427)

As in (
112
),
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
is the volume of
G
with respect to the metric defined by the trace appearing in the Yang-Mills action.
As a check, for
G=U(1)
we have
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}=2\pi
, so
Z=g\,e^{-\zeta(3)/4\pi^{2}}\sqrt{\ell}
in agreement with
Klebanov:2011td
eq. (3.25).

•

We could also consider the Chern-Simons partition function on
S^{3}
,

\displaystyle Z_{k}=\int{\cal D}A\,e^{i\,k\,S_{\rm CS}[A]}\,,\qquad S_{\rm CS}[A]\equiv\frac{1}{4\pi}\int{\rm Tr}(A\wedge dA+\frac{2}{3}A\wedge A\wedge A)\,,

(428)

with
k>0
suitably quantized (
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for
G=SU(N)
with
{\rm Tr}
the trace in the
N
-dimensional representation). Because in this case the action is first order in the derivatives and not parity-invariant, it falls outside the class of theories we have focused on in this paper. It is not too hard though to generalize the analysis to this case. The main difference with Yang-Mills is that
\chi_{\rm bulk}=0=\chi_{\rm edge}
: like in the
s\geq 2
case, the
s=1
Chern-Simons theory has no particles.
The function
\hat{F}_{1}
is no longer given by the Maxwell version (
421
), but rather by (
420
), except without the factors of
2
, related to the fact that the CS action is first order in the derivatives. This immediately gives
F_{1}=\hat{F}_{1}=-2\,\frac{1+q}{1-q}
.
The computation of the volume factor is analogous to our earlier discussions. The result (in canonical framing
Witten:1989ti
) is

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{k}=\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}(G)_{\rm Tr}}\,e^{-2\dim G\int^{\times}\frac{dt}{2t}\frac{1+q}{1-q}}\,,\qquad Z^{(1)}_{k,\rm fin}=\frac{(2\pi\tilde{\gamma})^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}(G)_{\rm Tr}}\,,\qquad\tilde{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\,,

(429)

where
{\rm vol}(G)_{\rm Tr}
is the volume with respect to the metric defined by the trace appearing in the Chern-Simons action (
428
). This agrees with the standard results in the literature, nicely reviewed in section 4 of
Marino:2011nm
.

H.2
Chern-Simons formulation of Einstein gravity

3D Einstein gravity can be reformulated as a Chern-Simons theory
Witten:1988hc
;
Achucarro:1986uwr
. Although well-known, we briefly review some of the basic ingredients and conceptual points here to facilitate the discussion of the higher-spin generalization in section
H.3
. A more detailed review of certain aspects, including more explicit solutions, can be found in section 4 of
Cotler:2019nbi
. Explicit computations using the Chern-Simons formulation of
\Lambda>0
Euclidean quantum gravity with emphasis on topologies more sophisticated than the sphere can be found in
Carlip:1992wg
;
Guadagnini:1994ahx
;
Castro:2011xb
.

Lorentzian gravity

For the Lorentzian theory with positive cosmological constant, amplitudes are computed by path integrals
\int{\cal D}A\,e^{iS_{L}}
with real Lorentzian
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
Chern-Simons action
Witten:1989ip

\displaystyle S_{L}=(l+i\kappa)S_{\rm CS}[A_{+}]+(l-i\kappa)S_{\rm CS}[A_{-}]\,,\quad\qquad A_{+}^{*}=A_{-},

(430)

where
S_{\rm CS}
is as in (
428
) with

A_{\pm}
an
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
-valued connection and
{\rm Tr}={\rm Tr}_{2}
.
The vielbein
e
and spin connection
\omega
are the real and imaginary parts of the connection:

\displaystyle A_{\pm}=\omega\pm ie/\ell\,,\qquad ds^{2}=2\,{\rm Tr}_{2}\,e^{2}=\eta_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}\,.

(431)

For the last equality we decomposed
e=e^{i}L_{i}
in a basis
L_{i}
of
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, say

\displaystyle(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})\equiv(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{1},\tfrac{1}{2}i\sigma_{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{3})\,\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\eta_{ij}\equiv 2\,{\rm Tr}_{2}(L_{i}L_{j})={\rm diag}(1,-1,1)\,.

(432)

Note that
[L_{i},L_{j}]=-\epsilon_{ijk}L^{k}
with
L^{k}\equiv\eta^{kk^{\prime}}L_{k^{\prime}}
. When
l=0
, the action reduces to the firs-order form of the Einstein action with Newton constant
G_{\rm N}=\ell/4\kappa
and cosmological constant
\Lambda=1/\ell^{2}
. The equations of motion stipulate
A_{\pm}
must be flat connections:

\displaystyle dA_{\pm}+A_{\pm}\wedge A_{\pm}=0\,,

(433)

equivalent with the Einstein gravity torsion constraint (with
{\omega^{i}}_{j}\equiv\eta^{il}\epsilon_{ljk}\omega^{k}
) and the Einstein equations of motion
Witten:1988hc
.
Turning on
l
deforms the action by parity-odd terms of gravitational Chern-Simons type. This does not affect the equations of motion (
433
). We can take
l\geq 0
without loss of generality. The part of the action multiplied by
l
has a discrete ambiguity forcing
l
to be integrally quantized, like
k
in (
428
