). Summarizing,

\displaystyle 0\leq l\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\,,\qquad 0<\kappa=\frac{2\pi\ell}{8\pi G_{\rm N}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,

(434)

dS
3
vacuum solution

A flat connection corresponding to the de Sitter metric can be obtained as follows. (We will be brief because the analog for the sphere below will be simpler and make this more clear.) Define
Q(X)\equiv 2\,(X^{4}L_{4}+iX^{i}L_{i})
with
L_{4}\equiv\frac{1}{2}{\bf 1}
and note that
\det Q=X_{4}^{2}+\eta_{ij}X^{i}X^{j}=:\eta_{IJ}X^{I}X^{J}
, so
{\cal M}\equiv\{X|\det Q(X)=1\}
is the dS
3
hyperboloid, and
Q
is a map from
{\cal M}
into
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
. Its square root
h\equiv Q^{1/2}
is then a map from
{\cal M}
into
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\simeq SO(1,3)
, so
A_{+}\equiv h^{-1}dh
is a flat
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})
-valued connection on
{\cal M}
. Moreover on
{\cal M}
we have
Q^{*}=Q^{-1}
, so
h^{*}=h^{-1}
,
A_{-}=A_{+}^{*}=-(dh)h^{-1}
, and
ds^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\ell^{2}\,{\rm Tr}(A_{+}-A_{-})^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\ell^{2}\,{\rm Tr}\,(Q^{-1}dQ)^{2}=\ell^{2}\eta_{IJ}dX^{I}dX^{J}
, which is the de Sitter metric of radius
\ell
on
{\cal M}
.

Euclidean gravity

Like the Einstein-Hilbert action — or any other action for that matter — (
430
) may have complex saddle points, that is to say flat connections
A_{\pm}
which do not satisfy the reality constraint (
430
), or equivalently solutions for which some components of the vielbein and spin connection are not real. Of particular interest for our purposes is the solution corresponding to the round metric on
S^{3}
. This can be obtained from the dS
3
solution as usual by a Wick rotation of the time coordinate. Given our choice of
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
basis (
432
), this means
X^{2}\to-iX^{2}
. At the level of the vielbein
e=e^{i}L_{i}
such a Wick rotation is implemented as
e^{2}\to-ie^{2}
. Similarly, recalling
\omega_{ij}=\epsilon_{ijk}\omega^{k}
, the spin connection
\omega=\omega^{i}L_{i}
rotates as
\omega^{1}\to i\omega^{1}
,
\omega^{3}\to i\omega^{3}
. Equivalently,
A_{\pm}\to(\omega^{i}\pm e^{i}/\ell)S_{i}
where
S_{i}\equiv\frac{1}{2}i\sigma_{i}
. Notice the
S_{i}
are the generators of
{\rm su}(2)
, satisfying
[S_{i},S_{j}]=-\epsilon_{ijk}S_{k}
,
-2\,{\rm Tr}_{2}(S_{i}S_{j})=\delta_{ij}
and
S_{i}^{\dagger}=-S_{i}
. Thus the Lorentzian metric
\eta_{ij}
gets replaced by the Euclidean metric
\delta_{ij}
, the Lorentzian
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{C}})={\rm so}(1,3)
reality condition gets replaced by the Euclidean
{\rm su}(2)\oplus{\rm su}(2)={\rm so}(4)
reality condition,
and the Lorentzian path integral
\int{\cal D}A\,e^{iS_{L}}
becomes a Euclidean path integral
\int{\cal D}A\,e^{-S_{E}}
, where
S_{E}\equiv-iS_{L}
is the Euclidean action:

\displaystyle S_{E}=(\kappa-il)\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{+}]-(\kappa+il)\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{-}]\,,\qquad A_{\pm}^{\dagger}=-A_{\pm}\,.

(435)

This can be interpreted as the Chern-Simons formulation of Euclidean Einstein gravity with positive cosmological constant. The
{\rm su}(2)\oplus{\rm su}(2)
-valued connection
(A_{+},A_{-})
encodes the Euclidean vielbein, spin connection and metric as

\displaystyle A_{\pm}=\omega\pm e/\ell=(\omega^{i}\pm e^{i}/\ell)S_{i}\,,\qquad S_{i}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}i\sigma_{i}\,,\qquad ds^{2}=-2\,{\rm Tr}_{2}\,e^{2}=\delta_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}\,.

(436)

The Euclidean counterpart of the reality condition of the Lorentzian action is that
S_{E}
gets mapped to
S_{E}^{*}
under reversal of orientation. Reversal of orientation maps
S_{\rm CS}[A]\to-S_{\rm CS}[A]
, and in addition here it also exchanges the
\pm
parts of the decomposition
{\rm so}(4)={\rm su}(2)_{+}\oplus{\rm su}(2)_{-}
into self-dual and anti-self-dual parts, that is to say it exchanges
A_{+}\leftrightarrow A_{-}
. Thus orientation reversal maps
S_{E}\to-(\kappa-il)\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{-}]+(\kappa+il)\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{+}]=S_{E}^{*}
, as required.

Round sphere solutions

Parametrizing
S^{3}
by
g\in SU(2)\simeq S^{3}
, it is easy to write down a flat
{\rm su}(2)\oplus{\rm su}(2)
connection yielding the round metric of radius
\ell
:

\displaystyle(A_{+},A_{-})=(g^{-1}dg,0)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad e/\ell=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{-1}dg=\omega\,,\quad ds^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\ell^{2}\,{\rm Tr}(g^{-1}dg)^{2}\,.

(437)

The radius can be checked by observing that along an orbit
g(\varphi)=e^{\varphi S_{3}}
, we get
g^{-1}dg=d\varphi\,S_{3}
so
ds=\frac{1}{2}\ell d\varphi
and the orbit length is
\int_{0}^{4\pi}ds=2\pi\ell
. The on-shell action is
S_{E}=-\frac{\kappa-il}{12\pi}\int_{S^{3}}{\rm Tr}_{2}(g^{-1}dg)^{3}=-\frac{2(\kappa-il)}{3\pi\ell^{3}}\int e^{i}e^{j}e^{k}\,{\rm Tr}_{2}(S_{i}S_{j}S_{k})=-\frac{\kappa-il}{6\pi\ell^{3}}\int e^{i}e^{j}e^{k}\,\epsilon_{ijk}=-2\pi(\kappa-il)
, so

\displaystyle\exp(-S_{E})=\exp\bigl(2\pi\kappa+2\pi il\bigr)=\exp\Bigl(\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G_{\rm N}}\Bigr)\,,

(438)

where we used (
434
). This
reproduces the standard Gibbons-Hawking result
PhysRevD.15.2752
for dS
3
.

More generally we can consider flat connections of the form
(A_{+},A_{-})=(h_{+}^{-1}dh_{+},h_{-}^{-1}dh_{-})
with
h_{\pm}=g^{n_{\pm}}
, where
n_{\pm}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
if we take the gauge group to be
G=SU(2)\times SU(2)
.
These are all related to the trivial connection
(0,0)
by a
large
gauge transformation
g\in S^{3}\to(h_{+},h_{-})\in G
. All other flat connections on
S^{3}
are obtained from these by gauge transformations continuously connected to the identity, which are equivalent to diffeomorphisms and vielbein rotations continuously connected to the identity in the metric description
Witten:1988hc
.
Large gauge transformations on the other hand are in general
not
equivalent to large diffeomorphisms. Indeed,

\displaystyle e^{-S_{E}}=e^{2\pi n\kappa+2\pi i\tilde{n}l}=e^{2\pi n\kappa}\,,\qquad n\equiv n_{+}-n_{-},\quad\tilde{n}\equiv n_{+}+n_{-}\,,

(439)

so evidently different values of
n
are physically inequivalent. Conversely, for a fixed value of
n
but different values of
\tilde{n}
, we get the same metric, so these solutions are geometrically equivalent. In particular the
n=1
solutions all produce the same round metric (
437
). For
n=0
, the metric vanishes. For
n<0
, we get a vielbein with negative determinant. Only vielbeins with positive determinant reproduce the Einstein-Hilbert action with the correct sign, so from the point of view of gravity we should discard the
n<0
solutions. Finally the cases
n>1
correspond to a metric describing a chain of
n
spheres connected by throats of zero size, presumably more appropriately thought of as
n
disconnected spheres.

Euclidean path integral

The object of interest to us is the Euclidean path integral
Z=\int{\cal D}A\,e^{-S_{E}[A]}
, defined perturbatively around an
n=n_{+}-n_{-}=1
round sphere solution
(\bar{A}_{+},\bar{A}_{-})=(g^{-n_{+}}dg^{n_{+}},g^{-n_{-}}dg^{n_{-}})
, such as the
(1,0)
solution (
437
). Physically, this can be interpreted as the all-loop quantum-corrected Euclidean partition function of the dS
3
static patch. For simplicity we take
G=SU(2)\times SU(2)
, so
n_{\pm}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and we can formally factorize
Z
as an
SU(2)_{k_{+}}\times SU(2)_{k_{-}}
CS partition function where
k_{\pm}=l\pm i\kappa
, with
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
and
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
:

\displaystyle Z=\int_{(n_{+},n_{-})}\!\!\!{\cal D}A\,e^{ik_{+}S_{\rm CS}[A_{+}]+ik_{-}S_{\rm CS}[A_{-}]}=Z_{\rm CS}\bigl(SU(2)_{k_{+}}|\bar{A}_{n_{+}}\bigr)\,Z_{\rm CS}\bigl(SU(2)_{k_{-}}|\bar{A}_{n_{-}}\bigr)\,,

(440)

Here the complex-
k
CS partition function
Z_{\rm CS}(SU(2)_{k}|\bar{A}_{m})\equiv\int_{m}{\cal D}A\,e^{ikS_{\rm CS}[A]}
is defined perturbatively around the critical point
\bar{A}=g^{-m}dg^{m}
. It is possible, though quite nontrivial in general, to define Chern-Simons theories at complex level
k
on general 3-manifolds
M_{3}

Witten:2010cx
;
Gukov:2016njj
. Our goal is less ambitious, since we only require a perturbative expansion of
Z
around a given saddle, and moreover we restrict to
M_{3}=S^{3}
. In contrast to generic
M_{3}
, at least for integer
k
, the CS action on
S^{3}
has a unique critical point modulo gauge transformations, and its associated perturbative large-
k
expansion is not just asymptotic, but actually converges to a simple, explicitly known function: in canonical framing
Witten:1989ti
,

\displaystyle Z_{\rm CS}\bigl(SU(2)_{k}|\bar{A}\bigr)_{0}=\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{2+k}}\,\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2+k}\bigr)\,e^{i(2+k)S_{\rm CS}[\bar{A}]}\qquad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+})\,.

(441)

The dependence on the choice of critical point
\bar{A}=g^{-m}dg^{m}
actually drops out for integer
k
, as
S_{\rm CS}[\bar{A}]=-2\pi m\in 2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
. We have kept it in the above expression to because this is no longer the case for complex
k
. Analytic continuation to
k_{\pm}=l\pm i\kappa
with
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
and
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}

in (
440
) then gives:

\displaystyle Z_{0}

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{2+l+i\kappa}}\,\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2+l+i\kappa}\bigr)\Bigr|^{2}\,e^{2\pi n\kappa-2\pi i\,\tilde{n}(2+l)}=\Bigl|\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{2+l+i\kappa}}\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2+l+i\kappa}\bigr)\cdot e^{\pi\kappa}\Bigr|^{2}\,.

(442)

Framing dependence of phase and one-loop check

For a general choice of
S^{3}
framing with
SO(3)
spin connection
\hat{\omega}
, (
441
) gets replaced by
Witten:1989ti

\displaystyle Z_{\rm CS}\bigl(SU(2)_{k}|\bar{A}\bigr)=\exp\bigl(\tfrac{i}{24}c(k)I(\hat{\omega})\bigr)\,Z_{\rm CS}\bigl(SU(2)_{k}|\bar{A}\bigr)_{0}\,,\qquad c(k)=3\bigl(1-\tfrac{2}{2+k}\bigr)\,,

(443)

where
I(\hat{\omega})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int{\rm Tr}_{3}(\hat{\omega}\wedge d\hat{\omega}+\frac{2}{3}\hat{\omega}\wedge\hat{\omega}\wedge\hat{\omega})
is the gravitational Chern-Simons action. The action
I(\hat{\omega})
can be defined more precisely as explained under (2.22) of
Witten:2007kt
, by picking a 4-manifold
M
with boundary
\partial M=S^{3}
and putting

\displaystyle I(\hat{\omega})\equiv I_{M}\equiv\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{M}{\rm Tr}(R\wedge R)\,,

(444)

where
R
is the curvature form of
M
,
{R^{\mu}}_{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}{R^{\mu}}_{\nu\rho\sigma}dx^{\rho}\wedge dx^{\sigma}
. Taking
M
to be a flat 4-ball
B
, the curvature vanishes so
I_{B}=0
, corresponding to canonical framing. Viewing
B
as a 4-hemisphere with round metric has
{\rm Tr}(R\wedge R)=0
pointwise so again
I_{B}=0
. Gluing any other 4-manifold
M
with boundary
S^{3}
to
B
, we get a closed 4-manifold
X=M-B
, with
I_{M}-I_{B}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{X}{\rm Tr}(R\wedge R)=2\pi p_{1}(X)
, where
p_{1}(X)
is the Pontryagin number of
X
. According to the Hirzebruch signature theorem, the signature
\sigma(X)=b_{2}^{+}-b_{2}^{-}
of the intersection form of the middle cohomology of
X
equals
\frac{1}{3}p_{1}(X)
. Therefore, for any choice of
M
,

\displaystyle I_{M}=6\pi r\,,\qquad r=\sigma(X)\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(445)

For example
r=1
for
X={\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
and
r=p-q
for
X=p{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\#q\overline{{\mathbb{CP}}}^{2}
. Thus for general framing,
(
443
) becomes
Z_{\rm CS}(k|m)=Z_{\rm CS}(k|m)_{0}\,\exp\bigl(r\,c(k)\,\tfrac{i\pi}{4}\bigr)
and (
442
) becomes

\displaystyle Z_{r}=e^{ir\phi}\,\Bigl|\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{2+l+i\kappa}}\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2+l+i\kappa}\bigr)e^{\pi\kappa}\Bigr|^{2}\,,\qquad r\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,,

(446)

where, using
c(k)=3\bigl(1-\tfrac{2}{2+k}\bigr)
, the phase is given by

\displaystyle r\phi=r\bigl(c(l+i\kappa)+c(l-i\kappa)\bigr)\frac{\pi}{4}=r\bigl(1-\tfrac{2(2+l)}{(2+l)^{2}+\kappa^{2}}\bigr)\frac{3\pi}{2}\,.

(447)

In the weak-coupling limit
\kappa\to\infty
,

\displaystyle Z_{r}\to(-i)^{r}\,\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\kappa^{3}}\cdot e^{2\pi\kappa}\,.

(448)

Using (
434
) and taking into account that we took
G=SU(2)\times SU(2)
here, the absolute value agrees with our general one-loop result (
424
) in the metric formulation, with the phase
(-i)^{r}
matching Polchinski’s phase
i^{-P}=i^{-5}=-i
in (
112
) for odd framing
r
.
44
44

44

Strictly speaking for
r=1
mod 4, but
i^{P}
vs
i^{-P}
in (
112
) is a matter of conventions, so there is no meaningful distinction we can make here.
We do not have any useful insights into why (or whether) CS framing and the phase
i^{-P}
might have anything to do with each other, let alone why odd but not even framing should reproduce the phase of
Polchinski:1988ua
. Perhaps different contour rotation prescriptions as those assumed in
Polchinski:1988ua
might reproduce the canonically framed (
r=0
) result in the metric formulation of Euclidean gravity. We leave these questions open.

Comparison to previous results:

The Chern-Simons formulation of gravity was applied to calculate Euclidean
\Lambda>0
partition functions in
Carlip:1992wg
;
Guadagnini:1994ahx
;
Castro:2011xb
. The focus of these works was on summing different topologies. Our one-loop (
448
) in canonical framing agrees with
Carlip:1992wg
up to an unspecified overall normalization constant in the latter, agrees with
Z(S^{3})/Z(S^{1}\times S^{2})
in
Guadagnini:1994ahx
combining their eqs. (13),(32), and
disagrees with eq. (4.39) in
Castro:2011xb
,
Z^{(1)}(S^{3})=\pi^{3}/(2^{5}\kappa)
.

H.3
Chern-Simons formulation of higher-spin gravity

The
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
Chern-Simons formulation of Einstein gravity (
430
) has a natural extension to an
SL(n,{\mathbb{C}})
Chern-Simons formulation of higher-spin gravity — the positive cosmological constant analog of the theories studied e.g. in
Blencowe:1988gj
;
Bergshoeff:1989ns
;
Castro:2011fm
;
Perlmutter:2012ds
;
Campoleoni:2010zq
;
Ammon:2011nk
. The Lorentzian action is

\displaystyle S_{L}=(l+i\kappa)\,S_{\rm CS}[{\cal A}_{+}]+(l-i\kappa)\,S_{\rm CS}[{\cal A}_{-}]\,,\qquad{\cal A}_{+}^{*}={\cal A}_{-}\,,

(449)

where
S_{\rm CS}[{\cal A}]=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int{\rm Tr}_{n}\bigl({\cal A}\wedge d{\cal A}+\tfrac{2}{3}{\cal A}\wedge{\cal A}\wedge{\cal A}\bigr)
, an
{\cal A}
is an
{\rm sl}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
-valued connection,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
. The corresponding Euclidean action
S_{E}=-iS_{L}
extending (
435
) is given by

\displaystyle S_{E}=(\kappa-il)\,S_{\rm CS}[{\cal A}_{+}]-(\kappa+il)\,S_{\rm CS}[{\cal A}_{-}]\,,\qquad{\cal A}_{\pm}^{\dagger}=-{\cal A}_{\pm}\,,

(450)

where
{\cal A}_{\pm}
are now independent
{\rm su}(n)
-valued connections.

H.3.1
Landscape of dS
3
vacua

The solutions
A
of the original (
n=2
) Einstein gravity theory can be lifted to solutions
{\cal A}=R(A)
of the extended (
n>2
) theory by choosing an embedding
R
of
{\rm sl}(2)
into
{\rm sl}(n)
. More concretely, such lifts are specified by picking an
n
-dimensional representation
R
of
{\rm su}(2)
,

\displaystyle R=\oplus_{a}{\bf m}_{a}\,,\qquad\sum_{a}m_{a}=n\,,\qquad{\cal S}_{i}=R(S_{i})=\oplus_{a}J^{(m_{a})}_{i}\,.

(451)

Here
J^{(m)}_{i}
are the standard anti-hermitian spin
j=\frac{m-1}{2}
representation matrices of
{\rm su}(2)
, satisfying the same commutation relations and reality properties as the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
generators
S_{i}
in (
436
). Then the matrices
{\cal L}_{i}\equiv R(L_{i})
with the
L_{i}
as in (
432
) are real, generating the corresponding
n
-dimensional representation of
{\rm sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. The Casimir eigenvalue of the spin
j=\frac{m-1}{2}
irrep is
j(j+1)=\frac{1}{4}(m^{2}-1)
, so

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{n}({\cal S}_{i}{\cal S}_{j})=-\tfrac{1}{2}T_{R}\,\delta_{ij}\,,\qquad{\rm Tr}_{n}({\cal L}_{i}{\cal L}_{j})=\tfrac{1}{2}T_{R}\,\eta_{ij}\,,\qquad T_{R}\equiv\frac{1}{6}\sum_{a}m_{a}(m_{a}^{2}-1)\,;

(452)

A general
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
connection
A=A^{i}L_{i}
has curvature
dA+A\wedge A=\bigl(dA^{i}-\frac{1}{2}{\epsilon^{i}}_{jk}A^{j}A^{k}\bigr)L_{i}
, and an
SL(n,{\mathbb{C}})
connection of the form
{\cal A}=R(A)=A^{i}{\cal L}_{i}
has curvature
d{\cal A}+{\cal A}\wedge{\cal A}=\bigl(dA^{i}-\frac{1}{2}{\epsilon^{i}}_{jk}A^{j}A^{k}\bigr){\cal L}_{i}
, hence
{\cal A}=R(A)
solves the equations of motion of the extended
SL(n,{\mathbb{C}})
theory iff
A
solves the equations of motion of the original Einstein
SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})
theory. In other words, restricting to connections
{\cal A}=A^{i}{\cal L}_{i}
amounts to a consistent truncation, which may be interpreted as the gravitational subsector of the
n>2
theory. Substituting
{\cal A}=R(A)
into the action (
449
) gives the consistently truncated action

\displaystyle S_{L}=(l+i\kappa)T_{R}\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{+}]+(l-i\kappa)T_{R}\,S_{\rm CS}[A_{-}]\,,\qquad A_{+}^{*}=A_{-}\,,

(453)

which is of the exact same form as the original Einstein CS gravity theory (
430
), except
l+i\kappa
is replaced by
(l+i\kappa)T_{R}
. Thus we can naturally interpret the components
A^{i}_{\pm}
again as metric/vielbein/spin connection degrees of freedom, just like in (
431
), i.e.
A^{i}_{\pm}=\omega^{i}\pm ie^{i}/\ell
,
ds^{2}=\eta_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}
, and the lift
{\cal A}=R(A)
of the original solution
A
corresponding to the dS
3
metric again as a solution corresponding to the dS
3
metric. The difference is that the original relation (
434
) between
\kappa
and
\ell/G_{\rm N}
gets modified to

\displaystyle\kappa\,T_{R}=\frac{2\pi\ell}{8\pi G_{\rm N}}\,.

(454)

Since
\kappa
is fixed, this means the dimensionless ratio
\ell/G_{\rm N}
depends on the choice of
R
. Thus the different solutions
{\cal A}=R(A)
of the
SL(n,{\mathbb{C}})
theory can be thought of as different de Sitter vacua of the theory, labeled by
R
, with different values of the curvature radius in Planck units
\ell/G_{\rm N}
. These are the dS analog of the AdS vacua discussed in
Ammon:2011nk
. The total number of vacua labeled by
R=\oplus_{a}{\bf m}_{a}
equals the number of partitions of
n=\sum_{a}m_{a}
,

\displaystyle{\cal N}_{\rm vac}\sim e^{2\pi\sqrt{n/6}}\,\qquad(n\gg 1)\,.

(455)

For, say,
n\sim 2\times 10^{5}
, this gives
{\cal N}_{\rm vac}\sim 10^{500}
.

Analogous considerations hold for the Euclidean version of the theory. For example the round sphere solution (
437
) is lifted to

\displaystyle({\cal A}_{+},{\cal A}_{-})=\bigl(R(A_{+}),0\bigr)=\bigl(R(g)^{-1}dR(g),0\bigr),\qquad R(e^{\alpha^{i}S_{i}})\equiv e^{\alpha^{i}{\cal S}_{i}}\,,

(456)

with the sphere radius
\ell
in Planck units given again by (
454
). The tree-level contribution of the solution (
456
) to the Euclidean path integral is

\displaystyle\exp(-S_{E})=\exp\bigl(2\pi\kappa\,T_{R}\bigr)=\exp\Bigl(\frac{2\pi\ell}{4\,G_{\rm N}}\Bigr)\,.

(457)

Note that
{\cal S}^{(0)}\equiv-S_{E}=\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G_{\rm N}}
is the usual dS
3
Gibbons-Hawking horizon entropy
PhysRevD.15.2752
. Its value
{\cal S}^{(0)}=2\pi\kappa\,T_{R}
depends on the vacuum
R=\oplus_{a}{\bf m}_{a}
through
T_{R}
as given by (
452
). The vacuum
R
maximizing
e^{-S_{E}}
corresponds to the partition of
n=\sum_{a}m_{a}
maximizing
T_{R}
. Clearly the maximum is achieved for
R={\bf n}
:

\displaystyle\max_{R}\,T_{R}=T_{\bf n}=\frac{n(n^{2}-1)}{6}\,.

(458)

The corresponding embedding of
{\rm su}(2)
into
{\rm su}(n)
is called the “principal embedding”. Thus the “principal vacuum” maximizes the entropy at
S_{\rm GH,{\bf n}}=\frac{1}{6}n(n^{2}-1)\,2\pi\kappa
, exponentially dominating the Euclidean path integral in the semiclassical (large-
\kappa
) regime. In the remainder we focus on the Euclidean version of the theory.

H.3.2
Higher-spin field spectrum and algebra

Of course for
n>2
, there are more degrees of freedom in the
2(n^{2}-1)
independent components of
{\cal A}_{\pm}
than just the 3+3 vielbein and spin connection degrees of freedom
{\cal A}^{i}_{\pm}{\cal S}_{i}
. The full set of fluctuations around the vacuum solution can be interpreted in a metric-like formalism as higher-spin field degrees of freedom. The precise spectrum depends on the vacuum
R
. For the principal vacuum
R={\bf n}
, we get the higher-spin vielbein and spin connections of a set of massless spin-
s
fields of
s=2,3,\ldots,n
, as was worked out in detail for the AdS analog in
Campoleoni:2010zq
. Indeed
{\rm su}(n)
decomposes under the principally embedded
{\rm su}(2)
subalgebra into spin-
r
irreps,
r=1,2,\ldots,n-1
, generated by the traceless symmetric products
{\cal S}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}
of the generators
{\cal S}_{i}
. As reviewed in
Joung:2014qya
, this means we can identify the
{\rm su}(n)_{+}\oplus{\rm su}(n)_{-}
Lie algebra of the theory (
450
) with the higher-spin algebra

{\rm hs}_{n}({\rm su}(2))_{+}\oplus{\rm hs}_{n}({\rm su}(2))_{-}
, where
{\rm su}(2)_{+}\oplus{\rm su}(2)_{-}={\rm so}(4)
is the principally embedded gravitational subalgebra. In the metric-like formalism the spin-
r
generators correspond to (anti-)self-dual Killing tensors of rank
r
. These are the Killing tensors of massless symmetric spin-
s
fields with
s=r+1
. As a check, recall the number of (anti-)self-dual rank
r
Killing tensors on
S^{3}
equals
D^{4}_{r,\pm r}=2r+1
, correctly adding up to

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}D^{4}_{r,\pm r}=2\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(2r+1)=2(n^{2}-1)\,.

(459)

For different choices of embedding
R
, we get different
{\rm su}(2)
decompositions of
{\rm su}(n)
. For example for
n=12
, while the principal embedding
R={\bf 12}
considered above gives the
{\rm su}(2)
decomposition
{\bf 143}_{{\rm su}(12)}={\bf 3}+{\bf 5}+{\bf 7}+{\bf 9}+{\bf 11}+{\bf 13}+{\bf 15}+{\bf 17}+{\bf 19}+{\bf 21}+{\bf 23}
, taking
R={\bf 6}\oplus{\bf 4}\oplus{\bf 2}
gives
{\bf 143}_{{\rm su}(12)}=2\cdot{\bf 1}+7\cdot{\bf 3}+8\cdot{\bf 5}+6\cdot{\bf 7}+3\cdot{\bf 9}+{\bf 11}
. Interpreting these as Killing tensors for
n_{s}
massless spin-
s
fields, we get for the former
n_{2}=1,n_{3}=1,\ldots,n_{12}=1
, and for the latter
n_{1}=2,n_{2}=7,n_{3}=8,n_{4}=6,n_{5}=3,n_{6}=1
. The tree-level entropy
{\cal S}^{(0)}=2\pi\ell/4G_{\rm N}
for
R={\bf 12}
is
{\cal S}^{(0)}=286\cdot 2\pi\kappa
, and for
R={\bf 6}\oplus{\bf 4}\oplus{\bf 2}
it is
{\cal S}^{(0)}=46\cdot 2\pi\kappa
.

H.3.3
One-loop Euclidean path integral from metric-like formulation

In view of the above higher-spin interpretation of the theory, we can apply our general massless HS formula (
424
) with
G=SU(n)\times SU(n)
to obtain the one-loop contribution to the Euclidean path integral (for
l=0
). In combination with (
454
) this takes the form

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI}=i^{-P}\,\frac{(2\pi\gamma)^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}}\,,\qquad\gamma=\sqrt{\frac{8\pi G_{\rm N}}{2\pi\ell}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa\,T_{R}}}\,.

(460)

Recall that
{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
is the volume of
G
with respect to the metric normalized such that
\langle M|M\rangle_{\rm c}=1
, where
M
is one of the standard
{\rm so}(4)={\rm su}(2)\oplus{\rm su}(2)
generators, which we can for instance take to be the rotation generator
M={\cal S}_{3}\oplus{\cal S}_{3}
. In the context of Chern-Simons theory, it is more natural to consider the volume
{\rm vol}(G)_{{\rm Tr}_{n}}
with respect to the metric defined by the trace appearing in the Chern-Simons action (
450
). Using the definition of
T_{R}
in (
453
), we see the trace norm of
M
is
\langle M|M\rangle_{{\rm Tr}_{n}}=-2\,{\rm Tr}_{n}({\cal S}_{3}{\cal S}_{3})=T_{R}=T_{R}\langle M|M\rangle_{\rm c}
, hence
{\rm vol}(G)_{{\rm Tr}_{n}}=(\sqrt{T_{R}})^{\dim G}\,{\rm vol}{(G)}_{{\rm c}}
. Note that upon substituting this in (
460
), the
T_{R}
-dependent factors cancel out. Finally, using (
422
), we get
P=\sum_{s=2}^{n}P_{s}=\frac{1}{3}(2s-3)(2s-1)(2s+1)=\frac{2}{3}n^{2}(n-1)(n+1)-(n^{2}-1)
. Because
(n-1)\cdot n\cdot(n+1)
is divisible by 3, the first term is an integer, and moreover a multiple of
8
because either
n^{2}
or
(n+1)(n-1)
is a multiple of
4
. Hence
i^{-P}=i^{(n^{2}-1)}
, which equals
-i
for even
n
and
+1
for odd
n
. Thus we get

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI}=i^{n^{2}-1}\,\frac{(2\pi\tilde{\gamma})^{\dim G}}{{\rm vol}(G)_{{\rm Tr}_{n}}}\,,\qquad\quad\tilde{\gamma}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\,.

(461)

H.3.4
Euclidean path integral from CS formulation

As in the
SU(2)\times SU(2)
Einstein gravity case, we can derive an all-loop expression for the Euclidean partition function
Z(R)
of the
SU(n)\times SU(n)
higher-spin gravity theory (
450
) expanded around a lifted round sphere solution
\bar{\cal A}=R(\bar{A})
such as (
456
), by naive analytic continuation of the exact
SU(n)_{k_{+}}\times SU(n)_{k_{-}}
partition function on
S^{3}
to
k_{\pm}=l\pm i\kappa
, paralleling (
440
) and the subsequent discussion there. The
SU(n)_{k}
generalization of the canonically framed
SU(2)_{k}
result (
441
) as spelled out e.g. in
Marino:2004uf
;
Periwal:1993yu
is

\displaystyle Z_{\rm CS}(SU(n)_{k}|\bar{\cal A})_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{(n+k)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}\prod_{p=1}^{n-1}\Bigl(2\sin\frac{\pi p}{n+k}\Bigr)^{(n-p)}\cdot e^{i(n+k)S_{\rm CS}[\bar{\cal A}]}\,.

(462)

The corresponding higher-spin generalization of (
442
) is therefore

\displaystyle Z(R)_{0}=\biggl|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{(n+l+i\kappa)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}\prod_{p=1}^{n-1}\Bigl(2\sin\frac{\pi p}{n+l+i\kappa}\Bigr)^{(n-p)}\,\biggr|^{2}\,\cdot e^{2\pi\kappa T_{R}}\,.

(463)

Physically this can be interpreted as the all-loop quantum-corrected Euclidean partition function of the dS
3
static patch in the vacuum labeled by
R
.
The analog of the result (
443
) for more general framing
I_{M}
is

\displaystyle Z_{\rm CS}(SU(n)_{k}|\bar{\cal A})=\exp\bigl(\tfrac{i}{24}c(k)I_{M}\bigr)\,Z_{\rm CS}(SU(n)_{k}|\bar{\cal A})_{0}\,,\qquad c(k)=(n^{2}-1)\bigl(1-\tfrac{n}{n+k}\bigr)\,,

(464)

hence the generalization of (
446
) for arbitrary framing
I_{M}=6\pi r
,
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, is

\displaystyle Z(R)_{r}=e^{ir\phi}\,Z(R)_{0}\,,

(465)

where
\phi=\bigl(c(l+i\kappa)+c(l-i\kappa)\bigr)\frac{\pi}{4}=\bigl(1-\frac{2(n+l)}{(n+l)^{2}+\kappa^{2}}\bigr)(n^{2}-1)\frac{\pi}{2}
. In the limit
\kappa\to\infty
,

\displaystyle Z(R)_{r}\to i^{r(n^{2}-1)}\,\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{\kappa^{n-1}}\prod_{r=1}^{n-1}\Bigl(\frac{2\pi r}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2(n-r)}=i^{r(n^{2}-1)}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\right)^{2(n^{2}-1)}\biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{s=2}^{n}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{(2\pi)^{s}}\biggr)^{2}\,.

(466)

Recognizing
n^{2}-1=\dim SU(n)
and
\sqrt{n}\prod_{s=2}^{n}(2\pi)^{s}/\Gamma(s)={\rm vol}(SU(n))_{{\rm Tr}_{n}}
(
288
),
we see this precisely reproduces the one-loop result (
461
). Like in the original
n=2
case, the phase again matches for odd framing
r
. (The agreement at one loop can also be seen more directly by a slight variation of the computation leading to (
429
).) This provides a nontrivial check of our higher-spin gravity formula (
424
) and more generally (
112
).

H.3.5
Large-
n
limit and topological string description

In generic dS
d+1
higher-spin theories,
\dim G=\infty
. To mimic this case, consider the
n\to\infty
limit of
SU(n)\times SU(n)
dS
3
higher-spin theory with
l=0
.
A basic observation is that the loop expansion is only reliable then if
n/\kappa\ll 1
. Using (
454
), this translates to
T_{R}\,n\ll\frac{\ell}{G_{\rm N}}

For the exponentially dominant principal vacuum
R={\bf n}
, this becomes
n^{4}\ll\ell/G_{\rm N}
while at the other extreme, for the nearly-trivial
R={\bf 2}\oplus{\bf 1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\bf 1}
, this becomes
n\ll\ell/G_{\rm N}
. Either way, for fixed
\ell/G_{N}
, the large-
n
limit is necessarily strongly coupled, and the one-loop formula (
460
), or equivalently (
461
) or (
466
), becomes unreliable. Indeed, according to this formula,
\log Z^{(1)}\sim\log\left(\frac{n}{\kappa}\right)\cdot n^{2}
in this limit, whereas the exact expression (
463
) actually implies
\log Z^{\rm{(loops)}}\to 0
.

In fact, the partition function
does
have a natural weak coupling expansion in the
n\to\infty
limit — not as a 3D higher-spin gravity theory, but rather as a topological string theory.
U(n)_{k}
Chern-Simons theory on
S^{3}
has a description
Witten:1992fb
as an open topological string theory on the deformed conifold
T^{*}S^{3}
with
n
topological D-branes wrapped on the
S^{3}
, and a large-
n
’t Hooft dual description
Gopakumar:1998ki
as a
closed
string theory on the
resolved
conifold. Both descriptions are reviewed in
Marino:2004uf
, whose notation we follow here. The string coupling constant is
g_{s}=2\pi/(n+k)
and the Kähler modulus of the resolved conifold is
t=\int_{S^{2}}J+iB=ig_{s}n=2\pi in/(n+k)
. Under this identification,

\displaystyle Z_{\rm CS}(SU(n)_{k})_{0}=\sqrt{\tfrac{n+k}{n}}\,Z_{\rm CS}(U(n)_{k})_{0}=\sqrt{\tfrac{2\pi i}{t}}\,Z_{\rm top}(g_{s},t)\equiv\tilde{Z}_{\rm top}(g_{s},t)\,.

(467)

Thus we can write the
SU(n)_{l+i\kappa}\times SU(n)_{l-i\kappa}
higher-spin Euclidean gravity partition function (
463
) expanded around the round
S^{3}
solution
\bar{\cal A}=R(\bar{A})
as

\displaystyle\boxed{Z(R)_{0}=\left|\tilde{Z}_{\rm top}(g_{s},t)\,e^{-\pi T_{R}\cdot 2\pi i/g_{s}}\right|^{2}}

(468)

where
T_{R}
was defined in (
452
), maximized for
R={\bf n}
at
T_{\bf n}=\frac{1}{6}n(n^{2}-1)
, and

\displaystyle g_{s}=\frac{2\pi}{n+l+i\kappa}\,,\qquad t=ig_{s}n=\frac{2\pi in}{n+l+i\kappa}\,.

(469)

Note that
t
takes values inside a half-disk of radius
\frac{1}{2}
centered at
t=i\pi
, with
{\rm Re}\,t>0
. The higher-spin gravity theory (or the open string theory description on the deformed conifold) is weakly coupled when
\kappa\gg n
, which implies
|t|\ll 1
. In the free field theory limit
\kappa\to\infty
, we get
g_{s}\sim-2\pi i/\kappa\to 0
and
t\sim 2\pi n/\kappa\to 0
, which is singular from the closed string point of view.
In the ’t Hooft limit
n\to\infty
with
t
kept finite, the closed string is weakly coupled and sees a smooth geometry. The earlier discussed Vasiliev-like limit
n\to\infty
with
l=0
and
\ell/G_{\rm N}\sim T_{\bf n}\kappa\sim n^{3}\kappa
fixed, infinitely strongly coupled from the 3D field theory point of view, maps to
g_{s}\sim 2\pi/n\to 0
and
t\sim 2\pi i+2\pi\kappa/n\to 2\pi i
, which is again singular from the closed string point of view, differing from the 3D free field theory singularity by a mere
B
-field monodromy, reflecting the more general
n\leftrightarrow l+i\kappa
,
t\leftrightarrow 2\pi i-t
level-rank symmetry.

Appendix I
Quantum dS entropy: computations and examples

Here we provide the details for section
8
.

I.1
Classical gravitational dS thermodynamics

I.1.1
3D Einstein gravity example

For concreteness we start with pure 3D Einstein gravity as a guiding example, but we will phrase the discussion so generalization will be clear. The Euclidean action in this case is

S_{E}[g]=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int d^{3}x\sqrt{g}\Bigl(\Lambda-\frac{1}{2}R\Bigr)\,,

(470)

with
\Lambda>0
. The tree-level contribution to the entropy (
153
) is

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}=\log{\cal Z}^{(0)}\,,\qquad{\cal Z}^{(0)}=\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,{\cal D}g\,e^{-S_{E}[g]}\,.

(471)

The dominant saddle of (
471
) is a round
S^{3}
metric
g_{\ell}
of radius
\ell\!=\!\ell_{0}
minimizing
S_{E}(\ell)\equiv S_{E}[g_{\ell}]
:

\displaystyle{\cal Z}^{(0)}=\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,d\ell\,e^{-S_{E}(\ell)}\,,\qquad S_{E}(\ell)=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{8\pi G}\bigl(\Lambda\ell^{3}-3\ell\bigr)\,,

(472)

where
\int_{\rm tree}
means evaluation at the saddle point, here at the on-shell radius
\ell=\ell_{0}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{\ell}S_{E}(\ell_{0})=0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1}{\ell_{0}^{2}}\,,\qquad{\cal S}^{(0)}=-S_{E}(\ell_{0})=\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}\,,

(473)

reproducing the familiar area law
{\cal S}^{(0)}=A/4G
for the horizon entropy.

We now recast the above in a way that will allow us to make contact with the formulae of section
7.1
and will naturally generalize beyond tree level in a diffeomorphism-invariant way. To this end we define an “off-shell” tree-level partition function at fixed (off-shell) volume
V
:

\displaystyle Z^{(0)}(V)\equiv\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,d\sigma\,\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,{\cal D}g\,e^{-S_{E}[g]+\sigma(\int\!\sqrt{g}-V)}\,.

(474)

Evaluating the integral is equivalent to a constrained extremization problem with Lagrange multiplier
\sigma
enforcing the constraint
\int\!\sqrt{g}=V
.
The dominant saddle is the round sphere
g=g_{\ell}
of radius
\ell(V)
fixed by the volume constraint:

\displaystyle Z^{(0)}(V)=e^{-S_{E}(\ell)}\,,\qquad 2\pi^{2}\ell^{3}=V\,.

(475)

Paralleling (
135
) and (
136
), we define from this an off-shell energy density and entropy,

\displaystyle\rho^{(0)}

\displaystyle\equiv-\partial_{V}\log Z^{(0)}=-{\tfrac{1}{3}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(0)}}\,/\,{V}=\bigl(\Lambda-\ell^{-2}\bigr)/8\pi G

(476)

\displaystyle S^{(0)}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigl(1-V\partial_{V}\bigr)\log Z^{(0)}=\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{3}\ell\partial_{\ell}\bigr)\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}\,.

\rho^{(0)}
is the sum of the positive cosmological constant and negative curvature energy densities.
S^{(0)}
is independent of
\Lambda
. It is the Legendre transform of
\log Z^{(0)}
:

\displaystyle S^{(0)}=\log Z^{(0)}+V\rho^{(0)}\,,\qquad d\log Z^{(0)}=-\rho^{(0)}dV\,,\qquad dS^{(0)}=Vd\rho^{(0)}\,,

(477)

Note that evaluating
\int_{\rm tree}d\sigma
in (
474
) sets
\sigma=-\partial_{V}\log Z^{(0)}=\rho^{(0)}(V)
.
On shell,

\displaystyle\rho^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=0\,,\qquad{\cal S}^{(0)}=\log Z^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}\,.

(478)

Paralleling (
137
), the differential relations in (
477
) can be viewed as the first law of tree-level de Sitter thermodynamics. We can also consider variations of coupling constants such as
\Lambda
. Then
d\log Z^{(0)}=-\rho^{(0)}dV-\frac{1}{8\pi G}Vd\Lambda
,
dS^{(0)}=Vd\rho^{(0)}-\frac{1}{8\pi G}Vd\Lambda
. On shell,
d{\cal S}^{(0)}=-\frac{V_{0}}{8\pi G}\,d\Lambda
.

I.1.2
General
d
and higher-order curvature corrections

The above formulae readily extend to general dimensions and to gravitational actions
S_{E}[g]
with general higher-order curvature corrections.
Using that
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=(g_{\mu\rho}g_{\nu\sigma}-g_{\mu\sigma}g_{\nu\rho})/\ell^{2}
for the round
45
45

45

By virtue of its
SO(d+2)
symmetry, the round sphere metric
g_{\ell}
with
\Omega_{d+1}\ell^{d+1}=V
is a saddle of (
474
). Spheres of dimension
\geq 5
admit a plentitude of Einstein metrics that are
not
round
10.4310/jdg/1214431962
;
Bohm:1998vc
;
Gibbons:2002th
;
Boyer:2003pe
, but as explained e.g. in
Gibbons:2011dh
, by Bishop’s theorem
bishop
, these saddles are subdominant in Einstein gravity. In the large-size limit, higher-order curvature corrections are small, hence the round sphere dominates in this regime.

S^{d+1}
,
Z^{(0)}(V)
(
474
) can be evaluated explicitly for any action. It takes the form

\displaystyle\log Z^{(0)}(V)=-S_{E}[g_{\ell}]=\frac{\Omega_{d+1}}{8\pi G}\bigl(-\Lambda\ell^{d+1}+\tfrac{d(d+1)}{2}\,\ell^{d-1}+\cdots\bigr)\,,\qquad\Omega_{d+1}\ell^{d+1}=V\,,

(479)

where
+\cdots
is a sum of
R^{n}
higher-order curvature corrections
\propto\ell^{-2n}
and
\Omega_{d+1}=(\ref{volSn})
.
The off-shell energy density and entropy are defined as in (
476
)

\displaystyle\rho^{(0)}

\displaystyle={-\tfrac{1}{d+1}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(0)}}\,/\,{V}=\bigl(\Lambda-\tfrac{d(d-1)}{2}\,\ell^{-2}+\cdots\bigr)/8\pi G

(480)

\displaystyle S^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{d+1}\ell\partial_{\ell}\bigr)\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{A}{4G}\bigl(1+\cdots\bigr)\,.

where
A=\Omega_{d-1}\ell^{d-1}

and
+\cdots
are
1/\ell^{2n}
curvature corrections. The on-shell radius
\ell_{0}
solves
\rho^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=0
, most conveniently viewed as giving a parametrization
\Lambda(\ell_{0})
.

As an example, consider the general action up to order
R^{2}
written as

\displaystyle S_{E}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\Bigl(\Lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}R-l_{s}^{2}\bigl(\lambda_{C^{2}}C^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+\lambda_{R^{2}}R^{2}+\lambda_{E^{2}}E^{\mu\nu}E_{\mu\nu}\bigr)\Bigr)\,,

(481)

where
E_{\mu\nu}\equiv R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{d+1}R\,g_{\mu\nu}
,
C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is the Weyl tensor,
l_{s}
is a length scale and the
\lambda_{i}
are dimensionless. The Weyl tensor vanishes on the round sphere and
R_{\mu\nu}=d\,g_{\mu\nu}/\ell^{2}
, hence

\displaystyle\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{\Omega_{d+1}}{8\pi G}\bigl(-\Lambda\,\ell^{d+1}+\tfrac{1}{2}d(d+1)\,\ell^{d-1}+\lambda_{R^{2}}\,d^{2}(d+1)^{2}\,l_{s}^{2}\,\ell^{d-3}\,\bigr)\,,

(482)

For example for
d=2
,

\displaystyle\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{\pi}{4G}\Bigl(-\Lambda\,\ell^{3}+3\,\ell+\frac{36\,l_{s}^{2}\lambda_{R^{2}}}{\ell}\Bigr)\,,

(483)

hence, using (
480
) and
\rho^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=0
,

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}\Bigl(1+\frac{24\,l_{s}^{2}\lambda_{R^{2}}}{\ell_{0}^{2}}\Bigr)\,,\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1}{\ell_{0}^{2}}\Bigl(1-\frac{12\,l_{s}^{2}\lambda_{R^{2}}}{\ell_{0}^{2}}\Bigr)\,.

(484)

I.1.3
Effective field theory expansion and field redefinitions

Curvature corrections such as those considered above naturally appear as terms in the derivative expansion of low-energy effective field theories of quantum gravity, with
l_{s}
the characteristic length scale of UV-completing physics and higher-order curvature corrections terms suppressed by higher powers of
l_{s}^{2}/\ell^{2}\ll 1
. The action (
481
) is then viewed as a truncation at order
l_{s}^{2}
, and
(
484
) can be solved perturbatively to obtain
\ell_{0}
and
{\cal S}^{(0)}
as a function of
\Lambda
.

Suppose someone came up with some fundamental theory of de Sitter quantum gravity, producing both a precise microscopic computation of the entropy and a precise low-energy effective action, with the large-
\ell_{0}/l_{s}
expansion reproduced as some large-
N
expansion. At least superficially, the higher-order curvature-corrected entropy obtained above looks like a Wald entropy
Wald:1993nt
;
Iyer:1994ys
.
In the spirit of for instance the nontrivial matching of
R^{2}
corrections to the macroscopic BPS black hole entropy computed in
LopesCardoso:1998tkj
and the microscopic entropy computed from M-theory in
Maldacena:1997de
, it might seem then that matching microscopic
1/N
-corrections and macroscopic
l_{s}^{2}/\ell_{0}^{2}
-corrections to the entropy such as those in (
484
) could offer a nontrivial way of testing such a hypothetical theory.

However, this is not the case. Unlike the Wald entropy, there are no charges
Q
(such as energy, angular momentum or gauge charges) available here to give these corrections physical meaning as corrections in the large-
Q
expansion of a function
S(Q)
. Indeed, the detailed structure of the
l_{s}/\ell_{0}
expansion of
{\cal S}^{(0)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})
has no intrinsic physical meaning at all, because all of it can be wiped out by a local metric field redefinition, order by order in
l_{s}/\ell_{0}
, bringing the entropy to pure Einstein area law form, and leaving only the value of
{\cal S}^{(0)}
itself as a physically meaningful, field-redefinition invariant, dimensionless quantity.

This is essentially a trivial consequence of the fact that in perturbation theory about the round sphere, the round sphere itself is the unique solution to the equations of motion. Let us however recall in more detail how this works at the level of local field redefinitions, and show how this is expressed at the level of
\log Z^{(0)}(\ell)
, as this will be useful later in interpreting quantum corrections. For concreteness, consider again (
481
) viewed as a gravitational effective field theory action expanded to order
l_{s}^{2}R^{2}
. Under a local metric field redefinition

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\to g_{\mu\nu}+\delta g_{\mu\nu}+O(l_{s}^{4})\,,\qquad\delta g_{\mu\nu}\equiv l_{s}^{2}\bigl(u_{0}\,\Lambda\,g_{\mu\nu}+u_{1}\,R\,g_{\mu\nu}+u_{2}\,R_{\mu\nu}\bigr)\,,

(485)

where the
u_{i}
are dimensionless constants,
the action transforms as

\displaystyle S_{E}\to S_{E}+\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\,\bigl(R^{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}Rg^{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g^{\mu\nu}\bigr)\,\delta g_{\mu\nu}\,+\,O(l_{s}^{4})\,,

(486)

shifting
\lambda_{R^{2}},\lambda_{E^{2}}
and rescaling
G,\Lambda
in (
481
). A suitable choice of
u_{i}
brings
S_{E}
to the form

\displaystyle S_{E}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\Bigl(\!\Lambda^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}R-l_{s}^{2}\lambda_{C^{2}}C^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+O(l_{s}^{4})\!\Bigr),\qquad\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda\bigl(1-\tfrac{4(d+1)^{2}}{(d-1)^{2}}\,\lambda_{R^{2}}l_{s}^{2}\Lambda\bigr).

(487)

Equivalently, this is obtained by using the
O(l_{s}^{0})
equations of motion
R_{\mu\nu}=\frac{2}{d-1}\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}
in the
O(l_{s}^{2})
part of the action. Since
\lambda^{\prime}_{R^{2}}=0
, the entropy computed from this equivalent action takes a pure Einstein area law form
{\cal S}^{(0)}=\Omega_{d+1}\ell_{0}^{\prime\,d-1}/4G
, with
\ell_{0}^{\prime}=\sqrt{d(d-1)/2\Lambda^{\prime}}
. The on-shell value
{\cal S}^{(0)}
itself remains unchanged of course under this change of variables.

In the above we picked a field redefinition keeping
G^{\prime}=G
. Further redefining
g_{\mu\nu}\to\alpha\,g_{\mu\nu}
leads to another equivalent set of couplings
G^{\prime\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime},\ldots
rescaled with powers of
\alpha
according to their mass dimension. We could then pick
\alpha
such that instead
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}=\Lambda
, or such that
\ell_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\ell_{0}
, now with
G^{\prime\prime}\neq G
. If we keep
\ell_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\ell_{0}
, we get

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{\Omega_{d+1}\ell_{0}^{d-1}}{4G^{\prime\prime}}\,,\qquad\Lambda^{\prime\prime}=\frac{d(d-1)}{2\ell_{0}^{2}}\,,

(488)

where for example in
d=2
starting from (
484
),
G^{\prime\prime}=G\bigl(1-24\,\lambda_{R^{2}}l_{s}^{2}/\ell_{0}^{2}+O(l_{s}^{4})\bigr)
.

At the level of
\log Z^{(0)}(\ell)
in (
482
) the metric redefinition (
485
) amounts to a radius redefininition
\ell\to\ell\,f(\ell)
with
f(\ell)=1+l_{s}^{2}\bigl(v_{10}\Lambda+v_{11}\ell^{-2}\bigr)+O(l_{s}^{4})
.
For suitable
v_{i}
this brings
\log Z^{(0)}
and therefore
{\cal S}^{(0)}
to pure Einstein form. E.g. for the
d=2
example (
483
),

\displaystyle\ell=\bigl(1-12\,\lambda_{R^{2}}l_{s}^{2}\bigl(\Lambda+\ell^{\prime\,-2}\bigr)\bigr)\ell^{\prime}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\log Z^{(0)}=\frac{\pi}{4G}\bigl(-\Lambda^{\prime}\,\ell^{\prime\,3}+3\,\ell^{\prime}+O(l_{s}^{4})\bigr)\,.

(489)

The above considerations generalize to all orders in the
l_{s}
expansion.
R^{n}
corrections to
\log Z^{(0)}
are
\propto(l_{s}/\ell)^{2n}
and can be removed order by order by a local metric/radius redefinition

\ell\to\alpha\,f(\ell)\,\ell\,,\qquad f(\ell)=1+l_{s}^{2}\bigl(v_{10}\Lambda+v_{11}\ell^{-2}\bigr)+l_{s}^{4}\bigl(v_{20}\Lambda^{2}+v_{21}\Lambda\ell^{-2}+v_{22}\ell^{-4}\bigl)\,+\cdots

(490)

bringing
\log Z^{(0)}
and thus
{\cal S}^{(0)}
to Einstein form to any order in the
l_{s}
expansion.

In
d=2
, the Weyl tensor vanishes identically. The remaining higher-order curvature invariants involve the Ricci tensor only, so can be removed by field redefinitions, reducing the action to Einstein form in general. Thus in
d=2
,
{\cal S}^{(0)}
is the only tree-level invariant in the theory, i.e. the only physical coupling constant. In the Chern-Simons formulation of
H.2
,
{\cal S}^{(0)}=2\pi\kappa
. In
d\geq 3
, there are infinitely many independent coupling constants, such as the Weyl-squared
\lambda_{C^{2}}
in (
481
), which are not picked up by
{\cal S}^{(0)}
, but are analogously probed by invariants
{\cal S}^{(0)}_{M}=\log{\cal Z}^{(0)}[g_{M}]=-S_{E}[g_{M}]
for saddle geometries
g_{M}
different from the round sphere. We comment on those and their role in the bigger picture in section
I.5
.

The point of considering quantum corrections to the entropy
{\cal S}
is that these include nonlocal contributions, not removable by local redefinitions, and thus, unlike the tree-level entropy
{\cal S}^{(0)}
, offering actual data quantitatively constraining candidate microscopic models.

I.2
Quantum gravitational thermodynamics

The
quantum
off-shell partition function
Z(V)
generalizing the tree-level
Z^{(0)}(V)
(
474
) is defined by replacing
\int_{\text{tree}}{\cal D}g\to\int{\cal D}g
in that expression:
46
46

46

Z(V)
is reminiscent of but different from the fixed-volume partition function considered in the 2D quantum gravity literature, e.g. (2.20) in
Ginsparg:1993is
. The latter would be defined as above but with
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{i{\mathbb{R}}}d\sigma
instead of
\int_{\text{tree}}d\sigma
, constraining the volume to
V
, whereas
Z(V)
constrains the
expectation value
of the volume to
V
.

\displaystyle Z(V)\equiv\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,d\sigma\,\mbox{\Large$\int$}{\cal D}g\,e^{-S_{E}[g]+\sigma(\int\!\sqrt{g}-V)}\,.

(491)

The quantum off-shell energy density and entropy generalizing (
476
) are

\displaystyle\rho(V)\equiv-\partial_{V}\log Z\,,\qquad S(V)\equiv\bigl(1-V\partial_{V})\log Z\,.

(492)

S
is the Legendre transform of
\log Z
:

\displaystyle S=\log Z+V\rho\,,\qquad d\log Z=-\rho\,dV\,,\qquad dS=Vd\rho\,.

(493)

Writing
e^{-\Gamma(V)}\equiv Z(V)
, the above definitions imply that as a function of
\rho
,

\displaystyle S(\rho)=\log\mbox{\Large$\int$}_{\!\!\!\text{tree}}\,dV\,e^{-\Gamma(V)+\rho V}=\log\mbox{\Large$\int$}{\cal D}g\,e^{-S_{E}[g]+\rho\int\!\sqrt{g}}

(494)

hence
S(\rho)
is the generating function for moments of the volume. In particular

\displaystyle V=\bigl\langle\mbox{\large$\int$}\!\sqrt{g}\bigr\rangle_{\rho}=\partial_{\rho}S(\rho)

(495)

is the expectation value of the volume in the presence of a source
\rho
shifting the cosmological constant
\frac{\Lambda}{8\pi G}\to\frac{\Lambda}{8\pi G}-\rho
.

\Gamma(V)
can be viewed as a quantum effective action for the volume, in the spirit of the QFT 1PI effective action
weinberg_1996
;
Vilkovisky:1984st
;
Barvinsky:1985an
but taking only the volume off-shell. At tree level it reduces to
S_{E}[g_{\ell}]
appearing in (
479
).
At the quantum on-shell value
V=\bar{V}=\bigl\langle\mbox{\large$\int$}\!\sqrt{g}\bigr\rangle_{0}
,

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{V})=0\,,\qquad{\cal S}=\log Z(\bar{V})=S(\bar{V})\,.

(496)

It will again be convenient to work with a linear scale variable
\ell
instead of
V
,
defined
by

\displaystyle\Omega_{d+1}\ell^{d+1}\equiv V\,,

(497)

Since the mean volume
V=\bigl\langle\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\bigr\rangle_{\rho}
is diffeomorphism invariant, (
497
) gives a manifestly diffeomorphism-invariant definition of the “mean radius”
\ell
of the fluctuating geometry. Given
Z(\ell)\equiv Z(V(\ell))
, the off-shell energy density and entropy are then computed as

\displaystyle\rho(\ell)=-\frac{\frac{1}{d+1}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z}{V}\,,\qquad S(\ell)=\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{d+1}\ell\partial_{\ell}\bigr)\log Z\,.

(498)

The quantum on-shell value of
\ell
is denoted by
\bar{\ell}
and satisfies
\rho(\bar{\ell})=0
.

The magnitude of quantum fluctuations of the volume about its mean value is given by
\delta V^{2}\equiv\bigl\langle\bigl(\int\!\!\sqrt{g}-V\bigr)^{2}\bigr\rangle_{\rho}=S^{\prime\prime}(\rho)=1/\Gamma^{\prime\prime}(V)=1/\rho^{\prime}(V)=V/S^{\prime}(V)
. At large
V
,
\delta V/V\propto 1/\sqrt{S}
.

I.3
One-loop corrected de Sitter entropy

The path integral (
491
) for
\log Z
can be computed perturbatively about its round sphere saddle in a semiclassical expansion in powers of
G
. To leading order it reduces to
\log Z^{(0)}
defined in (
474
). For 3D Einstein gravity,

\displaystyle\log Z(V)=\log Z^{(0)}(V)+O(G^{0})=\frac{\Omega_{3}}{8\pi G}\bigl(-\Lambda\,\ell^{3}+3\,\ell\bigr)\,+\,O(G^{0})\,,

(499)

To compute the one-loop
O(G^{0})
correction, recall that evaluation of
\int_{\rm tree}d\sigma
in (
491
) is equivalent to extremization with respect to
\sigma
, which sets
\sigma=-\partial_{V}\log Z(V)=\rho(V)
and

\displaystyle\log Z(V)=\log\mbox{\Large$\int$}{\cal D}g\,e^{-S_{E}[g]+\rho(V)(\int\!\sqrt{g}-V)}\,.

(500)

To one-loop order, we may replace
\rho
by its tree-level approximation
\rho^{(0)}=-\partial_{V}\log Z^{(0)}
. By construction this ensures the round sphere metric
g=g_{\ell}
of radius
\ell(V)
given by (
497
) is a saddle. Expanding the action to quadratic order in fluctuations about this saddle then gives a massless spin-2 Gaussian path integral of the type solved in general by (
112
), or more explicitly in (
148
)-(
149
). For 3D Einstein gravity, using (
149
),

\displaystyle\log Z=-\Bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{8\pi G}+c_{0}^{\prime}\Bigr)\Omega_{3}\ell^{3}+\Bigl(\frac{1}{8\pi G}+c_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{3}{4\pi\epsilon}\Bigr)3\Omega_{3}\ell-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)+O(G)

(501)

Here
c_{0}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
arise from
O(G^{0})
local counterterms

\displaystyle S_{E,\rm ct}=\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\Bigl(c_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{c_{2}^{\prime}}{2}R\Bigr)\,,

(502)

split off from the bare action (
470
) to keep the tree-level couplngs
\Lambda
and
G
equal to their “physical” (renormalized) values to this order. We define these physical values as
the coefficients of the local terms
\propto\ell^{3},\ell
in the
V\to\infty
asymptotic expansion of the
quantum

\log Z(V)
. That is to say, we fix
c_{0}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
by imposing the renormalization condition

\displaystyle\log Z(V)=\frac{\Omega_{3}}{8\pi G}\bigl(-\Lambda\,\ell^{3}+3\,\ell\,\bigr)+\cdots\qquad(V\to\infty)\,.

(503)

This renormalization prescription is diffeomorphism invariant, since
Z(V)
,
V
and
\ell
were all defined in a manifestly diffeomorphism-invariant way.
In (
501
) it fixes
c_{0}^{\prime}=0
,
c_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{3}{4\pi\epsilon}
, hence
\log Z(\ell)=\log Z^{(0)}+\log Z^{(1)}+O(G)
, where

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}=-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)\,.

(504)

We can express the renormalization condition (
503
) equivalently as

\displaystyle\boxed{\log Z^{(1)}=\log Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}+\log Z_{\rm ct}\,,\qquad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(1)}=0}

(505)

where
\log Z_{\rm ct}=-S_{E,\rm ct}[g_{\ell}]
with
g_{\ell}
the round sphere metric of volume
V
. On
S^{3}
we have
\log Z_{\rm ct}=c_{0}\ell^{3}+c_{2}\ell
, and the
\ell\to\infty
condition fixes
c_{0}
and
c_{2}
. Recalling (
135
), we can physically interpret this as requiring the renormalized one-loop Euclidean energy
U^{(1)}
of the static patch vanishes in the
\ell\to\infty
limit.

For general
d
, the UV-divergent terms in
\log Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
come with non-negative powers
\propto\ell^{d+1-2n}
, canceled by counterterms consisting of
n
-th order curvature invariants. For example on
S^{5}
,
\log Z_{\rm ct}=c_{0}\ell^{5}+c_{2}\ell^{3}+c_{4}\ell
. In odd
d+1
, the renormalization prescription (
505
) then fixes the
c_{2n}
. In even
d+1
,
\log Z_{\rm ct}
has a constant term
c_{d+1}
, which is not fixed by (
505
). As we will make explicit in examples later, it can be fixed by
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}Z^{(1)}=0
for massive field contributions, and for massless field contributions by minimal subtraction at scale
L
,
c_{d+1}=-\alpha_{d+1}\log(M_{\epsilon}L)
,
M_{\epsilon}=2e^{-\gamma}/\epsilon
(
261
), with
L\partial_{L}\log Z=0
, i.e.
L\partial_{L}\log Z^{(0)}=\alpha_{d+1}
.

The renormalized off-shell
\rho
and
S
are obtained from
\log Z
as in (
498
). For 3D Einstein,

\displaystyle\rho^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}\ell^{3}}\,,\qquad S^{(1)}=-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)+1\,.

(506)

The on-shell quantum dS entropy
{\cal S}=\log Z(\bar{\ell})=S(\bar{\ell})
(
496
) is

\displaystyle\boxed{{\cal S}=S(\bar{\ell})=S^{(0)}(\bar{\ell})+S^{(1)}(\bar{\ell})\,+\,O(G)}

(507)

where
\bar{\ell}
is the quantum mean radius satisfying
\rho(\bar{\ell})\propto\partial_{\ell}\log Z(\bar{\ell})=0
. For 3D Einstein,

\displaystyle{\cal S}=\frac{2\pi\bar{\ell}}{4G}-3\log\frac{2\pi\bar{\ell}}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)+1\,+\,O(G)\,,\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1}{\bar{\ell}^{2}}-\frac{4G}{\pi\bar{\ell}^{3}}+O(G^{2})\,.

(508)

Alternatively,
{\cal S}
can be expressed in terms of the tree-level
\ell_{0}
,
\rho^{(0)}(\ell_{0})\propto\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(0)}(\ell_{0})=0
, using
{\cal S}=\log Z^{(0)}(\bar{\ell})+\log Z^{(1)}(\bar{\ell})+O(G)
,
\bar{\ell}=\ell_{0}+O(G)
and Taylor expanding in
G
:

\displaystyle\boxed{{\cal S}=\log Z(\bar{\ell})=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})+\log Z^{(1)}(\ell_{0})\,+\,O(G)}

(509)

This form would be obtained from (
153
) by a more standard computation. For 3D Einstein,

\displaystyle{\cal S}=\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)\,+\,O(G)\,,\qquad\Lambda=\frac{1}{\ell_{0}^{2}}\,.

(510)

The equivalence of (
507
) and (
509
) can be checked directly here noting
\bar{\ell}=\ell_{0}-\frac{2}{\pi}G\,+\,O(G^{2})
, so
\frac{2\pi\bar{\ell}}{4G}=\frac{2\pi\ell_{0}}{4G}-1+O(G)
. The
-1
cancels the
+1
in (
508
), reproducing (
510
).

More generally and more physically, the relation between these two expressions can be understood as follows. At tree level, the entropy equals the geometric horizon entropy
S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})
, with radius
\ell_{0}
such that the geometric energy density
\rho^{(0)}
vanishes. At one loop, we get additional contributions from quantum field fluctuations. The UV contributions are absorbed into the gravitational coupling constants. The remaining IR contributions shift the entropy by
S^{(1)}
and the energy density by
\rho^{(1)}
. The added energy backreacts on the fluctuating geometry: its mean radius changes from
\ell_{0}
to
\bar{\ell}
such that the geometric energy density changes by
\delta\rho^{(0)}=-\rho^{(1)}
, ensuring the total energy density vanishes. This in turn changes the geometric horizon entropy by an amount dictated by the first law (
477
),

\displaystyle\delta S^{(0)}=V_{0}\,\delta\rho^{(0)}=-V_{0}\,\rho^{(1)}\,.

(511)

We end up with a total entropy
{\cal S}=S^{(0)}(\bar{\ell})+S^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})-V_{0}\,\rho^{(1)}+S^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})+\log Z^{(1)}
, up to
O(G)
corrections, relating (
507
) to (
509
). (See also fig.
21
.)

More succinctly, obtaining (
509
) from (
507
) is akin to obtaining the canonical description of a thermodynamic system from the microcanonical description of system + reservoir. The analog of the canonical partition function is
Z^{(1)}=e^{S^{(1)}-V_{0}\,\rho^{(1)}}
, with
-V_{0}\,\rho^{(1)}
capturing the reservoir (horizon) entropy change due to energy transfer to the system.

I.4
Examples

I.4.1
3D scalar

Figure 21:

One-loop contributions to the dS
3
entropy from metric and scalars with
\eta=1,\frac{1}{4},\frac{5}{4}
, i.e.
\xi=0,\frac{1}{8},-\frac{1}{24}
. Blue dotted line = renormalized entropy
S^{(1)}
. Green dotted line = horizon entropy change
\delta S^{(0)}=2\pi\delta\ell/4G=-V\rho^{(1)}
due to quantum backreaction
\ell_{0}\to\bar{\ell}=\ell_{0}+\delta\ell
, as dictated by first law.
Solid red line = total
\delta{\cal S}=S^{(1)}-V\rho^{(1)}=\log Z^{(1)}
. The metric contribution is negative within the semiclassical regime of validity
\ell\gg G
. The renormalized scalar entropy and energy density are positive for
m\ell\gg 1
, and for all
m\ell
if
\eta=1
. If
\eta\!>\!1
and
\ell_{0}\to\ell_{*}\equiv\frac{\sqrt{\eta-1}}{m}
, the correction
\delta\ell\sim-\frac{G}{3\pi}\frac{\ell_{*}}{\ell_{0}-\ell_{*}}\to-\infty
, meaning the one-loop approximation breaks down. The scalar becomes tachyonic beyond this point. If a
\phi^{4}
term is included in the action, two new dominant saddles emerge with
\phi\neq 0
.

An example with matter is 3D Einstein gravity + scalar
\phi
as in (
140
). Putting
\xi\equiv\frac{1-\eta}{6}
,

\displaystyle S_{E}[g,\phi]=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\,\bigl(\Lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}R\bigr)\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\int\!\!\sqrt{g}\,\phi\bigl(-\nabla^{2}+m^{2}+\tfrac{1-\eta}{6}R\,\bigr)\phi\,,

(512)

The metric contribution to
\log Z^{(1)}
remains
\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm metric}=-3\log\frac{2\pi\ell}{4G}+5\log(2\pi)
as in (
504
).
The scalar
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
was given in (
143
). Its finite part is

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI,fin,scalar}=\frac{\pi\nu^{3}}{6}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{\nu^{k}}{k!}\,\frac{{\rm Li}_{3-k}(e^{-2\pi\nu})}{(2\pi)^{2-k}}\,,\qquad\nu\equiv\sqrt{m^{2}\ell^{2}-\eta}\,.

(513)

The polynomial

\log Z_{\rm ct}(\ell)=c_{0}\ell^{3}+c_{2}\ell
corresponding to the counterterm action (
502
) is
fixed by the renormalization condition (
505
), resulting in

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}=\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm PI,fin,scalar}-\frac{\pi}{6}\,m^{3}\ell^{3}+\frac{\pi\eta}{4}\,m\ell\,.

(514)

The finite polynomial cancels the local terms
\propto\ell^{3},\ell
in the large-
\ell
asymptotic expansion of the finite part:
\log Z_{\rm scalar}^{(1)}=\frac{\pi\eta^{2}}{16}(m\ell)^{-1}+\frac{\pi\eta^{3}}{96}(m\ell)^{-3}+\cdots
when
m\ell\to\infty
. The
(m\ell)^{-{2n-1}}
terms have the
\ell
-dependence of
R^{n}
terms in the action and can effectively be thought of as finite shifts of higher-order curvature couplings in the
m\ell\gg 1
regime. In the opposite regime
m\ell\ll 1
, IR bulk modes of the scalar becomes thermally activated and
\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}
ceases to have a local expansion. In particular in the minimally-coupled case
\eta=1
,

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}\simeq-\log(m\ell)\qquad(m\ell\to 0)\,.

(515)

The total energy density is
\rho=-\frac{1}{3}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z/V=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\bigl(\Lambda-\ell^{-2}\bigr)+1+\rho^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}
where

\displaystyle V\rho^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}=-\frac{\pi}{6}(m\ell)^{2}\nu\coth(\pi\nu)+\frac{\pi}{6}(m\ell)^{3}-\frac{\pi\eta}{12}\,m\ell\,.

(516)

The on-shell quantum dS entropy is given to this order by (
509
) or by (
507
) as

\displaystyle{\cal S}={\cal S}^{(0)}+{\cal S}^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})+\log Z^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\ell_{0})-V\rho^{(1)}+S^{(1)}=S^{(0)}(\bar{\ell})+S^{(1)}\,,

(517)

where

\ell_{0}^{-2}=\Lambda=\bar{\ell}^{-2}-8\pi G\,\rho^{(1)}(\bar{\ell})
and
S^{(1)}=S_{\rm PI,fin}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{6}\pi\eta\,m\ell
, with the scalar contribution to
S_{\rm PI,fin}^{(1)}
given by the finite part of (
145
).
Some examples are shown in fig.
21
.

For a massless scalar,
m=0
, the renormalized scalar one-loop correction to
{\cal S}
is a constant independent of
\ell_{0}
given by (
513
) evaluated at
\nu=\sqrt{-\eta}
, and
\rho^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}=0
. For example for a massless conformally coupled scalar,
\eta=\frac{1}{4}
,
Z^{(1)}_{\rm scalar}=\frac{3\,\zeta(3)}{16\pi^{2}}-\frac{\log(2)}{8}
.

I.4.2
3D massive spin
s

The renormalized one-loop correction
{\cal S}^{(1)}_{s}=\log Z^{(1)}_{s}
to the dS
3
entropy from a massive spin-
s
field is obtained similarly from (
90
):

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(1)}_{s}=\log Z^{(1)}_{s,\rm bulk}-\log Z^{(1)}_{s,\rm edge}\,,

(518)

where
\log Z^{(1)}_{s,\rm bulk}
equals twice the contribution of an
\eta=(s-1)^{2}
scalar as given in (
514
), while the edge contribution is, putting
\nu\equiv\sqrt{m^{2}\ell^{2}-(s-1)^{2}}
,

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}_{s,\rm edge}=s^{2}\bigl(\pi(m\ell-\nu)-\log(1-e^{-2\pi\nu})\bigr)\,.

(519)

The edge contribution to (
518
) is manifestly negative. It dominates the bulk part, and increasingly so as
s
grows. Examples are shown in fig.
22
.

Figure 22:

Contributions to the dS
3
entropy from massive spin
s=1,2,3
fields, as a function of
m\ell_{0}
, with coloring as in fig.
21
. Singularities = Higuchi bound, as discussed under (
145
).

I.4.3
2D scalar

As mentioned below (
505
), the counterterm polynomial
\log Z_{\rm ct}^{(0)}
has a constant term in even spacetime dimensions
d+1
, which is not fixed yet by the renormalization prescription given there. Let us consider the simplest example: a
d=1
scalar with action (
140
).
Denoting

\displaystyle{\tt f}(\nu)\equiv\sum_{\pm}\zeta^{\prime}(-1,\tfrac{1}{2}\pm i\nu)\mp i\nu\zeta^{\prime}(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\pm i\nu)\,,\,,

(520)

and
M_{\epsilon}={2e^{-\gamma}}/{\epsilon}
as in (
261
),
we get from (
253
) with
\nu\equiv\sqrt{m^{2}\ell^{2}-\eta}
,
\eta\equiv\tfrac{1}{4}-2\xi
,

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}=\bigl(2\epsilon^{-2}-m^{2}\log(M_{\epsilon}\ell)+m^{2}\bigr)\,\ell^{2}+\bigl(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12}\bigr)\log\bigl(M_{\epsilon}\ell\bigr)-\eta+{\tt f}(\nu)\,,

(521)

In the limit
m\ell\to\infty
, using the asymptotic expansion of the Hurwitz zeta function
47
47

47

See e.g. appendix A of
Dib:2000hd
,

\displaystyle\log Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}=\bigl(2\epsilon^{-2}-m^{2}\log(M_{\epsilon}/m)-\tfrac{1}{2}m^{2}\bigr)\,\ell^{2}+(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\log(M_{\epsilon}/m)\,+\,O((m\ell)^{-2})\,.

(522)

Notice the
\log\ell
dependence apparent in (
521
) has canceled out. The counterterm action to this order is again of the form (
502
), corresponding to
\log Z_{\rm ct}=4\pi(-c_{0}^{\prime}\ell^{2}+c_{2}^{\prime})
. The renormalization condition (
505
) fixes
c_{0}^{\prime}
but leaves
c_{2}^{\prime}
undetermined. Its natural extension here is to pick
c_{2}=4\pi c_{2}^{\prime}
to cancel off the constant term as well, that is

\displaystyle c_{2}=-(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\log(M_{\epsilon}/m)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\log Z^{(1)}=0\,,

(523)

ensuring the tree-level
G
equals the renormalized Newton constant to this order, as in (
503
).
The renormalized scalar one-loop contribution to the off-shell partition function is then

\log Z^{(1)}=\bigl(\tfrac{3}{2}-\log(m\ell)\bigr)(m\ell)^{2}+(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\log(m\ell)-\eta+{\tt f}(\nu)\,.

(524)

In the large-
m\ell
limit,
\log Z^{(1)}=\frac{240\,\eta^{2}+40\,\eta+7}{960}(m\ell)^{-2}+\cdots>0
, while in the small-
m\ell
limit

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}\simeq\bigl(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12}\bigr)\log(m\ell)\quad(\eta<\tfrac{1}{4})\,,\qquad\log Z^{(1)}\simeq\bigl(\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{1}{12}-1\bigr)\log(m\ell)\quad(\eta=\tfrac{1}{4})\,.

(525)

The extra
-\log(m\ell)
in the minimally-coupled case
\eta=\frac{1}{4}
is the same as in (
515
) and has the same thermal interpretation. The energy density is
\rho^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{2}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(1)}/V
with
V=4\pi\ell^{2}
:

\displaystyle V\rho^{(1)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})+\tfrac{1}{2}(m\ell)^{2}\bigl(2\log(m\ell)-\mbox{$\sum_{\pm}$}\psi^{(0)}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\pm i\nu\bigr)\bigr)

(526)

In the massless case
m=0
,
\nu=\sqrt{-\eta}
is
\ell
-independent, and we cannot use the asymptotic expansion (
522
), nor the renormalization prescription (
523
). Instead we fix
c_{2}
by minimal subtraction, picking a reference length scale
L
and putting (with
M_{\epsilon}=\frac{2e^{-\gamma}}{\epsilon}
(
261
) as before)

\displaystyle c_{2}(L)\equiv-(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\log(M_{\epsilon}L)\,,

(527)

The renormalized
G
then satisfies
\partial_{L}(\frac{4\pi}{8\pi G}+c_{2})=0
, i.e.
L\partial_{L}\frac{1}{2G}=\eta+\frac{1}{12}
, and

\displaystyle\log Z^{(1)}=(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\log(\ell/L)-\eta+{\tt f}\bigl(\sqrt{-\eta}\bigr)\,,\qquad V\rho^{(1)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\eta+\tfrac{1}{12})\,.

(528)

The total
\log Z=\frac{1}{2G}(-\Lambda\ell^{2}+1)+\log Z^{(1)}
is of course independent of the choice of
L
.

I.4.4
4D massive spin
s

Figure 23:

Edge contributions to the dS
4
entropy from massive spin
s=1,2,3
fields, as a function of
m\ell_{0}
, with coloring as in fig.
21
. The Higuchi/unitarity bound in this case is
(m\ell_{0})^{2}-(s-\frac{1}{2})^{2}>-\frac{1}{4}
.

4D massive spin-
s
fields can be treated similarly, starting from (
C.2
). In particular the edge contribution
\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm edge}
equals
minus
the
\log Z^{(1)}
of
D_{s-1}^{5}=\frac{1}{6}s(s+1)(2s+1)
scalars on
S^{2}
, computed earlier in (
524
), with the same
\nu=\sqrt{m^{2}\ell^{2}-\eta_{s}}
as the bulk spin-
s
field, which according to
(
79
) means
\eta_{s}=(s-\frac{1}{2})^{2}
. The corresponding contribution to the renormalized energy density is
\rho^{(1)}_{\rm edge}=-\frac{1}{4}\ell\partial_{\ell}\log Z^{(1)}_{\rm edge}/V
with
V=\Omega_{4}\ell^{4}
, so
V\rho^{(1)}_{\rm edge}
equals
-\frac{1}{2}D_{s-1}^{5}
times the scalar result (
526
).

As in the
d=2
case, the renormalized one-loop edge contribution
{\cal S}^{(1)}_{\rm edge}
to the entropy is negative and dominant. Some examples are shown in fig.
23
.

I.4.5
Graviton contribution for general
d

For
d\geq 3
, UV-sensitive terms in the loop expansion renormalize higher-order curvature couplings in the gravitational action, prompting the inclusion of such terms in
S_{E}[g]
. Some caution is in order then if we wish to apply (
112
) or (
148
)-(
149
) to compute
\log Z^{(1)}
.
The formula (
112
) for
Z_{\rm PI}^{(1)}
depends on
\gamma=\sqrt{8\pi G_{\rm N}/A_{d-1}}
, gauge-algebraically defined by (
109
) and various normalization conventions. We picked these such that in pure Einstein gravity,
\gamma=\sqrt{8\pi G/A(\ell_{0})}
,
\ell_{0}=\sqrt{d(d-1)/2\Lambda}
, with
G
and
\Lambda
read off from the gravitational Lagrangian. However this expression of
\gamma
in terms of Lagrangian parameters
will in general be modified in the presence of higher-order curvature terms. This is clear from the discussion in
I.1.3
, and (
488
) in particular. Since
\gamma_{0}
is field-redefinition invariant, and since after transforming to a pure Einstein frame we have
\gamma_{0}=\sqrt{2\pi/{\cal S}^{(0)}}
, with the right hand side also invariant, we have in general (for Einstein + perturbative higher-order curvature corrections)

\displaystyle\gamma=\sqrt{{2\pi}/{{\cal S}^{(0)}}}\,.

(529)

From (
148
) we thus get (ignoring the phase)

\displaystyle{\cal S}={\cal S}^{(0)}-\frac{D_{d}}{2}\log{\cal S}^{(0)}+\alpha^{(2)}_{d+1}\log\frac{\ell_{0}}{L}+K_{d+1}\,+\,O\bigl(1/{\cal S}^{(0)}\bigr)

(530)

where
D_{d}=\frac{(d+2)(d+1)}{2}
,
\alpha_{d+1}^{(2)}=0
for even
d
and given by
(
116
) for odd
d
, and
K_{d+1}
a numerical constant obtained by evaluating (
115
).
For odd
d
the constant in the counterterm
\log Z_{\rm ct}(\ell)
is fixed by minimal subtraction at a scale
L
,
c_{d+1}(L)\equiv-\alpha_{d+1}\log(M_{\epsilon}L)
, with
M_{\epsilon}=2e^{-\gamma}/\epsilon
determined by the heat kernel regulator as in (
261
), and
L\partial_{L}{\cal S}=0
, i.e.
L\partial_{L}{\cal S}^{(0)}=\alpha_{d+1}^{(2)}
.
Explicitly for
d=2,3,4
, using (
149
), (
12
)

\begin{array}[]{l|l}d&{\cal S}\\
\hline\cr 2&{\cal S}^{(0)}-3\log{\cal S}^{(0)}+5\log(2\pi)\\
3&{\cal S}^{(0)}-5\log{\cal S}^{(0)}-\frac{571}{90}\log(\frac{\ell_{0}}{L})-\log(\frac{8\pi}{3})+\frac{715}{48}-\frac{47}{3}\zeta^{\prime}(-1)+\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{\prime}(-3)\\
4&{\cal S}^{(0)}-\frac{15}{2}\log{\cal S}^{(0)}+\log(12)+\frac{27}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{65\,\zeta(3)}{48\,\pi^{2}}+\frac{5\,\zeta(5)}{16\,\pi^{4}}\end{array}

(531)

For a
d=3
action (
481
) up to
O(l_{s}^{2}R^{2})
, with dots denoting
O(l_{s}^{4})
terms,

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{\pi}{G}\left(\ell_{0}^{2}+{48\,\lambda_{R^{2}}\,l_{s}^{2}}+\cdots\right)\,,\qquad\Lambda=\frac{3}{\ell_{0}^{2}}+\cdots\,.

(532)

where
L\partial_{L}\lambda_{R^{2}}=-\frac{G}{48\,\pi\,l_{s}^{2}}\cdot\frac{571}{45}
. Putting
L=\ell_{0}
, and defining the scale
\ell_{R^{2}}
by
\lambda_{R^{2}}(\ell_{R^{2}})=0
,

\displaystyle{\cal S}={\cal S}^{(0)}-5\log{\cal S}^{(0)}+K_{4}\,+\,O\bigl(1/{\cal S}^{(0)}\bigr)\,,\qquad{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{\pi\ell_{0}^{2}}{G}-\frac{571}{45}\log\frac{\ell_{0}}{\ell_{R^{2}}}+\cdots\,.

(533)

The constant
K_{4}
could be absorbed into
\lambda_{R^{2}}
at this level. Below, in (
539
), we will give it relative meaning however, by considering saddles different from the round
S^{4}
.

I.5
Classical and quantum observables

Here we address question
3
in our list below (
153
). To answer this, we need “observables” of the
\Lambda>0
Euclidean low-energy effective field theory probing independent gravitational couplings (for simplicity we restrict ourselves to purely gravitational theories here), i.e. diffeomorphism and field-redefinition invariant quantities, analogous to scattering amplitudes in asymptotically flat space. For this to be similarly useful, an infinite amount of unambiguous data should be extractable, at least in principle, from these observables.

As discussed above,
{\cal S}^{(0)}=\log{\cal Z}^{(0)}=-S_{E}[g_{\ell_{0}}]
invariantly probes the dimensionless coupling given by
\ell_{0}^{d-1}/G\propto 1/G\Lambda^{{(d-1)}/2}
in Einstein frame.
The obvious tree-level invariants probing different couplings in the gravitational low-energy effective field theory are then the
analogous
{\cal S}^{(0)}_{M}\equiv\log{\cal Z}^{(0)}_{M}=-S_{E}[g_{M}]
evaluated on saddles
g_{M}
different from the round sphere, in the parametric
\ell_{0}\gg l_{s}
regime of validity
of the effective field theory, with
g_{M}
asymptotically Einstein in the
\ell_{0}\to\infty
limit. These are the analogs of tree-level scattering amplitudes. The obvious quantum counterparts are the corresponding generalizations of
{\cal S}
, i.e.
{\cal S}_{M}\equiv\log{\cal Z}_{M}
evaluated in large-
\ell_{0}
perturbation theory about the saddle
g_{M}
. These are the analogs of quantum scattering amplitudes. Below we make this a bit more concrete in examples.

3D

In
d=2
, the Weyl tensor vanishes identically, so higher-order curvature invariants involve
R_{\mu\nu}
only and can be removed from the action by a field redefinition in large-
\ell_{0}
perturbation theory, reducing it to pure Einstein form in general. As a result,
{\cal S}^{(0)}
is the only independent invariant in pure 3D gravity, all
g_{M}
are Einstein, and the
{\cal S}^{(0)}_{M}
are all proportional to
{\cal S}_{0}\equiv{\cal S}^{(0)}_{S^{3}}
.

As discussed under (
168
), the quantum
{\cal S}={\cal S}_{S^{3}}
takes the form

{\cal S}={\cal S}_{0}={\cal S}_{0}-3\log{\cal S}_{0}+5\log(2\pi)+\mbox{$\sum_{n}$}\,c_{n}\,{\cal S}_{0}^{-2n}

(534)

The corrections terms in the expansion are all nonlocal (no odd powers of
\ell_{0}
), and the coefficients provide an unambiguous, infinite data set.

odd D
\geq
5

In 5D gravity, there are infinitely many independent coupling constants. There are also infinitely many different
\Lambda>0
Einstein metrics on
S^{5}
, including a discrete infinity of Böhm metrics with
SO(3)\times SO(3)
symmetry
Bohm:1998vc
amenable to detailed numerical analysis
Gibbons:2002th
, and 68 Sasaki-Einstein families with moduli spaces up to real dimension 10
Boyer:2003pe
. Unlike the round
S^{5}
, these are not conformally flat, and thus, unlike
{\cal S}^{(0)}
, the corresponding
{\cal S}^{(0)}_{M}
will pick up couplings such as the Weyl-squared coupling
\lambda_{C^{2}}
in (
481
).
It is plausible that this set of known Einstein metrics (perturbed by small higher-order corrections to the Einstein equations of motion at finite
\ell_{0}
) more than suffices to invariantly probe all independent couplings of the gravitational action, delivering moreover infinitely many quantum observables
{\cal S}_{M}
, providing an infinity of unambiguous low-energy effective field theory data to any order in perturbation theory, without ever leaving the sphere — at least in principle.

The landscape of known
\Lambda>0
Einstein metrics on odd-dimensional spheres becomes increasingly vast as the dimension grows, with double-exponentially growing numbers
Boyer:2003pe
. For example there are at least 8610 families of Sasaki-Einstein manifolds on
S^{7}
, spanning all 28 diffeomorphism classes, with the standard class admitting a 82-dimensional family, and there are at least
10^{828}
distinct families of Einstein metrics on
S^{25}
, featuring moduli spaces of dimension greater than
10^{833}
.

4D

4D gravity likewise has infinitely many independent coupling constants. It is not known if
S^{4}
has another Einstein metric besides the round sphere. In fact the list of 4D topologies known to admit
\Lambda>0
Einstein metrics is rather limited
2008arXiv0810.4830A
:
S^{4}
,
S^{2}\times S^{2}
,
{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
, and the connected sums
{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\#k\overline{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}
,
1\leq k\leq 8
. However for
k\geq 5
these have a moduli space of nonzero dimension
Tian:1987tw
;
Tian:1990wd
, which might suffice to probe all couplings. (The moduli space would presumably be lifted at sufficiently high order in the
l_{s}
expansion upon turning on higher-order curvature perturbations.)

Below we illustrate in explicit detail how the Weyl-squared coupling can be extracted from suitable linear combinations of pairs of
{\cal S}_{M}^{(0)}
with
M\in\{S^{4},S^{2}\times S^{2},{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\}
, and how a suitable linear combination of all three can be used to extract an unambiguous linear combination of the constant terms arising at one loop.

The Weyl-squared coupling
\lambda_{C^{2}}
in
S_{E}[g]=\mbox{(\ref{SERiem2})}+\cdots
is invisible to
{\cal S}^{(0)}
(
532
) but it is picked up by
{\cal S}_{M}^{(0)}
by
M=S^{2}\times S^{2}
:

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}=\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{\pi}{G}\bigl(\ell_{0}^{2}+48\,\lambda_{R^{2}}\,l_{s}^{2}+16\,\lambda_{C^{2}}\,l_{s}^{2}\,+\,\cdots\bigr)\,,

(535)

with the dots denoting
O(l_{s}^{4})
terms and
\ell_{0}=\sqrt{3/\Lambda}+\cdots
as in (
532
). Physically,
{\cal S}^{(0)}_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}
is the horizon entropy of the
{\rm dS}_{2}\times S^{2}
static patch, i.e. the Nariai spacetime between the cosmological and maximal Schwarzschild-de Sitter black hole horizons, both of area
A=\frac{1}{3}\cdot 4\pi\ell_{0}^{2}
. Comparing to (
532
), the linear combination

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}_{C^{2}}\equiv 3\,{\cal S}^{(0)}_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}-2\,{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{32\pi l_{s}^{2}}{G}\bigl(\,\lambda_{C^{2}}\,+\,\cdots\bigr)

(536)

extracts the Weyl-squared coupling of
S_{E}[g]
. Analogously, for the Einstein metric on
{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
, we get
\tilde{\cal S}^{(0)}_{C^{2}}\equiv 8\,{\cal S}^{(0)}_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}-6\,{\cal S}^{(0)}=\frac{48\pi l_{s}^{2}}{G}(\lambda_{C^{2}}+\cdots)
.
Then

\displaystyle{\cal S}^{(0)}_{\rm cub}\equiv 2\,\tilde{\cal S}^{(0)}_{C^{2}}-3\,{\cal S}^{(0)}_{C^{2}}=16\,{\cal S}^{(0)}_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}-9\,{\cal S}^{(0)}_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}-6\,{\cal S}^{(0)}\,=0\,+\,\cdots\,,

(537)

which extracts some curvature-cubed coupling in the effective action.

To one loop, the quantum
{\cal S}_{M}=\log{\cal Z}_{M}
can be expressed in a form paralleling (
530
):

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{M}={\cal S}^{(0)}_{M}-\frac{D_{M}}{2}\log{\cal S}_{M}^{(0)}+\alpha_{M}\log\frac{\ell_{0}}{L}+K_{M}\,+\,\cdots\,,

(538)

where
D_{M}
is the number of Killing vectors of
M
:
D_{S^{4}}=10
,
D_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}=6
,
D_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}=8
, and
\alpha_{M}
can be obtained from the local expressions in
Christensen:1979iy
:
\alpha_{S^{4}}=-\frac{571}{45}
,
\alpha_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}=-\frac{98}{45}
,
\alpha_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}=-\frac{359}{60}
. Computing the constants
K_{M}
generalizing
K_{S^{4}}
given in (
531
) would require more work. Moreover, computing them for one or two saddles would provide no unambiguous information because they may be absorbed into
\lambda_{C^{2}}
and
\lambda_{R^{2}}
. However, since there only two undetermined coupling constants at this order, computing them for all
three
does provide unambiguous information, extracted by the quantum counterpart of (
537
):

\displaystyle{\cal S}_{\rm cub}\,\equiv\,16\,{\cal S}_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}-9\,{\cal S}_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}-6\,{\cal S}_{S^{4}}=-7\,\log{\cal S}^{(0)}+16\,K_{{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}}-9\,K_{S^{2}\times S^{2}}-6\,K_{S^{4}}+\cdots

(539)

The
\log(\ell_{0}/L)
terms had to cancel in this linear combination because the tree-level parts at this order cancel by design and
L\partial_{L}{\cal S}_{\rm cub}=0
.
